QUANTUM REFERENCE FRAMES AND SINGLE-OUTCOME DYNAMICS - Generative Presentism Compatibility

Word document, 62,665 words, 159 KB

Download

QUANTUM REFERENCE FRAMES AND SINGLE-OUTCOME DYNAMICS A Compatibility Framework for Generative Presentism ==================================================================================================== ABSTRACT Quantum reference frames (QRFs) permit different quantum systems to serve as reference frames, raising a foundational question for single-outcome theories: how can frame-dependent descriptions represent one objectively realized history? Cosmic-Time Generative Presentism (CTGP) holds that only the present exists, that physical reality is generated through successive stages, and that truths about the determinate past are grounded in primitive past-tensed properties of the present world. We develop a mathematical compatibility framework connecting these commitments to QRF covariance, stochastic quantum instruments, and relativistic measurement theory. We first construct an explicit QRF-covariant, single-outcome quantum model using relational GRW-type events and a Lucretian truth profile. We establish a frame-independent determinacy criterion, characterize when different frame descriptions identify the same complete physical state and realized history, and prove covariance of the generative law and its history probabilities. We distinguish physical-state identity, historical identity, and observational indistinguishability, and show that the present quantum state need not determine the complete past. The construction demonstrates the joint mathematical consistency of these commitments without deriving their ontological interpretation or establishing physical adequacy. We then formulate a compatibility program for relativistic quantum field theory, quantum clocks, and quantum gravity. For specified causally local instruments, we establish conditions for foliation- independent outcome histories and operational no-signaling. Free-field detector and Gaussian- collapse constructions identify both sufficient conditions for causal admissibility and obstructions to extending them to arbitrary local operations and to covariantly located collapses. We calculate the energy–momentum cost of Gaussian collapse and examine, without adopting it, a cosmological-time- guided candidate that defines normalized finite-volume dynamics but introduces preferred-frame structure. To distinguish formal collapse prescriptions from physical implementations, we compare centre-time ordering, completion-time ordering, and a local field–pointer coupling. The pointer construction yields foliation-independent statistics, reproduces ordered-collapse predictions for causally orderable configurations with suitably prepared pointers, and defines a consistent law of outcome records even in spatially infinite universes. Interfering collapse regions, however, produce different joint outcome laws, showing that this local coupling does not in general reproduce a prescribed collapse ordering. Definite pointer readings require an explicit single-outcome postulate, and infinite-volume generation of the physical state remains open. We also give clock- neutral descriptions of late-record instruments for two ideal clocks and exhibit interference between alternative clock-dependent interaction orders. These results establish a concrete mathematical compatibility framework, not a completed relativistic theory or an empirical confirmation of CTGP. They separate frame-dependent descriptions from the identity of realized events, identify which features of single-outcome dynamics survive changes of reference frame and foliation, and isolate the additional physical requirements for relativistic collapse, temporal quantum reference frames, and quantum gravity. Keywords: presentism; generative presentism; Lucretianism; quantum reference frames; perspective- neutral framework; objective collapse; GRW; flash ontology; quantum instruments; quantum jump processes; single-outcome realism; determinacy; truth preservation; non-supervenience; relativistic quantum field theory; foliation independence; particle detectors; measurement models; Gaussian collapse; impossible measurements; no-signaling; Poisson point processes; collapse energy; temporal quantum reference frames; quantum clocks; clock-neutral instruments; quantum gravity; cosmological time; preferred frames; philosophy of time READER’S GUIDE Three layers of claim run through this preprint and should be kept distinct. Layer 1 is established mathematics adopted without modification: the perspective-neutral framework for quantum reference frames with a finite symmetry group, quantum instruments (Kraus operators) for stochastic single- outcome dynamics, and piecewise-deterministic quantum jump processes in continuous time. Layer 2 consists of the definitions proposed here: the complete ontological state, the space of admissible histories, the determinacy criterion, the cross-frame identification criterion, and the validity condition for frame-relative laws. Layer 3 is the concrete model of Part III, which shows that the Layer 2 definitions are jointly satisfiable by an explicit dynamics. Part V adds a further kind of material, a research program, whose items are labeled by status: proved theorems and propositions, definitions offered as proposals, and conjectures or targets that remain open. Three kinds of result should likewise be kept apart. An existence result says that at least one model in a specified class satisfies a given set of commitments. A general compatibility result would say that every physically admissible QRF theory can accommodate those commitments. A physical adequacy result would say that the model reproduces the relevant features of established quantum physics and relativity. This preprint establishes an existence result. It does not establish the other two, and §17 states precisely why not. Interpretation enters at identifiable points, and those points are flagged in the text. The theorems are mathematical consequences of the definitions. The claims that the perspective-neutral state and the truth profile represent what exists, and that each orbit of frame descriptions represents one realized present with one history, are interpretive theses (Ontological Reading 4.3 and Thesis 5.10). They are what CTGP adds to the mathematics, and they are argued for rather than derived. The spaces of histories and the probability measures over future trajectories used throughout are mathematical representations of possibilities; they do not assert that past or future stages exist (§4.5). Part I (§§1–2) states the problem and the background. Part II (§§3–5) develops the general formalism for any finite symmetry group. Part III (§§6–10) builds the concrete model in discrete-stage and continuous-time form and checks CTGP’s further requirements. Part IV (§11) identifies what CTGP adds beyond QRF covariance. Part V (§§12–16) sets out the compatibility program for relativistic quantum field theory, temporal quantum reference frames, and quantum gravity. Part VI (§§17–19) states the scope of the results and the open problems, and maps the results back to the original questions. Appendix A collects the frame-relative forms used in the model. Appendix B contains the verification script for the model and the finite theorems, with its output and the information needed to reproduce it, and Appendix C contains separate scripts for the results of §§13.8–13.11 and §14.5. Nothing here modifies the CTGP postulates, and nothing here is an empirical result. The aim is to replace schematic statements about CTGP and quantum theory with definitions precise enough to be proved or refuted. ==================================================================================================== PART I. The Problem ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 1. Introduction ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- CTGP claims that the present stage of the universe is all that exists, that it is generated from the preceding stage by physical law, and that truths about the past are grounded Lucretian-style in the present world’s primitive past-tensed properties: the present world has the property *having been, at stage σ′, such that φ* (CTGP – explained, §5.2.1 and Definition 17.20). Because it entails a unique factual past, CTGP requires single-outcome realism in quantum theory, which excludes the standard Everettian ontology, and it rejects relational interpretations on which facts are relative to observers (CTGP – explained, §10.1). Quantum reference frames put pressure on this combination. In the QRF literature (Giacomini, Castro- Ruiz & Brukner 2019; Vanrietvelde, Höhn, Giacomini & Castro-Ruiz 2020; de la Hamette & Galley 2020), there is no external classical reference frame: a physical system is described relative to another quantum system. A system in a definite position relative to one frame can be in a superposition, and entangled, relative to another. Three questions then arise for CTGP. Is determinacy frame-relative? If two frames describe the present differently, what makes them descriptions of one present and one history? And where do the Lucretian past-tensed properties sit relative to a quantum state that has many frame-relative descriptions? A prior analysis of these questions proposed the schematic representation Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ), S_{σ′} ∼ K(S_{σ′} | S_σ), L_{σ′} = L_σ ∪ ΔL_{σ→σ′}, in which S_σ is the present physical state governing successor-state generation, L_σ is the present world’s Lucretian past-tensed truth profile, K is a stochastic generative law, and ΔL_{σ→σ′} represents the truths newly acquired about what occurred during the transition. It drew a key distinction: CTGP’s unique factual history must be grounded in the chosen single-outcome quantum ontology, and L represents truths about the history actually generated rather than serving as an independent mechanism that determines which history occurs. It identified four tasks: (T1) Which propositions are determinate under the chosen quantum ontology. (T2) How corresponding physical facts are identified across quantum reference frames. (T3) Whether valid QRF transformations preserve the truth of those corresponding propositions. (T4) How Lucretian past-tensed properties relate to the physical quantum state without introducing unjustified dynamical assumptions. It added a fifth requirement: a QRF-compatible single-outcome model must also satisfy CTGP’s requirements of definite generative ordering and generability (T5). It also posed the question at the heart of T2: how can one establish that two frame descriptions refer to the same realized history, rather than simply assuming that they do? This preprint addresses all five tasks by construction. It specifies a class of QRF-covariant, single-outcome stochastic quantum dynamics, shows that every member of the class admits a CTGP interpretation, and proves what that interpretation implies. The strategy has three parts. First, the underlying present state lives in the perspective-neutral (gauge-invariant) Hilbert space of the QRF framework, and frame descriptions are obtained from it by unitary reduction maps. Second, the single-outcome dynamics is a stochastic quantum instrument whose operators are invariant under the symmetry group, so that each outcome is a frame-independent event; this is a relational version of the flash ontology of the Ghirardi–Rimini–Weber (GRW) collapse model (Ghirardi, Rimini & Weber 1986; Bell 1987; Tumulka 2006). Third, the truth profile L is defined as the past-tensing of the realized states and events, so that it is generated by the dynamics rather than feeding into it, and the space of admissible profiles is specified exactly. Two clarifications fix the status of the results. First, QRF covariance is not discovered here; within the perspective-neutral framework it is automatic for any law defined on physical states (Remark 3.9). What is shown is that a CTGP interpretation can be placed on such dynamics consistently, and what follows when it is. Second, the question whether CTGP contributes anything beyond “a QRF-covariant single-outcome model with an attached historical truth profile” has a definite answer, given in §11: CTGP’s generability, single-outcome, frame-invariant determinacy, no- revision, and definite-ordering requirements each exclude models that are QRF-covariant, and, within the perspective-neutral construction, the requirement that every realized event carry physical states to physical states makes the collapse operators act as relational operators. In brief, the answers are as follows. Determinacy is not frame-relative (§5.1). Two frame descriptions describe the same present and the same history exactly when they belong to one orbit of frame descriptions of a single complete state, which is equivalent to an explicit criterion on stage, state, and truth profile (§5.2). Matching states is not sufficient, because a present state does not fix its past; what settles identity of history is the truth profile (§5.2, §5.5). Valid frame changes intertwine the generative law, so the probability measure over histories, the conditional independence of the future from the past, and the truth of every determinate proposition are the same in every frame (§5.3–5.4). And L sits beside the quantum state without driving it: it is not a function of the present state, the law never takes it as input, and given the law it is accurate to the state it accompanies (§4.6, §5.6). Part III shows that an explicit dynamics, in discrete-stage and continuous-time form, has all of these properties. Part V looks beyond the finite model. Relativistic quantum field theory, temporal quantum reference frames, and quantum gravity each put CTGP’s commitments under new pressure, and none of them can be settled by the constructions of Parts II–IV. Part V therefore turns the commitments into an admissibility criterion and proves what can be proved now. In finite form, this includes a realized- history consistency theorem under which, in causally local finite models, the choice of generative foliation is empirically invisible. In relativistic form, it includes networks of gapless detectors on a free field with operational no-signaling in Sorkin’s configuration, a regulated spontaneous- collapse network built from Gaussian collapses of smeared fields together with the exact energy cost of such collapses, a no-signaling criterion that does not depend on free fields, and collapses at Poisson-distributed locations, conditionally on causally orderable configurations, together with the obstructions to a fully covariant version and a candidate completion using cosmological time that is developed but not adopted. For quantum clocks, it includes a construction with two ideal clocks in which the interventions are part of the constraint. Part V also states the remaining obligations and possible obstructions precisely, so that each direction can be pursued as a sequence of mathematical results. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 2. Background ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 2.1 Quantum Reference Frames and the Perspective-Neutral Framework In standard quantum mechanics, states are written relative to an external classical reference frame: positions are measured from a fixed origin. If the universe is treated as closed, there is no external frame, and only relations between subsystems are physical. The perspective-neutral framework (Vanrietvelde et al. 2020; de la Hamette & Galley 2020; Krumm, Höhn & Müller 2021) implements this by imposing invariance under a symmetry group G acting on all subsystems at once. The kinematical Hilbert space H_kin carries a unitary representation U of G, and the physical (perspective-neutral) Hilbert space H_phys is the subspace of states invariant under every U_g. These states make no reference to any external frame. Choosing a subsystem as reference frame defines a reduction map from H_phys to a frame-relative Hilbert space describing the remaining subsystems relative to that frame, and changes of quantum reference frame are compositions of one reduction map with the inverse of another. This construction reproduces the frame changes of Giacomini, Castro-Ruiz and Brukner (2019), and Krumm, Höhn and Müller (2021) show that QRF transformations are canonically defined on the invariant sector. Related ideas appear in the literature on reference frames and superselection (Aharonov & Kaufherr 1984; Bartlett, Rudolph & Spekkens 2007) and on relational quantities (Loveridge, Miyadera & Busch 2018). Two features matter for CTGP. First, frame changes in this framework are not choices of “whose facts” to consider; they are changes of description of one invariant state, which is the reading CTGP needs. Second, operators that are not invariant under G do not in general map H_phys to itself, so a law written in absolute, non-relational terms is not well defined on physical states. This constraint does substantive work in §7.4. 2.2 Single-Outcome Dynamics as Quantum Instruments and Jump Processes A stochastic quantum dynamics with definite outcomes can be written as a sequence of quantum instruments (Davies & Lewis 1970; Kraus 1983). An instrument is a family of operators {F_k}, indexed by outcomes k, satisfying the completeness relation Σ_k F_k† F_k = 1. Given a normalized state ψ, outcome k occurs with probability ‖F_k ψ‖², and the state is updated to F_k ψ / ‖F_k ψ‖. In continuous time, the corresponding object is a piecewise-deterministic jump process: the state evolves smoothly between random jump times and jumps by normalized jump operators, and its ensemble average obeys a Lindblad master equation (Lindblad 1976; Gorini, Kossakowski & Sudarshan 1976; Dalibard, Castin & Mølmer 1992; Breuer & Petruccione 2002). Objective-collapse models have this form. In the GRW model, a spontaneous “hit” multiplies the wave function by a Gaussian of fixed width centred at a random point, with the centre drawn according to the Born rule, and hits occur at the times of a Poisson process. Bell (1987) proposed taking the hit events themselves, the “flashes”, as the primitive ontology: the material world consists of the flashes, and the wave function governs their probabilities. Tumulka (2006) gave a relativistic version (rGRWf). Allori, Goldstein, Tumulka and Zanghì (2008) analyze the common structure of such primitive-ontology theories. The instrument picture gives the generative law a precise meaning. One stage-to-stage transition applies the deterministic part of the dynamics, then samples an outcome k from the instrument and updates the state. The pair (new state, outcome) is drawn from a probability kernel that depends only on the current state, which is the form of law required by CTGP’s generability constraint (CTGP – explained, §12.2): a law fixing transition probabilities P(S_{σ′} | S_σ) without reference to data on later stages. 2.3 The CTGP Commitments Used Here The following CTGP commitments are used below, cited to CTGP – explained and More Info. (C-a) Ontological primacy of the present (Postulate 1). Only the current stage exists. (C-b) Lucretian truthmakers (§5.2.1; Definition 17.20). “At σ′ it was the case that φ” is true at σ iff the present world W_σ instantiates the primitive property Had(σ′, φ). The accuracy condition requires that W_σ instantiates Had(σ′, φ) iff W_σ′ ⊨ φ, and past-tensed properties, once acquired, are retained (Proposition 17.22, No Revision of the Past). (C-c) Non-supervenience. Past-tensed properties do not supervene on the present’s categorical physical configuration: two present states could agree in every categorical respect while differing in their pasts (§5.2.1). (C-d) Single-outcome realism (§10.1). At each stage, each measurement has exactly one realized macroscopic outcome. Relational interpretations that make facts relative to observers are not available to CTGP. (C-e) Definite generative ordering (§6.4). Of two causally related stages, one precedes the other. (C-f) Generability (§12.2). There is a state S_σ on each stage and a law fixing P(S_{σ′} | S_σ) for σ′ > σ without reference to later data. Enlarging the state space is legitimate only if the added variables are physically instantiated on the present stage and have dynamics of their own. (C-g) Causal inheritance (More Info, §11). Whatever part of history can still affect the future must already be physically instantiated in the present; two present states that agree in every physically instantiated respect have futures governed identically by the laws, whatever their histories. (C-h) Continuous generation (CTGP – explained, Abstract and §5.4). Generation is a continuous causal-temporal progression, not a succession of disconnected states. (C-i) Truth versus recoverability (More Info, §§4, 9–10). Whether something happened is settled by the present world’s past-tensed profile; whether anyone can establish that it happened depends on surviving records and available signals. Some truths may be structurally unknowable. (C-j) Relational descriptive freedom. Different valid reference frames, clocks, and formulations may describe the same physical history differently; CTGP requires one history, not one description of it. (C-k) Emergent classical cosmic time (CTGP – explained, §6). In the general-relativistic regime, where the structure exists, the generative present is identified with the level sets of a regular cosmological time function. This commitment is conditional on the classical spacetime admitting that structure; §13.1 states the regularity results precisely. (C-l) No density-weighted generation (CTGP – explained, §11). Generation laws on which regions become real at rates set by local matter density fail CTGP’s consistency analysis; the generation rate may vary with cosmic epoch but not across space. One terminological point prevents a misunderstanding. The model below uses relational quantities: the transition events concern separations between particles, not positions relative to an external origin. This is not a relational interpretation in the sense rejected by (C-d). Whether a given event occurred is an absolute, frame-independent fact; what is relational is the quantity the event concerns, in the same way that “the distance between two stones is one metre” is an absolute fact about a relational quantity. ==================================================================================================== PART II. The Formalism ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 3. Kinematics: Perspective-Neutral States and Frame Reductions ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 3.1 The Perspective-Neutral State Space Let G be a finite group with identity e. Consider M + 1 subsystems labelled 0, 1, …, M, each with Hilbert space ℓ²(G), the space of complex functions on G, with orthonormal basis {|g⟩ : g ∈ G}. The basis state |g⟩ represents the subsystem having “orientation” g, for example being at site g of a lattice. This setting is chosen because every statement in it can be proved exactly. Definition 3.1 (QRF system). The kinematical space is H_kin = ℓ²(G)^{⊗(M+1)}. The group acts diagonally by U_h |g_0, g_1, …, g_M⟩ = |h g_0, h g_1, …, h g_M⟩, and the physical, or perspective-neutral, space is the invariant subspace H_phys = { Ψ ∈ H_kin : U_h Ψ = Ψ for all h ∈ G }, with projector Π = (1/|G|) Σ_{h∈G} U_h. A state Ψ in H_phys assigns amplitudes only to relations among subsystems: its wave function satisfies Ψ(h g_0, …, h g_M) = Ψ(g_0, …, g_M) for all h. 3.2 Frame Reductions Definition 3.2 (Frame reduction). Choosing subsystem A as the reference frame, the frame-relative space is H_A = ℓ²(G)^{⊗M}, describing the other M subsystems relative to A. The reduction map R_A : H_phys → H_A conditions the frame on the identity orientation and rescales: (R_A Ψ)(x_1, …, x_M) = √|G| · Ψ(e, x_1, …, x_M) (frame A taken as subsystem 0). Its inverse embeds a frame-relative state into the invariant sector: R_A^{−1} φ = |G|^{−1/2} Σ_{a∈G} Σ_{x} φ(x_1, …, x_M) |a, a x_1, …, a x_M⟩. The same definitions apply with any other subsystem as the frame, with the remaining subsystems listed in a fixed order. Proposition 3.3 (Reductions are unitary). For every subsystem A, R_A is a unitary map from H_phys onto H_A, with inverse given above. Proof. Let Ψ ∈ H_phys. Invariance gives Ψ(g_0, g_1, …, g_M) = Ψ(e, g_0^{−1} g_1, …, g_0^{−1} g_M). Write φ(x) = Ψ(e, x_1, …, x_M). Then ‖Ψ‖² = Σ_{g_0} Σ_{x} |φ(x)|² = |G| ‖φ‖², so ‖R_A Ψ‖ = √|G| ‖φ‖ = ‖Ψ‖. Conversely, for any φ ∈ H_A the vector R_A^{−1} φ defined above is invariant (left multiplication by h permutes the summation index a), and R_A R_A^{−1} φ = φ by direct substitution. Hence R_A is an isometry onto H_A, that is, unitary. □ Definition 3.4 (Valid frame change). For frames A and B, the frame change is V_{A→B} = R_B R_A^{−1} : H_A → H_B. Proposition 3.5 (Groupoid law). V_{A→B} is unitary; V_{A→A} = 1; V_{B→A} = V_{A→B}^{−1}; and V_{B→C} V_{A→B} = V_{A→C}. Proof. Immediate from Proposition 3.3 and Definition 3.4: V_{B→C} V_{A→B} = R_C R_B^{−1} R_B R_A^{−1} = R_C R_A^{−1}. □ Explicit form. If A is subsystem 0 and B is subsystem 1, then frame-A coordinates are x_j = g_0^{−1} g_j and frame-B coordinates are g_1^{−1} g_j. Substituting, g_1^{−1} g_0 = x_1^{−1} and g_1^{−1} g_j = x_1^{−1} x_j, so V_{A→B} |x_1, x_2, …, x_M⟩ = |x_1^{−1}, x_1^{−1} x_2, …, x_1^{−1} x_M⟩, where on the right the first slot is subsystem 0 (frame A) described relative to B. V_{A→B} permutes basis states, so a superposition over x_1 in frame A becomes, relative to B, a correlated state of frame A and all other subsystems. This is the familiar QRF effect: a frame-A product state can be a frame-B entangled state (§6.3). 3.3 Invariant Operators and Their Frame Forms An operator O on H_kin is invariant if U_h O = O U_h for all h. Invariant operators map H_phys to itself. Definition 3.6 (Frame form). The frame-A form of an invariant operator O is O^A = R_A O|_{H_phys} R_A^{−1}, an operator on H_A. Lemma 3.7 (Intertwining). For every invariant O and all frames A, B: V_{A→B} O^A V_{A→B}^{−1} = O^B. Moreover, for every Ψ ∈ H_phys, ‖O Ψ‖ = ‖O^A R_A Ψ‖ and ⟨Ψ, O Ψ⟩ = ⟨R_A Ψ, O^A R_A Ψ⟩. Proof. V_{A→B} O^A V_{A→B}^{−1} = R_B R_A^{−1} R_A O R_A^{−1} R_A R_B^{−1} = R_B O R_B^{−1} = O^B. The second statement holds because R_A is unitary and O^A R_A = R_A O on H_phys. □ Remark 3.8 (Every frame-A operator is a frame form). Because R_A is unitary, every operator X on H_A is the frame-A form of an invariant operator, namely R_A^{−1} X R_A on H_phys extended by zero on the orthogonal complement of H_phys. (This operator commutes with every U_h because U_h acts as the identity on H_phys and preserves its complement.) The invariance requirement therefore does not restrict what a single frame can say. It restricts the relation between what different frames say: if frame A uses X, frame B must use V_{A→B} X V_{A→B}^{−1}, not an operator that merely looks the same in B’s coordinates. Section 5.3 turns this into the validity condition for frame-relative laws, and §8 shows numerically that violating it produces a different world. Remark 3.9 (Covariance is built into the framework, not discovered). By Lemma 3.7 and Remark 3.8, any law that is well defined on H_phys has frame forms related by the frame-change unitaries. Frame covariance of the dynamics below is therefore a consequence of the perspective-neutral framework, not an independent finding. The substantive assumption is that the universe is closed, so that laws act on H_phys rather than on H_kin with an external frame. The contribution of this preprint lies elsewhere: in showing that a CTGP interpretation can be placed on such dynamics consistently, in proving what that interpretation implies, and in identifying what CTGP adds (§11). ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 4. Dynamics, Ontology, and the Complete State ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 4.1 The Generative Law Stages are indexed by σ in a discrete, linearly ordered set, written σ_0 < σ_1 < σ_2 < …, with σ′ denoting the successor of σ. The discrete index is used in Part II because every statement about it can be proved by finite induction; §9 gives the continuous-time form and states how the two are related. Definition 4.1 (Admissible generative law). An admissible generative law consists of an invariant unitary W on H_kin (the deterministic part of one stage-to-stage transition) and an instrument {F_k : k ∈ 𝒦} of invariant operators on H_kin with Σ_k F_k† F_k = 1. The outcome set 𝒦 is countable, and its elements are called transition events. Definition 4.2 (Generative kernel). For a normalized Ψ_σ ∈ H_phys, the transition to σ′ is the random pair (k, Ψ_{σ′}) with Pr(k | Ψ_σ) = ‖F_k W Ψ_σ‖², Ψ_{σ′} = F_k W Ψ_σ / ‖F_k W Ψ_σ‖. The kernel K(Ψ_{σ′}, k | Ψ_σ) is the joint distribution so defined. Because W and F_k are invariant, Ψ_{σ′} lies in H_phys again. States are rays: Ψ and e^{iθ} Ψ represent the same state, written Ψ ≐ e^{iθ} Ψ. A history of length n is a sequence ω = (k_1, …, k_n) of transition events. Its probability given Ψ_{σ_0} is the product of the successive transition probabilities, equivalently ‖F_{k_n} W ⋯ F_{k_1} W Ψ_{σ_0}‖². 4.2 Primitive Ontology: What Exists at a Stage A stochastic outcome label is not by itself an ontology. The following reading states what the formalism is taken to represent. Ontological Reading 4.3 (Primitive ontology). At stage σ, the present world consists of: (i) the present physical state [Ψ_σ], a ray in H_phys. It is physically real and governs the probabilities of all future events, but it is not itself material stuff located in space; it is a holistic state of the relational degrees of freedom; (ii) the present world’s past-tensed profile L_σ (§4.4). The material ontology consists of transition events. A transition event is a primitive occurrence: something that happens, once, in the generation of a stage from its predecessor. When it occurs it is part of the generating present; at every later stage it is something that was the case, recorded in L and carried forward causally through its effect on Ψ. Macroscopic facts, such as where a pointer points, are patterns of transition events. This is the flash ontology of GRW-type theories, with the wave function read realistically as part of the present physical state. An alternative reading treats the wave function as nomological, so that the events are the only physical ontology. On that reading, Tier 2 of the determinacy criterion (§5.1) would concern the law’s current parameter rather than a physical state, but Tiers 1 and 3 and every theorem below would be unchanged. Reading 4.3 is adopted because CTGP identifies S_σ as the present physical state governing successor generation (C-f). 4.3 Primitive Events Versus Sharp Relational Properties Each transition event k has an associated effect E_k = F_k† F_k, which is a positive operator on H_phys. The effect determines the probability of the event: Pr(k | Ψ_σ) = ⟨W Ψ_σ, E_k W Ψ_σ⟩. The event and the effect are different kinds of thing. The effect is part of the law. The event is an additional fact: that, of all the events the law made possible, this one occurred. That additional fact is what single-outcome realism (C-d) asserts and what Everettian ontology denies. In particular, an event concerning a relational quantity need not be the possession of a sharp value of that quantity. In the model of Part III, an event (i, j, d) is a flash centred on the separation d between particles i and j, with finite width s. Its occurrence does not make the proposition “the separation of i and j is d” true. Proposition 4.4 (Finite-width events do not create sharp values). Let F_k = Σ_r c_k(r) P(r) where {P(r)} is a complete family of orthogonal invariant projectors and c_k(r) ≠ 0 for every r. If, before the event, W Ψ_σ is not an eigenvector of a given P(r), then after the event Ψ_{σ′} is not an eigenvector of P(r) either. Proof. Since F_k is diagonal in the decomposition {P(r)} with nonzero coefficients, P(r′) Ψ_{σ′} ∝ c_k(r′) P(r′) W Ψ_σ is nonzero exactly when P(r′) W Ψ_σ is nonzero. The set of r′ with nonzero component is therefore unchanged by the event, and a vector is an eigenvector of P(r) (with eigenvalue 1 or 0) exactly when that set is {r} or omits r. □ So in the finite-width model the event is a Tier 1 fact, while the sharp-value proposition about separation remains without a determinate value (Tier 3 of §5.1). The sharp limit s → 0, in which c_k(r) vanishes for r ≠ d, is the special case in which an event does produce a sharp value. This distinction answers the objection that a flash ontology merely renames a measurement outcome: the event is not the readout of a pre-existing value, and not the acquisition of a sharp one, but a primitive occurrence whose probability the law fixes. 4.4 The Complete Ontological State and the Truth Profile Definition 4.5 (Complete ontological state). The complete ontological state at stage σ is Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ), where S_σ = [Ψ_σ] is the ray of the perspective-neutral state, and L_σ, the Lucretian truth profile, is a set of past-tensed propositions of two kinds: Had(σ″, [Ψ_{σ″}]), read “at stage σ″ the state was [Ψ_{σ″}]”, for σ″ < σ; Had(σ″ → σ‴, k), read “in the generation of σ‴ from σ″, transition event k occurred”, for σ‴ ≤ σ. The second kind extends the stage-indexed properties Had(σ′, φ) of CTGP – explained, Definition 17.20, to transitions. This adds no commitment: a transition from σ″ to σ‴ is something that occurred, and the present world can have been such that it occurred. Definition 4.6 (Truth-profile update). When the transition σ → σ′ realizes event k, L_{σ′} = L_σ ∪ ΔL_{σ→σ′}, ΔL_{σ→σ′} = { Had(σ, [Ψ_σ]), Had(σ → σ′, k) }. The truths acquired in a transition are exactly two: the state that was present has become past, and the event that occurred in the transition is something that was the case. The initial condition is L_{σ_0} = ∅ and S_{σ_0} = [Ψ_{σ_0}]. 4.5 Admissible Histories The truth profile is not an arbitrary set of past-tensed propositions. It ranges over a space fixed by the law. Histories, actuality, and futurity. Three uses of the word history must be kept apart, because the mathematics speaks of histories in a sense that carries no ontological commitment. The actual past history at a stage consists of the states and events that have occurred and are now past. Past stages do not continue to exist; truths about them are grounded in the present world’s Lucretian profile. The actual history through the present, where that phrase is needed, adds the presently existing state to its past; it does not mean that the present has already become past. An admissible history, by contrast, is a mathematical object: a possible sequence of events permitted by the law, an element of the probability space defined below. The set of admissible histories lists the alternatives the law permits. It does not represent alternative histories that exist side by side, and a probability measure on future trajectories describes possible continuations of the present, not an existing future. Within CTGP no state or event exists as future. Each state first exists as the present, and each realized transition becomes part of the actual past only after it has occurred. The actual past is therefore not a completed structure containing future events: it grows as successive presents are generated, without revision of what has occurred. What has occurred cannot later become something else (C-b), although records, memories, and evidence about it can change, which is the distinction between truth and recoverability (C-i). Only one history is ever realized. At each stage its actual past consists exclusively of events that have already occurred, and the other admissible histories are possibilities represented by the law, not additional existing histories. When the text speaks of a complete history, completeness is relative to a specified finite stage, or to a trajectory used in probabilistic analysis; it never means that the future has occurred or exists tenselessly. Definition 4.7 (Admissible histories and complete states). Fix the law and the initial state Ψ_{σ_0}. Let ℋ_n = { ω ∈ 𝒦^n : Pr(ω | Ψ_{σ_0}) > 0 } be the set of admissible histories of length n, the possible event sequences of nonzero probability. For ω ∈ ℋ_n write Ψ_m(ω) for the state after the first m events (Ψ_0(ω) = Ψ_{σ_0}), and define its truth profile L(ω) = { Had(σ_m, [Ψ_m(ω)]) : 0 ≤ m < n } ∪ { Had(σ_{m−1} → σ_m, k_m) : 1 ≤ m ≤ n }. The admissible truth profiles and admissible complete states at stage σ_n are ℒ_n = { L(ω) : ω ∈ ℋ_n }, 𝔒_n = { ([Ψ_n(ω)], L(ω)) : ω ∈ ℋ_n }. Theorem 4.8 (Characterization of admissible profiles). A set L of past-tensed propositions belongs to ℒ_n if and only if all of the following hold: (o) Restricted language. Every element of L is a past-tensed proposition of one of the two forms of Definition 4.5: a state proposition Had(σ_m, [Ψ]) with m ≥ 0 and Ψ ∈ H_phys, or a transition proposition Had(σ_{m−1} → σ_m, k) with m ≥ 1 and k ∈ 𝒦. (a) One event per transition. For each m = 1, …, n, L contains exactly one proposition of the form Had(σ_{m−1} → σ_m, k). (b) One state per past stage. For each m = 0, …, n−1, L contains exactly one proposition of the form Had(σ_m, [Ψ]). (c) Correct initial state. The state proposition for σ_0 is Had(σ_0, [Ψ_{σ_0}]). (d) Lawful consistency. For each m = 1, …, n−1, if L contains Had(σ_{m−1}, [Ψ]) and Had(σ_{m−1} → σ_m, k), then F_k W Ψ ≠ 0 and the state proposition for σ_m is Had(σ_m, [F_k W Ψ]); and, for the last event k_n, F_{k_n} W Ψ ≠ 0 where [Ψ] is the state recorded for σ_{n−1}. (e) Past-stage restriction. Every state proposition in L is indexed to a past stage σ_m with 0 ≤ m ≤ n−1, and every transition proposition is indexed to a transition σ_{m−1} → σ_m with 1 ≤ m ≤ n. Thus L contains no state proposition about the present stage σ_n and no proposition of either kind about any later stage. When these hold, the history ω with L = L(ω) is unique. Moreover, admissible profiles are nested: if ω ∈ ℋ_n and ω|m is its initial segment of length m ≤ n, then L(ω|m) ⊆ L(ω). Proof. (⇒) Each condition holds by construction of L(ω) and the definition of ℋ_n. (⇐) By (a), reading off the event proposition for each transition gives a unique sequence ω = (k_1, …, k_n). By (c) and (d), induction on m shows that the state proposition for σ_m is Had(σ_m, [Ψ_m(ω)]) and that each factor of Pr(ω | Ψ_{σ_0}) is nonzero, so ω ∈ ℋ_n. By (o), every element of L is a state or transition proposition. By (e), its state propositions concern only the stages σ_0, …, σ_{n−1} and its transition propositions only the n transitions up to σ_n; by (a) and (b), each of these carries exactly one proposition, namely the one just identified. Hence L = L(ω). Uniqueness follows because ω is read off from L. Nesting holds because the propositions defining L(ω|m) are among those defining L(ω). □ Theorem 4.8 makes precise the requirements that a truth profile must meet: propositions of the two permitted forms only, exactly one realized event per transition, state propositions only about past stages and consistent with the law, no incompatible state assignments, preservation of acquired truths, and no claim that an unrealized future event has occurred. The restriction of state propositions to past stages matters: the present state is part of S, not of the past-tensed profile L, and a set that added a state proposition about σ_n to an admissible profile would satisfy every other condition without belonging to ℒ_n. Theorem 4.8 establishes that the Lucretian profile is a consistent interpretation of the model’s realized histories. It does not establish that Lucretian properties exist in nature; that is a philosophical thesis of CTGP, which the model shows to be coherent. 4.6 L and S: Dynamical Idleness, Accuracy, and Non-Supervenience Proposition 4.9 (Dynamical idleness of L). For any two admissible complete states Ω = (S, L) and Ω* = (S, L*) with the same S, the joint distribution of the next state and next event is the same. Proof. The kernel of Definition 4.2 takes Ψ_σ as its only input. □ Corollary 4.10 (The future is conditionally independent of the past given the present). Let 𝔉⁺_σ denote the possible future trajectory (k_{σ′}, Ψ_{σ′}, k_{σ″}, Ψ_{σ″}, …) after stage σ, regarded as a random variable. Then for every admissible Ω_σ, Pr(𝔉⁺_σ | S_σ, L_σ) = Pr(𝔉⁺_σ | S_σ). Proof. The distribution of any finite segment of 𝔉⁺_σ is a product of kernels, each depending only on the preceding state (Proposition 4.9); the full trajectory measure is determined by its finite segments. □ The measure in Corollary 4.10 is a probability measure over possible continuations of the present state. Defining it does not presuppose that any future trajectory exists: exactly one continuation becomes actual, event by event, as the corresponding transitions occur. Corollary 4.10 is the formal version of (C-g). It separates three things that CTGP holds apart: the past being ontologically definite (L is determinate), the past being recoverable from present physical information (a matter of what S encodes, §5.5), and the past having further causal influence (it has none beyond what S carries). The first does not entail either of the others. L is part of the complete ontology but not a dynamical variable, so generability’s restriction on enlarging the state space (C-f) is not triggered: L does not enlarge the space on which the law acts. Proposition 4.11 (Accuracy). For n ≥ 1 and ω ∈ ℋ_n, the present state is fixed by the two most recent entries of L(ω) together with the law: if Had(σ_{n−1}, [Ψ]) and Had(σ_{n−1} → σ_n, k) belong to L(ω), then S_{σ_n} = [F_k W Ψ]. At the initial stage σ_0, L is empty and S_{σ_0} is an initial condition not fixed by L. Proof. Definitions 4.2 and 4.6. □ Proposition 4.11 is a consistency condition, not a dependence of the dynamics on L. Both S_{σ_n} and ΔL are outputs of the same transition, and L does not select k. Reconstructing S from L uses the law; reconstructing earlier states uses the initial-state entry Had(σ_0, [Ψ_{σ_0}]) as well. The non-supervenience thesis (C-c) says that a present state does not fix its past. Under presentism the initial stage is a past stage like any other, so the past includes the initial state. Two senses of non-supervenience should therefore be distinguished. The weak sense fixes the initial state and asks whether different event sequences can end in the same present. The strong sense asks whether the present state fixes the complete past, including the initial state. Proposition 4.12 (Strong non-supervenience for invertible laws). Suppose W is unitary and every F_k is invertible on H_phys. Then for every n ≥ 1, every present ray [Φ] in H_phys, and every sequence ω ∈ 𝒦^n, there is exactly one initial ray [Ψ_{σ_0}], depending on ω, from which ω has nonzero probability and ends at [Φ]. Consequently, across possible initial conditions, every present ray can be reached along every finite event sequence, and the complete past, including the initial condition, is not a function of the present state. The proposition does not assert that a single fixed initial state reaches a given present ray along every event sequence. Proof. Define Ψ_{σ_0} ∝ (F_{k_n} W ⋯ F_{k_1} W)^{−1} Φ, which exists because each factor is invertible. Running ω forward from this state returns a vector proportional to Φ, and each transition probability is nonzero because an invertible operator annihilates no nonzero vector. Any other initial ray reaching [Φ] along ω is mapped to the same ray by the invertible product, so it coincides with this one. □ Proposition 4.13 (Weak non-supervenience in a special regime). In the model of Part III with sharp flashes (s → 0) and no hopping (J = 0), there exist an initial state and two distinct histories ω ≠ ω* ∈ ℋ_2 that end in the same present ray. Proof. Proposition 8.1. □ In the model of Part III with finite width s > 0 and hit probability λ < 1, every Kraus operator is invertible (§7.2), so Proposition 4.12 applies: for all such parameter values, the complete past is not fixed by the present state, in the strong sense. Weak non-supervenience is established in the special regime of Proposition 4.13. Whether it holds for generic finite-width parameters is open; a numerical search over 4,096 three-stage histories at generic parameters found no exact coincidences (§8.3). Strong non-supervenience is the sense relevant to CTGP, since under presentism the initial condition is one more fact about the past. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 5. The Criteria ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 5.1 The Determinacy Criterion (Task T1) Definition 5.1 (Determinate propositions). At stage σ, with complete state Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ), the determinate propositions fall into three tiers. Tier 1 (events). For each past transition σ″ → σ‴ with σ‴ ≤ σ and each k ∈ 𝒦, the proposition “in the generation of σ‴, event k occurred” is determinate. It is true iff Had(σ″ → σ‴, k) ∈ L_σ. Boolean combinations of Tier 1 propositions are determinate. Tier 2 (states). For each σ″ ≤ σ, the proposition “at σ″ the state was [Ψ]” is determinate. For σ″ < σ it is true iff Had(σ″, [Ψ]) ∈ L_σ; for σ″ = σ it is true iff S_σ = [Ψ]. Tier 3 (sharp properties). For an invariant projector Q and σ″ ≤ σ, the proposition “Q held at σ″” is determinately true iff Q Ψ_{σ″} = Ψ_{σ″}, determinately false iff Q Ψ_{σ″} = 0, and otherwise has no determinate truth value. No proposition about a stage later than σ is determinate at σ. Future-directed claims have only the probabilities assigned by the kernel. Tier 1 carries single-outcome realism. In a flash ontology the macroscopic world is the pattern of events, so “the pointer pointed left” is a Tier 1 fact about which events occurred, not a Tier 3 fact about the wave function. This avoids the tails problem of collapse models: collapse never produces an exact eigenstate of position, so Tier 3 would almost never make a macroscopic position determinate, but Tier 1 does not depend on exact eigenstates (Proposition 4.4). Tier 3 is included because sharp-property talk is common and because, in the sharp-flash limit, events do produce sharp values (§8.2). Proposition 5.2 (Determinacy is frame-independent). Let τ_A be the frame-A translation of propositions defined in §5.2. A proposition p is determinate at σ iff its frame-A translation τ_A(p) is determinate in the frame-A description, with the same truth value, for every frame A. Proof. Tier 1: τ_A leaves the event label and the stage indices unchanged, and membership in L is preserved by the definition of τ_A. Tier 2: [Ψ] ↦ [R_A Ψ] is a bijection on rays. Tier 3: Q Ψ = Ψ iff Q^A R_A Ψ = R_A Ψ, and Q Ψ = 0 iff Q^A R_A Ψ = 0, by Lemma 3.7. □ In this framework, “definite relative to frame A but not relative to frame B” never occurs for the same proposition. What does occur is that one proposition takes different forms in different frames: a single-coordinate statement in frame A can be a statement about a correlation between two coordinates in frame B (§6.3). A different form is not a different fact. 5.2 Cross-Frame Identification (Task T2) Definition 5.3 (Frame description). A frame-A description at stage σ is a triple D^A_σ = (σ, ψ^A_σ, L^A_σ), where ψ^A_σ is a ray in H_A and L^A_σ is a set of frame-A past-tensed propositions of the forms Had(σ″, [φ]) with φ ∈ H_A and Had(σ″ → σ‴, k, E^A_k), where E^A_k = (F^A_k)† F^A_k is the frame-A form of the effect of event k. Definition 5.4 (Translations). The frame-A translation τ_A maps neutral propositions to frame-A propositions: τ_A Had(σ″, [Ψ]) = Had(σ″, [R_A Ψ]), τ_A Had(σ″ → σ‴, k) = Had(σ″ → σ‴, k, E^A_k). Because R_A is unitary and E^A_k is determined by k, τ_A is a bijection. The frame-change translation is τ_{A→B} = τ_B ∘ τ_A^{−1}; it replaces each state [φ] by [V_{A→B} φ] and each effect E^A_k by V_{A→B} E^A_k V_{A→B}^{−1} = E^B_k, keeping the stage indices and the event label. Criterion 5.5 (Cross-frame identification). Frame descriptions D^A_σ and D^B_τ describe the same present world, with the same realized history, iff (C1) Same stage: σ = τ. (C2) State correspondence: ψ^B ≐ V_{A→B} ψ^A. (C3) History correspondence: L^B = τ_{A→B}(L^A). Theorem 5.6 (Common perspective-neutral pre-image). D^A_σ and D^B_σ satisfy (C1)–(C3) iff there is a single neutral complete state Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ) with ψ^A ≐ R_A Ψ_σ, ψ^B ≐ R_B Ψ_σ, L^A = τ_A(L_σ), and L^B = τ_B(L_σ). Proof. (⇐) Given Ω_σ, ψ^B ≐ R_B Ψ_σ = R_B R_A^{−1} R_A Ψ_σ ≐ V_{A→B} ψ^A, which is (C2), and L^B = τ_B(L_σ) = τ_B τ_A^{−1} τ_A(L_σ) = τ_{A→B}(L^A), which is (C3). (⇒) Given (C1)–(C3), define Ψ_σ = R_A^{−1} ψ^A and L_σ = τ_A^{−1}(L^A). Then R_A Ψ_σ = ψ^A by construction, R_B Ψ_σ = V_{A→B} ψ^A ≐ ψ^B by (C2), τ_A(L_σ) = L^A, and τ_B(L_σ) = τ_{A→B}(L^A) = L^B by (C3). □ The content of Theorem 5.6 is clearest when stated in terms of orbits. Definition 5.7 (Description orbit). Let 𝔉 be the set of admissible frames. For a complete state Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ), its description orbit is 𝒟(Ω_σ) = { (σ, [R_A Ψ_σ], τ_A(L_σ)) : A ∈ 𝔉 }. Proposition 5.8 (Orbit identification). (i) Two frame descriptions satisfy Criterion 5.5 iff they belong to the same description orbit. (ii) Distinct complete states have disjoint orbits, so the orbits partition the set of frame descriptions. (iii) Consequently, satisfying Criterion 5.5 is an equivalence relation. (iv) If Ω_σ is admissible (Ω_σ ∈ 𝔒_n), every member of its orbit describes that admissible complete state. Proof. (i) is Theorem 5.6 restated in terms of orbits. (ii) Each frame description belongs to the description space of exactly one frame A. If two complete states Ω and Ω* share an orbit member, that member is (σ, [R_A Ψ], τ_A(L)) = (σ, [R_A Ψ*], τ_A(L*)) for a single frame A, and injectivity of R_A and τ_A gives [Ψ] = [Ψ*] and L = L*. (iii) follows from (i) and (ii). (iv) holds by definition of the orbit. □ Proposition 5.9 ((C3) is not redundant). There exist frame descriptions satisfying (C1) and (C2) but not (C3); they describe different realized histories with the same present state. More precisely: (a) For complete histories, in which the initial condition counts as part of the past, such pairs exist for every invertible law. (b) Within a single space ℋ_n with a fixed initial state, such pairs exist in the special regime of Proposition 4.13. Whether they exist for generic finite-width parameters is open (§8.3). Proof. (a) By Proposition 4.12, for any present ray [Φ] and two distinct event sequences there are initial rays from which each sequence ends at [Φ]. The two complete histories have different truth profiles, since their transition propositions differ. Take the frame-A description of one and the frame-B description of the other: (C1) and (C2) hold, and (C3) fails. (b) The same argument applies to the two histories of Proposition 4.13 (Proposition 8.1), which share an initial state. □ Case (a) compares descriptions drawn from admissible spaces with different initial conditions. Under presentism the initial condition is a past fact like any other, so this is the comparison relevant to CTGP; case (b) shows that the phenomenon also occurs, in a special regime, with the initial condition held fixed. Three levels of identity can now be distinguished, and the point at which interpretation enters can be located exactly. Representational equivalence. Two descriptions are related by a valid QRF transformation: (C1) and (C2) hold, so the two quantum states are reductions of one perspective-neutral state. This is a mathematical relation between representations. Dynamical equivalence. The two frames’ laws are related by the frame-change unitary (Definition 5.11), so their probability laws and event records transform consistently (Theorem 5.12). This is a mathematical relation between laws. Ontological identity. The two descriptions concern one realized present and one realized history. Within the model, this is membership in one description orbit (Proposition 5.8). The first two levels are theorems. The step from orbit membership to ontological identity is not a theorem of quantum mechanics. It is the following interpretive thesis, which is what CTGP adds. Thesis 5.10 (Orbit realism). Each description orbit of an admissible complete state represents exactly one realized present together with exactly one realized history. Frame descriptions in one orbit are descriptions of the same world; descriptions in different orbits are descriptions of different possible worlds, at most one of which is actual. Thesis 5.10 gives the precise answer to the question how one can establish that two descriptions refer to the same realized history rather than assuming it. Four observations make up the answer. First, given the thesis, identity of history is not an extra posit laid over frame descriptions; it is a definite condition, (C3), that can fail. Second, it cannot be settled by the quantum state alone: Proposition 5.9 shows that state correspondence is compatible with different histories, so identity of history is carried by L, which is a direct argument for including the Lucretian profile in the complete ontology. Third, correspondence is preserved by the dynamics (Theorem 5.12(c)), so frames cannot drift into describing different histories, and correspondence is not maintained by stipulation at each stage. Fourth, whether two descriptions do in fact correspond is an ontological question, and whether anyone can verify that they do is an epistemic question; §5.5 shows that these come apart and that Criterion 5.5 is an exact criterion of identity, not in general an experimentally decidable one. 5.3 Valid Transformations of the Law Definition 5.11 (Frame-relative laws describing one world). Frame-relative laws (W^A, {F^A_k}) on H_A and (W^B, {F^B_k}) on H_B describe one world iff W^B = V_{A→B} W^A V_{A→B}^{−1} and F^B_k = V_{A→B} F^A_k V_{A→B}^{−1} for every k. Equivalently, by Remark 3.8, both are frame forms of one admissible neutral law. Theorem 5.12 (Covariance of generation). Let (W, {F_k}) be an admissible neutral law, with frame forms (W^A, {F^A_k}) and (W^B, {F^B_k}). Let Ψ_{σ_0} ∈ H_phys, ψ^A_{σ_0} = R_A Ψ_{σ_0}, and ψ^B_{σ_0} = R_B Ψ_{σ_0}. Then: (a) For every finite sequence ω = (k_1, …, k_n), the probability of ω is the same whether computed in frame A, in frame B, or in the neutral description. (b) Along every history ω of nonzero probability, the frame-evolved states satisfy ψ^B_{σ_j} ≐ V_{A→B} ψ^A_{σ_j} for j = 0, …, n. (c) Consequently the probability measure over histories is frame-independent, the sets ℋ_n of admissible histories coincide in all frames, and if D^A_{σ_0} and D^B_{σ_0} correspond, then for every realized history the descriptions D^A_{σ_n} and D^B_{σ_n} generated from them correspond. Proof. By induction on n. Suppose ψ^A_{σ_j} ≐ R_A Ψ_{σ_j} and ψ^B_{σ_j} ≐ R_B Ψ_{σ_j}. By Lemma 3.7, Pr^A(k) = ‖F^A_k W^A R_A Ψ_{σ_j}‖² = ‖R_A F_k W Ψ_{σ_j}‖² = ‖F_k W Ψ_{σ_j}‖², and likewise for frame B. The updated frame states are R_A Ψ_{σ_{j+1}} and R_B Ψ_{σ_{j+1}} after normalization, which gives (b). The probability of a sequence is the product of conditional probabilities, which gives (a) and the equality of the sets ℋ_n. For (c), the truth-profile updates in the two frames add τ_A(ΔL) and τ_B(ΔL) for the same neutral ΔL, so (C3) is preserved; (C1) is preserved because both frames advance the same stage index; and (C2) is (b). □ Corollary 5.13 (Failure of same-form laws). If frame B adopts a law with the same matrices as frame A’s law but written in B’s coordinates, that is, W^B = W^A and F^B_k = F^A_k as matrices, then in general the two frames assign different probabilities to the same histories and do not describe one world. Proof. Such a pair satisfies Definition 5.11 only if W^A and every F^A_k commute with V_{A→B}, which fails in general. Section 8 gives an instance with total-variation distance 0.180 between the two measures over histories. □ Corollary 5.13 shows what “valid transformation” excludes: it is not enough for each frame to use a law that looks natural from its own point of view; the laws must be related by the frame-change unitary. This is the QRF analogue of requiring that a law be written in covariant form. Corollary 5.14 (Covariant generability). In every frame A, the future is conditionally independent of the past given the present: Pr^A(𝔉⁺_σ | ψ^A_σ, L^A_σ) = Pr^A(𝔉⁺_σ | ψ^A_σ), and this conditional distribution is the image under the frame change of the corresponding distribution in any other frame B, with event labels unchanged and states mapped by V_{A→B}. Proof. Apply Corollary 4.10 to the frame-A law, which is an admissible law on H_A because W^A is unitary and the F^A_k form an instrument (Lemma 3.7). The relation between frames follows from Theorem 5.12(a)–(b), applied from stage σ onward with initial states ψ^A_σ and ψ^B_σ ≐ V_{A→B} ψ^A_σ. □ Corollary 5.14 connects CTGP’s generability and causal-inheritance commitments directly to the QRF formalism. It is not merely that the operators transform covariantly: the very property that makes the law generable from the present, and that makes the past causally idle beyond what the present carries, holds in every frame and is preserved by every valid frame change. 5.4 Truth Preservation (Task T3) Definition 5.15 (Truth in a frame description). A frame-A proposition is true in D^A_σ iff it belongs to L^A_σ (Tiers 1 and 2 for earlier stages), or it is a present-stage Tier 2 proposition about [ψ^A_σ] that holds, or it is a Tier 3 proposition whose projector Q^A satisfies Q^A ψ^A = ψ^A at the relevant stage. Theorem 5.16 (Truth preservation). Suppose D^A_σ and D^B_σ satisfy Criterion 5.5. Then for every frame-A proposition p that is determinate in D^A_σ, τ_{A→B}(p) is determinate in D^B_σ and has the same truth value. Moreover, under the generative law, truth preservation continues to hold at every later stage of every realized history. Proof. For members of L, (C3) gives p ∈ L^A iff τ_{A→B}(p) ∈ L^B. For present-stage Tier 2 propositions, (C2) gives [ψ^A] = [φ] iff [V_{A→B} ψ^A] = [V_{A→B} φ]. For Tier 3, Q^A ψ^A = ψ^A iff (V Q^A V^{−1})(V ψ^A) = V ψ^A, and V Q^A V^{−1} = Q^B by Lemma 3.7. The final clause follows from Theorem 5.12(c). □ Proposition 5.17 (No revision across frames). For σ < σ″ and any frame A, a past-tensed frame-A proposition about a stage before σ is true in D^A_σ iff it is true in D^A_{σ″}. The same holds for its translation into any other frame. Proof. Admissible profiles are nested (Theorem 4.8), and τ_A is applied element-wise. Combine with Theorem 5.16. □ Proposition 5.17 combines CTGP’s No Revision of the Past (Proposition 17.22) with frame covariance: changing frames does not revise the past any more than the passage of stages does. 5.5 Ontological Identity, Physical Equivalence, and Observational Indistinguishability If L is dynamically idle and corresponds to no present observable, how could anyone determine which history occurred? The answer requires separating three relations that are easily run together. Definition 5.18 (Three relations between histories). Fix the law. A complete finite history through stage σ_n, h = (Ψ_{σ_0}, ω), consists of an initial state and an admissible event sequence of length n, so that it specifies everything from the initial state through σ_n; write S(h) for its present state and L(h) for its truth profile. For two complete finite histories h_1 and h_2 of the same length: h_1 ∼_phys h_2 (same present physical state) iff S(h_1) = S(h_2); h_1 ∼_hist h_2 (same realized history) iff L(h_1) = L(h_2); h_1 ∼_obs h_2 relative to a class 𝒪 of observers and a tolerance ε (observationally indistinguishable) iff no procedure available to 𝒪 discriminates h_1 from h_2, given equal prior probabilities, with error probability below ε. Proposition 5.19 (How the relations are ordered). Let 𝒪 be any class of observers whose evidence consists of the outcomes of measurements on the present state. (i) ∼_hist implies ∼_phys, by accuracy (Proposition 4.11; for n ≥ 1). (ii) ∼_phys implies ∼_obs for every tolerance ε ≤ 1/2, because identical present states yield identical statistics for every measurement, so no procedure does better than guessing. (iii) ∼_phys does not imply ∼_hist (Propositions 4.12 and 4.13). (iv) More generally, if the present states of h_1 and h_2 have overlap |⟨Ψ(h_1), Ψ(h_2)⟩| = c, then with equal prior probabilities no measurement on the present state discriminates them with error probability below (1 − √(1 − c²)) / 2 (Helstrom 1976). Histories whose present states are close are therefore observationally nearly indistinguishable even when they are not physically equivalent. Proof. (i)–(iii) are as stated. (iv) is the Helstrom bound for discriminating two pure states with equal priors. □ The relations are thus nested in one direction and strictly separated in the other: same history implies same present, which implies indistinguishability by present observation, and neither converse holds. In the model with finite-width flashes, Proposition 4.12 shows that a given present state can be reached along every event sequence from some initial state, so for present-state observers every fact in L beyond what S encodes is structurally unknowable in the sense of More Info, §4. The numerical search in §8.3 found two distinct histories whose present states have overlap 0.985, for which the Helstrom bound gives a minimum error probability of 0.414: the histories are nearly indistinguishable by any present measurement, though they are different histories. This is CTGP’s distinction between truth and recoverability (C-i), stated as a theorem about the model. It also answers the objection that Criterion 5.5 is not operationally checkable when L is inaccessible. The reply is that Criterion 5.5 is an exact criterion of ontological identity, not a decision procedure. Agents in different frames check correspondence through records: present physical structures that carry evidence about which events occurred. Agreement of records, translated by τ_{A→B}, is evidence that (C3) holds; disagreement is evidence that it fails. Where records are erased or never formed, the question has an answer that no one may be able to learn. This limitation belongs to CTGP itself, not to the criterion: the boundary of knowledge is not the boundary of fact (More Info, §4). 5.6 Where L Sits Relative to the Quantum State (Task T4) The preceding results can be collected into a single statement. Theorem 5.20 (Location of the Lucretian profile). In any model satisfying Definitions 4.1–4.7: (i) L is generated, not generating. L_σ is a function of the actual past history, which the mathematics represents as an initial segment of a sample path of the kernel K, realized only up to the present stage, and K takes only S_σ as input (Proposition 4.9, Corollary 4.10). (ii) L ranges over an exactly specified space. The admissible profiles are characterized by Theorem 4.8. (iii) L is not reducible to S. For invertible laws, every present ray is reachable along every finite event sequence from a suitably chosen initial ray, so the complete past is not a function of S (Proposition 4.12). (iv) L is accurate. Together with the law, L fixes S at every stage after the first (Proposition 4.11), and Had(σ″, φ) ∈ L_σ iff φ was the case at σ″. (v) L is frame-covariant. Its frame-relative forms are related by the translations τ_{A→B}, and identity of history across frames is exactly agreement of L modulo translation (Proposition 5.8). (vi) L carries no extra dynamical assumptions. No law refers to L, so including L in the complete ontology adds no variable that affects successor generation, and it does not require any past-tensed property to correspond to a measurable quantum observable. Point (vi) answers the concern that including past-tensed properties in the complete ontology might require treating them as additional variables that influence the dynamics, or might require every ontologically real property to have an independently measurable observable. Neither follows. Tier 1 truths about which events occurred are not eigenvalues of any present observable: by Proposition 4.12, no present measurement can distinguish the complete histories that end in a given state. They are nonetheless facts, and they are the facts that distinguish those histories. Schematically, the order of determination at one transition is: S_σ ──K──▶ k ──▶ S_{σ′} = [F_k W Ψ_σ] and ΔL_{σ→σ′} = { Had(σ, S_σ), Had(σ→σ′, k) }. The kernel produces the event. The event, together with the law, produces the next state and the next past-tensed truths. Nothing flows from L back into K. 5.7 Why a Primitive Lucretian Profile Rather Than an Encoding of the Event History A natural question remains. Why should the truth profile be regarded as a primitive ontological feature of the present world, rather than as a formal encoding of the realized event history, as it would be in an ordinary single-outcome flash ontology that simply associates historical propositions with earlier events? The mathematics cannot answer this question, and it is important to see why. Proposition 5.21 (Formal equivalence). For fixed law and initial state, the map ω ↦ L(ω) is a bijection from ℋ_n onto ℒ_n, and it commutes with the frame translations τ_A. Proof. Surjectivity holds by definition of ℒ_n, and injectivity by the uniqueness clause of Theorem 4.8. Commutation with τ_A holds because τ_A leaves stage indices and event labels unchanged and maps states by R_A. □ Mathematically, then, the truth profile is interchangeable with the event history: every theorem of this preprint could be restated with the realized sequence of events in place of L. The difference between the two readings is not structural but concerns where the truthmakers for past-tense truths are located, and that is fixed by the background ontology of time. On an eternalist reading of the flash ontology, past flashes exist, located at earlier times, and they are the truthmakers for propositions about them; nothing in the present need do that work. A growing-block reading agrees that past flashes exist. CTGP denies both (C-a): past stages, and the events that occurred in them, do not exist. CTGP also accepts a truthmaker principle for past-tense truths: what is true about the past must be grounded in what exists, and on presentism only the present exists (CTGP – explained, §5.2.1). Not every presentist accepts such a principle; some reject unrestricted truthmaker requirements for past-tense truths altogether (Merricks 2007). The argument that follows is therefore conditional: given CTGP’s truthmaker principle, something present must make past-tense propositions true, and the options are limited. (i) Records. Past-tense truths might be grounded in present physical traces. Proposition 4.12 rules this out within the model: the present physical state is reachable along every event sequence from a suitable initial state, so no function of the present physical state, and hence no record contained in it, fixes the complete past. Grounding past truths in records would leave many of them without truthmakers, and it would make the past revisable whenever records degrade, contrary to No Revision (C-b) and to the distinction between truth and recoverability (C-i). (ii) Abstract histories. Past-tense truths might be grounded in an abstract representation of history, such as an ersatz time series of maximal propositions (Bourne 2006; Crisp 2007). Such a representation is formally the event history again. It still requires a fact that selects which abstract history is the actual one, and on presentism that fact must be a fact about the present. Supplying it is exactly what a Lucretian property does. (iii) Lucretian properties. The present world instantiates primitive properties of the form Had(σ′, φ) (Bigelow 1996). This is CTGP’s choice. It supplies present truthmakers for every Tier 1 and Tier 2 truth without positing existing past events, at the acknowledged cost that these properties do not supervene on the present’s categorical physical state (C-c; Sider 2001). The independent work done by the Lucretian profile is therefore ontological, not mathematical. Given presentism together with CTGP’s truthmaker principle, the profile is what makes the truths about realized events true: what the eternalist locates in existing past flashes, the presentist locates in the present world’s past-tensed properties. Proposition 5.21 is what one should expect: the profile carries the same structure as the history it grounds, located in the present rather than spread across existing times. Within the formalism the profile does two further jobs that any adequate ontology must assign to something: it settles identity of history across frames when the present state does not (Proposition 5.9), and it does so without acting dynamically (Theorem 5.20). The disagreement with eternalism should be stated precisely. Eternalism is not the claim that past, present, and future times exist simultaneously, which would identify distinct times with one time. In its standard sense, it holds that entities and events located at different times exist tenselessly, each at its own time, and their equal ontological status does not make those times simultaneous. CTGP’s disagreement with eternalism is therefore not that eternalism collapses different times into one. It is that CTGP denies that past and future stages exist alongside the present stage in the unrestricted sense that eternalism accepts. Past events are determinate but no longer exist as stages, future events do not yet exist, the past is grounded in present Lucretian properties, and the future becomes actual only through generation. This ontological question is distinct from the questions of objective passage, of A-theoretic versus B-theoretic accounts of tense, and of how objects persist through time. Whether CTGP’s presentist reading is preferable to an eternalist flash ontology is not settled by the model. The two readings are empirically equivalent, as CTGP itself emphasizes (CTGP – explained, §12.1). The choice rests on CTGP’s independent arguments: explanatory economy, the phenomenological constraint, and the generability commitment that presentism makes and eternalism does not (CTGP – explained, §§3, 4.5, and 12.2). What the present construction shows is that the presentist reading is consistent, precisely specified, and costs nothing empirically. ==================================================================================================== PART III. A Concrete Single-Outcome Model ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 6. Three Particles on a Ring: Kinematics ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 6.1 The Model Take G = ℤ_N, the integers modulo N, written additively, and three particles A, B, C, each located at one of the sites 0, 1, …, N−1 of a ring. Then H_kin = ℓ²(ℤ_N)^{⊗3}, with basis |a, b, c⟩, and the global translation is U_g |a, b, c⟩ = |a+g, b+g, c+g⟩. H_phys is the N²-dimensional subspace of translation-invariant states. With no external origin, only the separations b−a, c−a, and c−b are physical. 6.2 Frames and Frame Changes Frame A uses the coordinates (x, y) = (b−a, c−a), the positions of B and C relative to A. Frame B uses (u, v) = (a−b, c−b), and frame C uses (p, q) = (a−c, b−c). The reductions are (R_A Ψ)(x, y) = √N Ψ(0, x, y), (R_B Ψ)(u, v) = √N Ψ(u, 0, v), (R_C Ψ)(p, q) = √N Ψ(p, q, 0). By Proposition 3.3 they are unitary. The frame changes are permutations of basis states: V_{A→B} |x, y⟩ = |−x, y−x⟩, V_{B→C} |u, v⟩ = |u−v, −v⟩, V_{A→C} |x, y⟩ = |−y, x−y⟩. The groupoid law can be checked directly: V_{B→C} V_{A→B} |x, y⟩ = V_{B→C} |−x, y−x⟩ = |−x−(y−x), −(y−x)⟩ = |−y, x−y⟩ = V_{A→C} |x, y⟩. 6.3 A Worked Example of Frame-Relative Form Let the frame-A description be ψ^A = (|1, 0⟩ + |2, 0⟩) / √2. Relative to A, particle C is definitely at separation 0, and B is in an equal superposition of separations 1 and 2. The state is a product state of B and C relative to A, with zero entanglement. Applying V_{A→B} gives ψ^B = (|−1, −1⟩ + |−2, −2⟩) / √2, so relative to B, particles A and C are each in a superposition and are perfectly correlated, with one bit of entanglement. Consider the Tier 3 proposition “C is at separation 0 from A”, with invariant projector Q onto c−a = 0. In frame A its form is Q^A = projector onto y = 0, and Q^A ψ^A = ψ^A, so the proposition is determinately true. In frame B its form is Q^B = projector onto v−u = 0, a statement about the correlation of two frame-B coordinates rather than about a single coordinate, and Q^B ψ^B = ψ^B, so it is determinately true there as well. By contrast, “B is at separation 1 from A” has no determinate value in frame A, because x is superposed, and its frame-B form, “A is at separation −1 from B”, has no determinate value in frame B either. Determinacy and truth agree across frames, as Proposition 5.2 requires, even though one frame represents the situation as a product state and the other as an entangled state. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 7. Dynamics: Relational Flashes ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 7.1 Deterministic Part Let S_A, S_B, S_C shift the respective particle by one site, and let P_{ij}(r) be the projector onto configurations with x_j − x_i = r (mod N) for each ordered pair (i, j) ∈ {(A,B), (A,C), (B,C)}. Define H = −J Σ_{i∈{A,B,C}} (S_i + S_i†) + Σ_{(i,j)} Σ_{r} v(r) P_{ij}(r), v(r) = g cos(2πr/N), W = exp(−i τ H) (units with ħ = 1; τ is the duration of one stage). Each S_i commutes with the global translation, because translations on ℤ_N commute, and each P_{ij}(r) depends only on a separation. So H and W are invariant. 7.2 The Flash Instrument Let w be a normalized discrete Gaussian on ℤ_N of width s > 0: w(r) ∝ exp(−|r|_N² / (2 s²)), Σ_r w(r) = 1, where |r|_N is the distance from r to 0 around the ring. Let λ ∈ (0, 1) be the hit probability per stage. The transition events are 𝒦 = {∅} ∪ {(i, j, d) : (i, j) an ordered pair as above, d ∈ ℤ_N}, with Kraus operators F_∅ = √(1−λ) · 1, F_{(i,j,d)} = √(λ/3) · Σ_r √(w(r−d)) P_{ij}(r). Event (i, j, d) is a relational flash: an objective event centred on the separation d between particles i and j. Completeness holds because Σ_d w(r−d) = 1 for every r, so Σ_d F_{(i,j,d)}† F_{(i,j,d)} = (λ/3) · 1 for each pair, and the three pairs together with F_∅ sum to the identity. Every F_k is invariant, since it is a function of separations. Because w(r) > 0 for every r and λ < 1, every F_k is invertible, so Proposition 4.12 applies. The sharp limit s → 0 gives w(r) = δ_{r,0}, in which a flash projects exactly onto separation d and is no longer invertible. 7.3 Frame Forms In frame A the relational projectors take the forms P_{AB}(r) → [x = r], P_{AC}(r) → [y = r], P_{BC}(r) → [y − x = r]; in frame B they become [u = −r], [v − u = r], [v = r]. Appendix A lists all three frames. Hopping of the frame particle itself appears as a joint shift of all relative coordinates: in frame A, moving A one site to the right lowers both x and y by one. This is how “motion of the reference frame” appears inside the frame’s own description. By Lemma 3.7, each of these forms is the conjugate of the others by the appropriate V, so the frame-relative laws satisfy Definition 5.11. 7.4 Why the Flashes Must Be Relational Suppose one tried to use an absolute collapse operator, for example the projector P_abs onto “C is at site 0”. P_abs is not invariant. Applied to any Ψ ∈ H_phys, it produces a vector supported on configurations with c = 0, and its translation-averaged part has squared norm only 1/N of the total, because the N translates of that support are mutually orthogonal and only the untranslated term contributes. Hence ‖(1 − Π) P_abs Ψ‖ / ‖P_abs Ψ‖ = √(1 − 1/N) for every Ψ ∈ H_phys with P_abs Ψ ≠ 0, which is about 0.894 for N = 5. Most of the collapsed state lies outside the physical space, so an absolute collapse operator does not carry physical states to physical states. The point generalizes within the perspective-neutral construction. If an operator X maps H_phys into itself, then for every Ψ ∈ H_phys we have Ψ = Π Ψ and X Ψ = Π X Ψ, so X agrees on H_phys with Π X Π, which is invariant. Hence, if every realized event is to carry a physical state to a physical state, as Definition 4.1 requires and as single-outcome realism needs when each event is to be a gauge- invariant fact, then each Kraus operator must act on physical states exactly as an invariant, relational operator does. Two qualifications fix the scope of this conclusion. First, it holds within the chosen framework. Other formulations require only that an instrument be covariant as a whole, with the symmetry permuting its Kraus operators; standard GRW, whose collapse centres are absolute positions, is of this kind. In such formulations the averaged channel can preserve the physical sector even though individual updates do not, and an account is then owed of what gauge-invariant fact an outcome with an absolute label records in a closed universe. Second, the CSL equation cited in CTGP – explained, §10.1, uses smeared mass-density operators A(x) at absolute positions x. Reformulating CSL in terms of mass densities relative to material frames is a natural route for CTGP’s collapse option (Q2), but whether CSL must be so reformulated depends on the formulation and would require further argument. What the present construction establishes is sufficiency: invariant relational collapse operators provide an explicit, verified realization of QRF-compatible single-outcome dynamics. The equivalence proved above concerns the action of an operator on physical states; it does not show that every physically acceptable collapse theory must use the particular relational flash ontology of §7.2, which is one realization among others. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 8. The Criteria in the Model, With Numerical Checks ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 8.1 Generative Law and Single Outcome With the law of §7, one stage-to-stage transition samples exactly one event k ∈ 𝒦 with probability ‖F_k W Ψ_σ‖² and updates the state. The realized history is a single sequence of events. Unrealized sequences are not facts: they have kernel probabilities before the transition and no Had-properties afterward. This is single-outcome realism (C-d) at the level of the primitive ontology.
