Macroscopic outcomes would be patterns of many flashes, as in Bell’s flash ontology; in a three-particle model, each flash is already an event. 8.2 Weak Non-Supervenience in the Sharp Regime Proposition 8.1 (Two histories, one present). Take the sharp limit s → 0 and set J = 0, so that W = exp(−iτ Σ v(r) P_{ij}(r)) is diagonal in the configuration basis and commutes with every P_{ij}(r). Let N ≥ 4, and let Ψ_{σ_0} be any state with nonzero amplitude on the configuration b−a = 1, c−a = 3. The histories ω = ((A,B,1), (A,C,3)) and ω* = ((A,C,3), (A,B,1)) both have nonzero probability, and both produce the present state Ψ_{σ_2} ≐ R_A^{−1}|1, 3⟩. The resulting truth profiles differ: L contains Had(σ_0 → σ_1, (A,B,1)) under ω and Had(σ_0 → σ_1, (A,C,3)) under ω*. Proof. In frame-A coordinates the two relevant flash operators are multiples of the projectors P_{AB}(1) → |x = 1⟩⟨x = 1| and P_{AC}(3) → |y = 3⟩⟨y = 3|. These commute, and their product is the rank-one projector P_{AB}(1) P_{AC}(3) = P_{AC}(3) P_{AB}(1) = |1, 3⟩⟨1, 3|. With J = 0, W = exp(−iτ Σ v(r) P_{ij}(r)) is diagonal in the configuration basis, W |x, y⟩ = e^{iθ(x,y)} |x, y⟩, so it commutes with both projectors. Along ω the unnormalized final vector is therefore (λ/3) P_{AC}(3) W P_{AB}(1) W ψ_0 = (λ/3) P_{AC}(3) P_{AB}(1) W² ψ_0 = (λ/3) e^{2iθ(1,3)} ⟨1, 3|ψ_0⟩ |1, 3⟩. Along ω* the projectors are applied in the opposite order, which by the identity above gives the same vector. Since ⟨1, 3|ψ_0⟩ ≠ 0, both histories have nonzero probability, and both final states are the ray [|1, 3⟩], that is, [R_A^{−1}|1, 3⟩] in the neutral description. □ In this regime, the sharp flash (A,B,1) also makes the Tier 3 proposition “B is at separation 1 from A just after σ_1” determinately true, illustrating the special case noted after Proposition 4.4. 8.3 Generic Parameters For finite width, the situation divides as follows. (i) Strong non-supervenience holds for all parameters with s > 0 and λ < 1, by Proposition 4.12. The numerical check (Appendix B, item 11) takes a random present state and two different three- stage histories, reconstructs the unique initial state for each, and confirms that both histories end in the given present state with nonzero probability. (ii) Weak non-supervenience at generic parameters is open. Among all 4,096 three-stage histories from one random initial state at the generic parameter values of §8.4, no two distinct histories end in exactly the same present ray. This is evidence that fixed-initial-state coincidences are non-generic, not a proof. (iii) Near-coincidences occur. The largest overlap between the present states of two distinct histories in that sample is 0.985, so by Proposition 5.19(iv) no measurement on the present state distinguishes them with error probability below 0.414. Distinct histories can thus be observationally nearly indistinguishable even when their present states are not equal. 8.4 Numerical Verification The claims above were checked numerically with N = 5, λ = 0.6, s = 0.8, J = 0.7, g = 0.9, and τ = 0.5 (and, for the continuous-time model of §9, hit rate Λ = 1.2 and total time T = 5), using a random initial frame-A state; the checks of Theorem 13.4, Proposition 13.2, and Proposition 14.2 use the separate settings stated in Appendix B. The checks of §§13.8–13.11 and §14.5 are reported separately, in Table C.1 of Appendix C. The numerical checks confirm the analytical results in a specific instance and guard against sign and ordering errors; they are not independent proofs. Agreement “to machine precision” means deviations of order 10⁻¹⁶, the rounding error of double- precision arithmetic. The script, its output, the software versions, and the random seeds are in Appendix B. The “Status” column names the analytical result that each check instantiates. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 9. Continuous-Time Embedding and the Status of Generative Stages ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 9.1 The Question CTGP describes generation as a continuous causal-temporal progression (C-h). Part II uses discrete stages σ_0 < σ_1 < … with one stochastic transition per stage. A discrete-stage model can represent selected stages of a continuous process, but it does not by itself establish compatibility with a continuous generative ontology. This section gives a continuous-time version of the model, proves that it has the properties of Part II, and states precisely what remains a postulate. 9.2 A Relational Jump Process Definition 9.1 (Continuous-time relational flash process). Let H be the invariant Hamiltonian of §7.1, let Λ > 0 be a hit rate, and define jump operators J_{(i,j,d)} = √(Λ/3) · Σ_r √(w(r−d)) P_{ij}(r), so that Σ_k J_k† J_k = Λ · 1. The process runs as follows. Hit times t_1 < t_2 < … form a Poisson process of rate Λ. Between hits the state evolves by exp(−iH(t − t_j)). At a hit time, event k is drawn with probability ‖J_k ψ‖² / Λ, and the state jumps to J_k ψ / ‖J_k ψ‖. This is the standard quantum-jump unravelling of a Lindblad equation. Because Σ_k J_k† J_k is proportional to the identity, the effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian H_eff = H − (i/2) Σ_k J_k† J_k = H − (iΛ/2) · 1 differs from H by a constant, so the evolution between hits is unitary up to a uniform decay of norm, and the hit times are independent of the state. This is precisely the structure of the original GRW model, here with relational jump operators. Proposition 9.2 (Well-definedness, invariance, and covariance). (i) The process of Definition 9.1 is a well-defined piecewise-deterministic Markov process on the unit sphere of H_phys, with almost surely finitely many hits in any bounded time interval. (ii) Every trajectory remains in H_phys. (iii) Its ensemble average ρ(t) obeys the Lindblad equation dρ/dt = −i [H, ρ] + Σ_k J_k ρ J_k† − Λ ρ, which preserves the set of density operators supported on H_phys. (iv) The frame-A and frame-B processes, built from the frame forms H^A, J^A_k and H^B, J^B_k, assign the same probabilities to every finite sequence of (hit time, event) pairs, and along every trajectory their states satisfy ψ^B(t) ≐ V_{A→B} ψ^A(t) for all t. Proof. (i) In finite dimension, with bounded H and state-independent Poisson hit times, the construction is explicit: a Poisson process of finite rate has almost surely finitely many points in any bounded interval, and between them the evolution is a fixed unitary flow. (ii) H and every J_k are invariant, so exp(−iHt) and the normalized jumps map H_phys to itself. (iii) The Lindblad form follows from averaging the jump process, which is standard; the term −Λρ is −(1/2){Σ_k J_k† J_k, ρ} with Σ_k J_k† J_k = Λ · 1. Invariance of H and J_k implies that the generator commutes with conjugation by U_h, so density operators supported on H_phys remain so. (iv) By Lemma 3.7, ‖J^A_k exp(−iH^A t) R_A Ψ‖ = ‖J_k exp(−iHt) Ψ‖ and likewise for frame B; hit times do not depend on the state; so the joint law of (hit time, event) sequences is the same in every frame, and induction over hits gives the state relation, as in Theorem 5.12. □ 9.3 Continuous-Time Histories as Marked Point Processes In continuous time a history consists of event labels together with their times, and the truth profile contains a continuum of state propositions. Both must be defined explicitly. Definition 9.3 (Records and their law). Fix Ψ_0 ∈ H_phys and t > 0. A record on [0, t] is a finite sequence ω = ((t_1, k_1), …, (t_m, k_m)) with m ≥ 0, 0 < t_1 < ⋯ < t_m ≤ t, and k_j ∈ 𝒦. The record space Ω_t is the disjoint union over m of the sets of such sequences, equipped with the reference measure given by Lebesgue measure on ordered m-tuples of times and counting measure on labels. Along ω, the state at time s ∈ [0, t] is the normalized vector Ψ_s(ω) ∝ e^{−iH(s − t_j)} J_{k_j} e^{−iH(t_j − t_{j−1})} ⋯ J_{k_1} e^{−iH t_1} Ψ_0 for t_j ≤ s < t_{j+1}, with t_0 = 0 and t_{m+1} = ∞, so that the state is right-continuous at each jump. Proposition 9.4 (Density of the record). The law of the record on [0, t] has density, with respect to the reference measure, p_t(ω) = e^{−Λt} ‖J_{k_m} e^{−iH(t_m − t_{m−1})} ⋯ J_{k_1} e^{−iH t_1} Ψ_0‖². The admissible records are the positive-density records, 𝒜_t = { ω ∈ Ω_t : p_t(ω) > 0 }. Proof. The hit times of a Poisson process of rate Λ have density Λ^m e^{−Λt} on ordered m-tuples in (0, t]. Given the hit times, the labels are drawn successively with probabilities ‖J_{k_j} φ_j‖² / Λ, where φ_j is the normalized state just before t_j. The product of these probabilities times Λ^m is Π_j ‖J_{k_j} φ_j‖². Because the evolution between hits is unitary, this product telescopes into the squared norm of the unnormalized vector shown. □ Definition 9.5 (Continuous-time truth profile). The past-tensed language in continuous time consists of state propositions Had(s, [Ψ]), read “at time s the state was [Ψ]”, and event propositions Had(s, k), read “at time s event k occurred”. For ω ∈ 𝒜_t the truth profile at time t is L_t(ω) = { Had(s, [Ψ_s(ω)]) : 0 ≤ s < t } ∪ { Had(t_j, k_j) : 1 ≤ j ≤ m }. An event at time t_m = t is counted as having occurred: it belongs to the generation of the present state, which is right-continuous. Remark 9.6 (Pointwise and almost-sure statements). Two kinds of statement about records must be kept apart. Pointwise statements hold for every record in a specified set: Definitions 9.3 and 9.5 assign a state path and a truth profile to every record, Theorem 9.7 characterizes the profiles of all admissible records, and Propositions 9.8 and 9.9 and Theorem 9.10 hold for every admissible record. Probabilistic statements hold almost surely, that is, outside a set of records of probability zero. Because p_t is a density, an individual record has probability zero. Admissibility means positive density, p_t(ω) > 0; it does not mean that ω has positive probability, and positivity of a density is not in general the same as membership in the topological support of the law. Throughout, “admissible” is used only in the positive-density sense. In the finite-width model every Kraus operator is invertible, so every record has positive density. In particular, records with an event exactly at the terminal time t form a set of probability zero: for a fixed t, the Poisson process almost surely has no point at t. Such records are admitted so that the profile at time t is defined for every record, with right-continuous states, and so that the profiles of a single trajectory are nested at all times; no probabilistic statement depends on them. Since every result in this section is proved pointwise for all admissible records, it holds in particular almost surely. The conditional law in Proposition 9.9 is likewise given by an explicit density defined for every record, which is a version of the conditional distribution; any other version agrees with it almost surely. Theorem 9.7 (Admissible continuous-time profiles). A set L of propositions belongs to { L_t(ω) : ω ∈ 𝒜_t } if and only if: (o) every element of L is a state proposition Had(s, [Ψ]) with s ≥ 0 or an event proposition Had(s, k) with s > 0; (a) the event propositions are finitely many, with distinct times in (0, t]; (b) for each s ∈ [0, t) there is exactly one state proposition with time s, and none with time s ≥ t; (c) the state proposition at time 0 is Had(0, [Ψ_0]); and (d) if ω is the record read off from the event propositions, then p_t(ω) > 0 and, for every s ∈ [0, t), the state proposition at time s is Had(s, [Ψ_s(ω)]). The record is then unique, and profiles are nested: if t ≤ t′, ω′ ∈ 𝒜_{t′}, and ω is the restriction of ω′ to [0, t] (its events with times at most t), then ω ∈ 𝒜_t and L_t(ω) ⊆ L_{t′}(ω′). Proof. As for Theorem 4.8. Conditions (o) and (a) determine a unique record ω; (d) states that ω is admissible and that every state proposition is the one the law assigns along ω; (b) and (o) exclude anything else. For nesting, the factorization in the proof of Proposition 9.9 gives p_{t′}(ω′) = p_t(ω) · c with c ≥ 0, so p_{t′}(ω′) > 0 implies p_t(ω) > 0 and hence ω ∈ 𝒜_t. Restriction preserves the event propositions with times up to t and the states at all times before t, so every element of L_t(ω) belongs to L_{t′}(ω′); for t < t′ the later profile also contains the state proposition at time t, which is why the inclusion can be strict. □ Proposition 9.8 (Reconstruction from an earlier time). For 0 ≤ s < t and ω ∈ 𝒜_t, the present state S_t = [Ψ_t(ω)] is fixed by the state proposition Had(s, [Ψ_s(ω)]), the event propositions with times in (s, t], and the law: it is obtained by applying to Ψ_s(ω) the unitary evolution between those events and the corresponding jump operators. Proof. Definition 9.3, applied from time s rather than time 0. □ Proposition 9.8 avoids any appeal to a “latest” earlier time, which does not exist in continuous time: any earlier time s, together with the events since s, suffices. Proposition 9.9 (Dynamical idleness in continuous time). For t < t′, the conditional law of the record on (t, t′], given the complete state (S_t, L_t), depends only on S_t. Its density is e^{−Λ(t′ − t)} ‖J_{k_r} e^{−iH(t_r − t_{r−1})} ⋯ J_{k_{m+1}} e^{−iH(t_{m+1} − t)} Ψ_t‖², where (t_{m+1}, k_{m+1}), …, (t_r, k_r) are the events in (t, t′]. Proof. Write the unnormalized vector of Proposition 9.4 on [0, t′] as A B Ψ_0, where B carries Ψ_0 to the unnormalized state at time t and A carries the state from t to t′. Then ‖A B Ψ_0‖² = ‖A Ψ_t‖² ‖B Ψ_0‖² with Ψ_t = B Ψ_0 / ‖B Ψ_0‖, and e^{−Λt′} = e^{−Λt} e^{−Λ(t′ − t)}. The density on [0, t′] therefore factorizes into the density on [0, t] and the stated factor, which depends on the past only through Ψ_t. □ Theorem 9.10 (Continuous-time covariance, correspondence, and truth preservation). For a frame A, let the frame-A description at time t be D^A_t = (t, [R_A Ψ_t], τ_A(L_t)), where τ_A maps Had(s, [Ψ]) to Had(s, [R_A Ψ]) and Had(s, k) to Had(s, k, E^A_k). Then: (a) For every record ω, the densities computed in frame A, in frame B, and in the neutral description coincide, so the sets 𝒜_t of admissible records coincide in all frames. (b) Along every ω ∈ 𝒜_t, the frame states satisfy [R_B Ψ_s(ω)] = [V_{A→B} R_A Ψ_s(ω)] for all s ∈ [0, t]. (c) Criterion 5.5, with stage replaced by time, Theorem 5.6, and Proposition 5.8 hold for continuous-time descriptions, and if the descriptions of two frames correspond at time 0, the descriptions generated from them correspond at every later time along every admissible record. (d) Truth preservation (Theorem 5.16) and no revision across frames (Proposition 5.17) hold at every time. Proof. (a) By Lemma 3.7, R_A J_k = J^A_k R_A and R_A e^{−iHs} = e^{−iH^A s} R_A on H_phys. Hence the frame-A unnormalized vector of Proposition 9.4, applied to R_A Ψ_0, equals R_A applied to the neutral one, and its norm is the same because R_A is unitary; likewise for frame B. (b) The same identities show that the frame-A state at time s is proportional to R_A Ψ_s(ω), and R_B = V_{A→B} R_A on H_phys. (c) The proofs of Theorem 5.6 and Proposition 5.8 use only the unitarity of the reductions and the bijectivity of the translations, both of which hold here; preservation of correspondence follows from (a) and (b), because both frames add, at each time, the translations of the same neutral propositions. (d) The proofs of Theorem 5.16 and Proposition 5.17 carry over verbatim, using (c) and the nesting in Theorem 9.7. □ The numerical check (Table 1) simulated 200 trajectories over total time T = 5 in frames A and B with common random numbers: the 1,158 events, and their times, were identical in the two frames, and the final states were related by V_{A→B} to machine precision. The resulting picture matches CTGP’s description of generation: continuous evolution of the present state, punctuated by discrete primitive events, with each moment’s state and each event passing into the past-tensed profile. 9.4 What Is Postulated: The Identification of t With Cosmic Time The continuous parameter t in Definition 9.1 is the time variable of a non-relativistic dynamics. Identifying it with CTGP’s generation parameter σ, which in the emergent geometric regime is the cosmological time function (CTGP – explained, §6), is a postulate of the application, not a result. Nothing in the model derives cosmic time. What the model shows is conditional: if the generation parameter is a global time with respect to which the present state evolves by a law of this form, then the continuous-time model has every property established in Part II and §9.3. Deriving the identification would require at least a relativistic version of the dynamics and an account of how its time parameter is fixed by the cosmological matter distribution; these are the subjects of §13 and §14, and they remain open (§17.3, O2 and O3). The regularity of cosmological time that the identification requires is stated precisely in §13.1: the level sets are future Cauchy surfaces in general, and Cauchy surfaces under the condition of Proposition 13.1. 9.5 The Discrete Stages as a Coarse-Grained Description The discrete-stage model of Part II is a coarse-grained description of the continuous one. With stage duration τ and λ = 1 − e^{−Λτ}, the probability of at least one hit in a stage is the same in both models. The discrete model differs in two respects: it allows at most one event per stage, and it places each event at the end of its stage. The probability of two or more hits in one stage is of order (Λτ)², so the discrete model approximates the continuous one when Λτ is small. The discrete stages are therefore not a claim that generation proceeds in jumps; they are a sampling of a continuous process, used in Part II because finite induction makes every proof exact. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 10. CTGP’s Further Requirements (Task T5) ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Cross-frame consistency is not the whole of CTGP’s demands. A QRF-compatible single-outcome model must also satisfy definite generative ordering and generability. This section checks these and related requirements. 10.1 Definite Generative Ordering Proposition 10.1 (Frame changes act within stages and commute with generation). For every admissible law, every pair of frames, and every event k, the frame-A and frame-B single-step maps G^A_k(ψ) = F^A_k W^A ψ / ‖F^A_k W^A ψ‖ and G^B_k satisfy V_{A→B} ∘ G^A_k ≐ G^B_k ∘ V_{A→B}. The stage index is unchanged by every frame change. The same holds in continuous time, with hit times unchanged. Proof. Lemma 3.7, Definition 3.4, and Proposition 9.2(iv). Frame changes are maps H_A → H_B at a fixed stage or time, and the stage or time index is a parameter of the law, not a quantum degree of freedom. □ The linear order of stages, and the order of events within a history, are therefore the same in every frame: event k_j precedes k_{j+1} in every description, and no frame change in this class superposes the order of events. This satisfies (C-e) for spatial QRFs. The status of this result should be stated plainly. The ordering is preserved because the stage or time index is a parameter on which frame changes do not act. That is an assumption of the class of models, not a derivation of definite order from more basic principles. Whether a definite order can be maintained when the reference frame is itself a quantum clock is the principal open problem (§14; §17.3, O3). 10.2 Generability The kernel of Definition 4.2, and the jump process of Definition 9.1, give the distribution of the next state and event from the present state alone. They use no data from later stages, no final- state condition, and no global self-consistency condition, so the model is generable in exactly the sense of (C-f). The state space on which the law acts is H_phys, the physically instantiated present state. The truth profile is not added to it (Proposition 4.9), so generability’s restriction on enlarging the state space is respected. By Corollary 5.14, the generability property holds in every frame. The model is stochastic, which CTGP – explained, §12.2, explicitly allows. 10.3 Open Future and Asymmetry At stage σ, L_σ contains no proposition about a later stage (Theorem 4.8(e)), and no proposition about a later stage is determinate (Definition 5.1). Before a transition there are only kernel probabilities over possible events; after it there is one realized event and its Had-property. This matches clause (d) of CTGP’s Definition 17.20 and the claim that the future is ungenerated rather than merely unknown. 10.4 No Relative Facts Every determinate proposition has the same truth value in every frame (Proposition 5.2 and Theorem 5.16), so the model contains no frame-relative facts. It contains frame-relative descriptions and relational quantities, which CTGP does not reject (§2.3). ==================================================================================================== PART IV. Evaluation ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 11. What CTGP Adds Beyond QRF Covariance ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- A skeptic can grant every theorem above and still ask whether CTGP contributes any substantive constraint beyond the existence of a QRF-covariant, single-outcome stochastic model with an attached historical truth profile. It does. The test of whether a commitment adds content is whether it excludes some model that satisfies everything else. Each of the following commitments passes that test. CTGP commitment | Formal content in this framework | Excludes -------------------------+-----------------------------------------+------------------------------- Generability (C-f) | The law is a Markov kernel on present | Laws depending on past facts | physical states; Cor. 4.10, Cor. 5.14 | not carried by the present | | state (Example 11.3) Single-outcome (C-d) | Exactly one event per transition; | Everettian readings on which | Theorem 4.8(a) | all branches are realized Frame-invariant | Determinacy and truth invariant under | Relational interpretations with determinacy (C-d) | all frame changes; Prop. 5.2, Thm 5.16 | frame-relative facts No revision (C-b) | Admissible profiles are nested; | Readings on which later events | Theorem 4.8; Prop. 5.17 | fix earlier facts Definite ordering (C-e) | Events totally ordered, order invariant | Fundamental indefinite causal | under frame changes; Prop. 10.1 | order at the level of stages Lucretian accuracy (C-b) | Profiles are admissible and accurate; | Truth profiles inconsistent | Theorem 4.8, Prop. 4.11 | with the law Table 2. Constraints CTGP imposes and what each excludes, even among QRF-covariant models. Generability needs the most care, because a law that looks future-dependent may have an equivalent forward formulation. CTGP – explained, §12.2, builds this into its criterion: future dependence counts against generability only if no empirically equivalent generable formulation exists. Three cases must therefore be distinguished: conditioning on the future as a mathematical operation, a final-boundary requirement imposed as a fundamental input, and genuine non-generability relative to the admissible present physical states. The first two are treated by Proposition 11.1 and Example 11.2, the third by Example 11.3. Proposition 11.1 (Future conditioning has a forward representation). Let E be an event label and n ≥ 1 a stage with Pr(k_n = E | Ψ_{σ_0}) > 0. For m < n define h_m(Ψ) as the probability that k_n = E given state Ψ at stage σ_m, computed from the kernel K. The measure over histories conditioned on k_n = E is generated forward by the stage-dependent kernel K^h_m(k | Ψ) = K(k | Ψ) · h_{m+1}(Ψ_k) / h_m(Ψ) for m < n−1, K^h_{n−1}(k | Ψ) = K(k | Ψ) · 1[k = E] / h_{n−1}(Ψ), where Ψ_k = F_k W Ψ / ‖F_k W Ψ‖ and 1[k = E] is 1 if k = E and 0 otherwise. This kernel is Markov in the present state at each stage, and it is frame-covariant. Proof. This is the Doob h-transform. The product of the kernels K^h along a history telescopes to Π_m K(k_{m+1} | Ψ_m) · 1[k_n = E] / h_0(Ψ_{σ_0}), which is the conditioned probability of the history; the normalization of each K^h_m follows from h_m(Ψ) = Σ_k K(k | Ψ) h_{m+1}(Ψ_k). Frame covariance holds because K and the h_m are computed from frame-independent probabilities (Theorem 5.12). □ The numerical check (Appendix B, item 13) confirms that the forward kernel reproduces the conditioned measure over all three-stage histories. Example 11.2 (What future conditioning does and does not violate). Take the model of Part III and the event E = (B, C, 0) at stage σ_n. (i) As a mathematical operation, conditioning on E computes probabilities given a hypothetical later event. It imposes nothing on the world and is not excluded. (ii) As a fundamental final-boundary requirement, the theory would consist of the ring dynamics together with the stipulation that E occurs at σ_n, imposed as an independent input. The forward kernel K^h of Proposition 11.1 exists, but its definition refers to E and n, which are data about a later stage. A law of this kind is precisely a final condition imposed as an independent input to fix earlier states, which CTGP – explained, §12.2, identifies as incompatible with presentism. (iii) If K^h_m is instead posited directly as a stage-dependent law, with no reference to E, it is generable, and CTGP does not exclude it. Since (ii) and (iii) are empirically equivalent, CTGP’s own criterion does not count finite-stage future conditioning in this model as a genuine failure of generability: an equivalent forward formulation exists. What CTGP rejects in case (ii) is the claim that the boundary condition is a fundamental input, not the probability measure that results. Conditioning is therefore not the right test case. A genuine failure must be one for which no forward formulation on the admissible present physical states exists. Example 11.3 (A QRF-covariant, history-dependent law). Fix the physical state space H_phys of Part III and a width s ≥ 0, where s = 0 denotes the sharp limit. Besides the flash instrument {F_k} of §7.2, which chooses each pair with weight 1/3, let {F″_k} be the instrument with the same flashes but pair weights q_{AB} = 1/2, q_{AC} = q_{BC} = 1/4: F″_∅ = √(1−λ) · 1, F″_{(i,j,d)} = √(λ q_{ij}) · Σ_r √(w(r−d)) P_{ij}(r). Completeness holds because the weights sum to 1. Define a law that applies {F_k} at the first transition and, at every later transition, applies {F_k} if the first event was a flash on the pair (A, B), and {F″_k} otherwise. Both instruments are invariant and the rule consults only an invariant event label, so the law is QRF-covariant; it is single-outcome; and each history carries a well- defined truth profile. The law is not generable on H_phys if two histories of the same length reach the same present ray while calling for different next-event distributions. Two versions of this claim should be distinguished: one across possible initial conditions, and one for a single fixed initial condition. Both hold, and the next proposition proves each by explicit construction. Proposition 11.4 (The history-dependent law is not generable on H_phys). No kernel on present states in H_phys, even one allowed to depend on the stage index, reproduces the law of Example 11.3. Specifically: (a) Across initial conditions (s > 0). For every present ray of the form [Φ] with W Φ = R_A^{−1}|x, y⟩, there are two histories of length 2, with different initial rays, that both reach [Φ] with nonzero probability and whose next-event distributions differ in total variation by exactly λ/6. (b) With one fixed initial condition (s = 0, J = 0). For every initial state with nonzero amplitude on the configuration b−a = 1, c−a = 3 (N ≥ 4), the two histories of Proposition 8.1 both have nonzero probability under the law of Example 11.3, both reach the same present ray, and their next-event distributions again differ in total variation by exactly λ/6. Proof. A kernel on present states, even a stage-dependent one, assigns the same next-event distribution to any two histories of equal length that reach the same present ray. It therefore suffices to exhibit such pairs with different next-event distributions. (a) Take the histories ω_1 = ((A,B,d), k) and ω_2 = (∅, k) with any d and any flash k. Under the law, ω_1 applies F_k at its second transition (its first event is on the pair (A,B)), and ω_2 applies F″_k (its first event is ∅). The branch products M_1 = F_k W F_{(A,B,d)} W and M_2 = F″_k W F_∅ W are invertible, because for s > 0 every Kraus operator of either instrument is invertible (w(r) > 0 for all r and λ < 1). Define the initial rays [M_1^{−1} Φ] and [M_2^{−1} Φ]. Running each history from its initial ray returns a vector proportional to Φ, and each transition has nonzero probability because invertible operators annihilate no nonzero vector. At the present stage, ω_1 calls for {F_k} and ω_2 for {F″_k}. Since W Φ is the configuration state with separations x, y, and y−x for the pairs (A,B), (A,C), (B,C), the probability of the flash (i, j, d′) is λ · (1/3) · w(r_{ij} − d′) under {F_k} and λ · q_{ij} · w(r_{ij} − d′) under {F″_k}, where r_{ij} is the corresponding separation; the probability of ∅ is 1 − λ in both. Summing |λ(q_{ij} − 1/3)| over the three pairs and over d′ (using Σ_{d′} w = 1) gives λ(1/6 + 1/12 + 1/12) = λ/3, so the total- variation distance is λ/6. (b) Take ω = ((A,B,1), (A,C,3)) and ω* = ((A,C,3), (A,B,1)), as in Proposition 8.1. With s = 0 the Kraus operators of both instruments are multiples of configuration projectors: F_{(i,j,d)} = √(λ/3) P_{ij}(d) and F″_{(i,j,d)} = √(λ q_{ij}) P_{ij}(d). With J = 0, W is diagonal in the configuration basis. Under the law, ω applies {F_k} at its second transition (its first event is on (A,B)), and ω* applies {F″_k} (its first event is on (A,C)). In both cases the second operator is a nonzero multiple of a configuration projector (√(λ/3) P_{AC}(3) along ω and √(λ/2) P_{AB}(1) along ω*), and the first is √(λ/3) times the other projector. Since W is diagonal and commutes with both projectors, and P_{AB}(1) P_{AC}(3) = P_{AC}(3) P_{AB}(1) = |1, 3⟩⟨1, 3| in frame-A coordinates, the unnormalized final vectors are (λ/3) e^{2iθ(1,3)} ⟨1, 3|ψ_0⟩ |1, 3⟩ and (λ/√6) e^{2iθ(1,3)} ⟨1, 3|ψ_0⟩ |1, 3⟩, which differ only by a positive factor. Hence, as in Proposition 8.1: both histories have nonzero probability, because the initial state has nonzero amplitude on the configuration (x, y) = (1, 3), and both reach the present ray [R_A^{−1}|1, 3⟩]. At that ray, W Φ is the configuration state (1, 3), and the computation in (a) gives next-event distributions differing in total variation by λ/6. □ The numerical check (Appendix B, item 12) carries out both constructions explicitly at the parameters of §8.4, with present configuration (x, y) = (1, 3): in each case the two histories end at the same present ray with nonzero probability, and the next-event distributions differ in total variation by 0.1000 = λ/6. Example 11.3 is a genuine failure of generability relative to the model’s physical state space. The law depends on a past fact that the present state does not carry, so it is not a Markov kernel on admissible present physical states, whether the comparison is made across initial conditions or with the initial condition held fixed. It could be made generable only by adding a record variable, such as a bit registering whether the first event was on the pair (A, B), to the present physical state. CTGP permits such an enlargement only if the variable is physically instantiated on the present stage and has dynamics of its own (C-f). The result is then a different theory with an enlarged physical ontology, not a forward formulation of the original one. In this sense generability does independent work: it excludes the history-dependent law on H_phys while QRF covariance does not. The exclusion is an ontological restriction imposed by CTGP, not a mathematical impossibility. Any history-dependent process becomes Markov on a suitably enlarged state space, for instance by adjoining a memory bit. What CTGP adds is the requirement that the enlarged state be physically instantiated in the present, rather than serve as a bookkeeping variable that records the past. Two further consequences arise from combining CTGP’s commitments with the perspective-neutral construction. First, requiring every realized event to carry physical states to physical states makes each collapse operator act on physical states as an invariant, relational operator does (§7.4). This constrains how a collapse realization of CTGP acts on physical states within the construction; it does not single out the particular flash ontology of §7.2. Second, for invertible single-outcome laws the complete past is not a function of the present state (Proposition 4.12), which gives CTGP’s acknowledged cost (C-c) an exact form. The answer to the skeptic, then, is that CTGP picks out a proper subclass of QRF-covariant single- outcome models: those whose laws are Markov kernels on the present physical state, whose determinacy is frame-invariant, whose truth profiles are admissible and nested, whose events are definitely ordered, and, within the perspective-neutral construction, whose collapse operators act on physical states as relational operators do. The model of Part III shows that this subclass is not empty. ==================================================================================================== PART V. A Compatibility Program: Quantum Fields, Quantum Clocks, and Quantum Gravity ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 12. The CTGP Admissibility Criterion ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Parts II–IV establish compatibility for a finite, non-relativistic model with spatial quantum reference frames. Part V asks how the same commitments can be carried toward relativistic quantum field theory (RQFT), temporal quantum reference frames, and quantum gravity. It does not redesign CTGP. It states the commitments to be preserved, turns them into a single admissibility criterion, proves the results that can be proved now in finite form, and identifies precisely what would have to be established, and what would count as an obstruction, in each of the three directions. Results in this Part are labeled by status: theorems and propositions are proved; definitions marked as proposals are offered for adoption rather than derived; conjectures and target results are open. 12.1 The Central Principle and the Commitments to Preserve The organizing principle of the program is this: CTGP requires one objectively generated physical history. It does not require every observer, clock, quantum representation, or mathematical formulation to describe that history in the same way. The principle is already at work in Parts II–IV: frame descriptions differ, sometimes as product versus entangled states (§6.3), while determinacy, truth, and the history measure are frame- independent (Proposition 5.2, Theorems 5.12 and 5.16). It leaves room for relativistic covariance, for quantum clocks, and potentially for quantum geometry, without abandoning the central ontology. It does not guarantee success: some quantum-gravity proposals could be fundamentally incompatible with CTGP, and no rigorous relativistic single-outcome model with CTGP’s structure yet exists. The commitments to be preserved are those of §2.3, grouped as follows. (P1) Presentism (C-a): only the currently generated physical configuration exists. (P2) A single realized history (C-d): there is one factual sequence of physical outcomes. (P3) Generability (C-f): the complete present physical state and the laws fix the probability distribution of successors, without indispensable independent future boundary inputs. (P4) Causal inheritance (C-g): whatever part of history can still affect future physics is instantiated in present physical variables. (P5) Relational descriptive freedom (C-j): different valid reference frames may describe the same physical history differently. (P6) Emergent classical cosmic time (C-k): in the general-relativistic regime where the required structure exists, the objective present corresponds to the level sets of a regular cosmological time function. Commitment (P6) is conditional on the classical spacetime admitting the required structure, and it should not be imposed unchanged on a quantum regime in which classical geometry is undefined (§15). CTGP’s negative result on density-weighted generation (C-l) constrains every realization: the extension must not make different regions become real at rates set by local matter density. Local matter, fields, clocks, and stochastic events may evolve differently while belonging to one underlying generative ordering. 12.2 The Criterion Write the ontological state at a generative stage σ as Ω_σ = (S_σ, L_σ), with S_σ the complete physically instantiated state and L_σ its primitive past-tensed truth profile. A physical extension must supply a forward transition law K_{σ,σ′}(S_σ, dS′ de) for σ′ > σ, where e denotes the realized event data of the transition. Definition 12.1 (CTGP-admissible theory; a proposal). A physical theory is CTGP-admissible on a specified domain if it has a formulation with the following features. (A1) Present state. A complete, physically instantiated present state S_σ on each stage of a generative ordering. (A2) Forward law. Transition kernels K_{σ,σ′} that are normalized and satisfy the composition relation K_{σ,σ″} = K_{σ′,σ″} ∘ K_{σ,σ′} for σ < σ′ < σ″, with integration over intermediate states and concatenation of event data. (A3) One realized history. A single realized sample path of events, with the truth profile updated from it, L_{σ′} = Update(L_σ, S_σ, e, S_{σ′}). (A4) No indispensable future input. No independent future boundary condition is required, in the sense of CTGP – explained, §12.2: future dependence disqualifies only if no empirically equivalent forward formulation exists (compare §11). (A5) Causal idleness of the past. Pr(S_{σ′}, e | S_σ, L_σ) = Pr(S_{σ′}, e | S_σ). (A6) Frame and gauge consistency. Admissible reference-frame and gauge transformations preserve the probabilities and the identities of realized physical events. (A7) Classical realization. Wherever the domain includes the classical general-relativistic regime in which CTGP claims (P6), the generative ordering is realized by the level sets of regular cosmological time. Primitive past-tensed truths may be included in the ontology, but by (A5) they supply no causal input absent from the complete physical state. Definition 12.1 is a proposal, not a theorem about any interacting relativistic or quantum- gravitational theory. Its value is that it turns the broad question whether CTGP can be compatible with quantum physics into a list of precise mathematical obligations. Proposition 12.2 (The finite model is CTGP-admissible on its domain). The discrete-stage and continuous-time models of Part III satisfy (A1)–(A6), with (A6) restricted to the spatial QRFs of §3; (A7) is vacuous on their domain, which contains no classical spacetime. Proof. (A1): the present state is the ray [Ψ_σ] in H_phys. (A2): the kernels are Markov; in discrete time, composition is the product of one-step kernels; in continuous time it follows from the factorization of record densities (Proposition 9.9). (A3): Definition 4.6 and Theorem 4.8; Definition 9.5 and Theorem 9.7. (A4): §10.2, with §11 for the sense of future dependence. (A5): Corollary 4.10 and Proposition 9.9. (A6): Theorems 5.12, 5.16, and 9.10. □ 12.3 Three Levels of Compatibility “Compatibility” is used in three distinct senses, and keeping them apart is essential to every claim in this Part. Level | What has been established -------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Conceptual | No identified logical contradiction under specified assumptions Mathematical | A well-defined model satisfies CTGP’s stated compatibility conditions Physical | The model reproduces the required observations and survives empirical tests Table 3. Three levels of compatibility. The finite model establishes mathematical compatibility on its own domain (Proposition 12.2). It does not establish physical compatibility, and a mathematically consistent model can be physically inadequate. The results below for the relativistic, temporal, and gravitational directions are of three kinds: finite-dimensional theorems that are analogues of the required results, conditional propositions, and open targets. 12.4 Ontology Versus Implementation CTGP’s foundational ontology is what (P1)–(P6) assert. A relativistic flash model, a Bohmian field theory, or a quantum-gravitational causal structure might each satisfy Definition 12.1 by quite different physical mechanisms. They are therefore candidate realizations to be investigated, not components of CTGP itself. The same holds for the ring model of Part III: it is one realization, chosen because every claim about it can be proved. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 13. Relativistic Quantum Fields: Realized-History Consistency ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 13.1 Background Structure The first construction should not quantize gravity. It should assume a fixed classical spacetime (M, g) with a regular cosmological time function T(p) = sup { L(γ) : γ a past-directed causal curve from p }, and take the proposed CTGP presents to be the level sets Σ_t = T^{−1}(t). The relevant regularity results are those of Andersson, Galloway and Howard (1998), and they must be stated with care. If T is regular, meaning finite everywhere and tending to zero along every past- inextendible causal curve, then (M, g) is globally hyperbolic; T is a time function, continuous and strictly increasing along future-directed causal curves; every point p lies at the future end of a maximal timelike geodesic of length T(p) issuing from the initial singularity; and T is locally Lipschitz, with first and second derivatives existing almost everywhere. The nonempty level sets are closed, acausal, edgeless, and future Cauchy surfaces: their future Cauchy horizons are empty (Andersson, Galloway & Howard 1998, Corollary 2.6). They need not be Cauchy surfaces in the full, two-sided sense, and the paper says so explicitly. The following proposition states exactly when they are. Proposition 13.1 (When a level set is a Cauchy surface). Let T be regular and let S_a = T^{−1}(a) be nonempty. Then S_a is a Cauchy surface if and only if every future-inextendible causal curve contains points at which T > a. Proof. (⇐) Let γ be an inextendible causal curve. By regularity, T tends to zero toward the past end of γ, and by hypothesis T exceeds a somewhere on γ. Since T is continuous and strictly increasing along γ, γ meets S_a exactly once. Every inextendible causal curve therefore meets S_a exactly once, so S_a is a Cauchy surface. (⇒) Suppose some future-inextendible causal curve γ has T ≤ a at all of its points, and extend it to the past to an inextendible causal curve. Because T is strictly increasing, T < a along the whole extended curve (were T = a at some point, T would exceed a beyond it). The curve never meets S_a, so S_a is not a Cauchy surface. □ In FLRW models with a big-bang singularity, whether they expand forever or recollapse, cosmological time is the FLRW time and every level set is a Cauchy surface. But a causal curve that ends at a singularity inside a black hole typically has bounded T, and level sets beyond that bound are not met by it. In universes containing black holes, later level sets can therefore be future Cauchy surfaces without being Cauchy surfaces. Any CTGP claim that the present is a Cauchy surface should accordingly be restricted to the level sets satisfying Proposition 13.1, with the future Cauchy property as the general statement. For quantum fields on such a background, a state is assigned to each admissible hypersurface, as a density operator ρ_Σ or a vector state Ψ_Σ in a suitable representation. In curved-spacetime quantum field theory there is in general no single preferred Fock representation, and the algebraic formulation, in which states are positive normalized functionals on a net of local observable algebras, is the more appropriate setting (Wald 1994; Hollands & Wald 2015). 13.2 Hypersurface Evolution and Its Limits A natural starting point is the Tomonaga–Schwinger equation (Tomonaga 1946; Schwinger 1948), i ħ δΨ_Σ / δΣ(x) = H_int(x) Ψ_Σ, which describes how a state changes under local deformations of a spacelike hypersurface. Its formal integrability condition is [H_int(x), H_int(y)] = 0 for spacelike-separated x and y. For interacting fields this expression requires a treatment of operator-valued distributions and their domains; it is not by itself an existence theorem. CTGP – explained proposes an open-system analogue, δρ_Σ / δΣ(x) = ℒ_x(ρ_Σ), with conditional integrability (Proposition 17.16) and a conditional continuum limit (Theorem 17.17). These are valuable starting points, but they are ensemble-level results: they concern density operators. The next subsection shows why a single-outcome ontology needs more. 13.3 Ensemble Consistency Is Not Realized-History Consistency Proposition 13.2 (Equal ensembles, different histories). There exist two instrument sequences whose channels coincide at every stage, so that they assign the same density operator to every stage for every initial state, but whose realized-history measures differ. Proof. On a qubit, let the first instrument be either I_1 = { |0⟩⟨0|, |1⟩⟨1| } or I_2 = { 1/√2, Z/√2 }, where Z = |0⟩⟨0| − |1⟩⟨1|, and let the second be the measurement of X with outcomes ±, {|+⟩⟨+|, |−⟩⟨−|}. Both first instruments implement the complete dephasing channel ρ ↦ |0⟩⟨0|ρ|0⟩⟨0| + |1⟩⟨1|ρ|1⟩⟨1|, so the averaged states agree at every stage. For the initial state |+⟩, I_1 yields outcomes 0 and 1 with probability 1/2 each and leaves |0⟩ or |1⟩, after which the X outcome is uniformly random. I_2 yields its two outcomes with probability 1/2 each and leaves |+⟩ or |−⟩, after which the X outcome is perfectly correlated with the first outcome. The joint laws of (first event, second event) are uniform in the first case and perfectly correlated in the second: the mutual information is 0 bits in one case and 1 bit in the other. □ Proposition 13.2 is an instance of the non-uniqueness of unravellings of a quantum channel (Wiseman & Milburn 2010). Its consequence for CTGP is direct: a covariant or hypersurface-consistent master equation does not by itself supply a covariant stochastic ontology. Two theories can agree on every density operator on every hypersurface and still disagree about the probabilities of the realized histories that, on CTGP, are what actually happened. Compatibility with a single-outcome ontology therefore needs a consistency theorem at the level of realized histories, not only at the level of states. 13.4 A Realized-History Consistency Theorem for Causally Local Instruments The following theorem is a finite-dimensional analogue of the required relativistic result. Events occur at a finite set of slots, ordered by a partial order intended to represent causal precedence; a foliation is any way of advancing through the slots consistently with that order. Definition 13.3 (Causally local event structure). A causally local event structure consists of a finite partially ordered set (E, ≺) of event slots, a finite-dimensional Hilbert space H with initial state Ψ_0, and for each slot x an instrument {K^x_a : a ∈ A_x} on H, such that for every pair of incomparable (spacelike) slots x and y, every K^x_a commutes with every K^y_b. A foliation is a linear extension λ = (x_1, …, x_N) of ≺; its successive initial segments {x_1, …, x_j} are the regions to the past of its successive hypersurfaces. For an outcome assignment α = (a_x)_{x∈E}, set v_λ(α) = K^{x_N}_{a_{x_N}} ⋯ K^{x_1}_{a_{x_1}} Ψ_0, P_λ(α) = ‖v_λ(α)‖². Theorem 13.4 (Realized-history consistency). In a causally local event structure: (a) Foliation independence. v_λ(α) does not depend on the foliation λ. Hence the probability of every complete event history, P(α) = P_λ(α), and the corresponding final state are foliation- independent, and Σ_α P(α) = 1. (b) Consistency on every hypersurface. For every order ideal I ⊆ E (the past of some hypersurface) and every assignment α_I of outcomes to the slots in I, the probability of α_I and the conditional state on the hypersurface bounding I are the same for every foliation of I, and they do not depend on the instruments at slots outside I. (c) No dependence on instruments outside the designated predecessor set. For every set X ⊆ E, the marginal law of the outcomes in X depends only on the instruments in its predecessor set with respect to ≺, ↓X = { y : y ⪯ x for some x ∈ X }. Identifying ↓X with the operationally relevant relativistic causal past of X requires additional physical assumptions, discussed after Corollary 13.5. Proof. (a) Any two linear extensions of a finite partial order are connected by a sequence of adjacent transpositions of incomparable elements. To see this, let λ and λ′ first differ at position j, where λ′ has x′. In λ, x′ occurs later; every element between position j and x′ in λ precedes x′ in λ, so it is not above x′, and follows x′ in λ′, so it is not below x′; hence it is incomparable with x′. Moving x′ leftward in λ by adjacent transpositions with these elements yields a linear extension that agrees with λ′ in one more position, and induction completes the argument. Each such transposition exchanges two commuting operators and leaves v_λ(α) unchanged. Normalization follows from Σ_a (K^x_a)† K^x_a = 1 applied slot by slot. (b) Because I is a down-set, any foliation of I extends to a foliation of E that lists I first, and (a) applied to the sub-order I gives independence of the foliation of I. Summing ‖v‖² over the outcomes of the slots after I and using completeness of their instruments gives ‖v_I(α_I)‖², which involves only instruments in I. (c) Apply (b) with I = ↓X, which is an order ideal containing X; the marginal on X is a marginal of the law of α_I. □ Corollary 13.5 (Foliation-invisibility of the generative order in causally local finite models). In a causally local event structure, suppose CTGP’s generative order is one particular foliation λ*. Then every event proposition about any order ideal has the same probability whether computed along λ* or along any other foliation. The realized assignment α is a single object, which different foliations merely order differently; once the truth profile indexes events by slot rather than by stage number, the complete profile after the whole region has been generated is the same set of propositions whichever foliation is taken as generative. Proof. Theorem 13.4(a)–(b). □ Corollary 13.5 is established for finite event structures under the stated locality assumption, not for arbitrary relativistic quantum dynamics. It is the realized-history counterpart of CTGP’s observational equivalence (CTGP – explained, §12.1): within causally local finite models, under the commutation assumption of Definition 13.3, the choice of generative foliation has no empirical signature, and a single realized history can be represented on any family of hypersurfaces without multiplying the number of histories. If each K^x_a is invariant under a symmetry group in the sense of §3, Lemma 3.7 applies slot by slot, and Theorem 13.4 holds in every frame. Foliation-independent probabilities do not by themselves establish that any foliation is ontologically privileged. What Corollary 13.5 shows is that privileging one foliation is empirically invisible under the stated assumptions. That one foliation is in fact generative is CTGP’s interpretive thesis, supplied separately, in the same way that Thesis 5.10 supplies the ontological reading of description orbits. The locality condition cannot be dropped. In the numerical check (Appendix B, item 14), a brickwork arrangement of eight two-outcome instruments on six qubits has 136 foliations, and all of them assign identical probabilities to all 256 outcome histories, to within 7 × 10⁻¹⁸. Changing the instruments outside the predecessor set of a slot changes that slot’s marginal by at most 8 × 10⁻¹⁶. But if two slots treated as spacelike act on overlapping qubits, so that their operators fail to commute, the two orders assign history measures that differ by total-variation distance 0.165. Theorem 13.4 must also be read with a caveat specific to quantum field theory. Part (c) is a statement about the abstract order ≺: it says that outcomes do not depend on instruments outside a designated predecessor set, not that relativistic operational no-signaling holds for arbitrary spacetime regions or measurement procedures. The two coincide only if ≺ is the operationally relevant causal order. For idealized point-like slots it is. For extended regions, however, the transitive closure of “overlaps the causal future of” can relate two regions that are in fact spacelike-separated, and naive local measurement rules then permit superluminal signaling (Sorkin 1993). A relativistic version of the theorem therefore needs a measurement framework in which operations localized in regions respect relativistic causality; frameworks of this kind exist in algebraic quantum field theory (Fewster & Verch 2020; Bostelmann, Fewster & Ruep 2021). The finite theorem is an analogue of the relativistic result, not that result itself. The relativistic target can now be stated precisely. Target result 13.6 (Relativistic realized-history consistency). For a regulated relativistic single- outcome model on a fixed globally hyperbolic background, given by a hypersurface-dependent family of completely positive maps ℰ_{Σ_1→Σ_2} together with instruments assigning probabilities to realized events between hypersurfaces, establish: (i) existence and normalization of the event-history measure; (ii) composition consistency for nested regions; (iii) compatibility of operations in spacelike-separated regions, in a framework free of Sorkin-type signaling; (iv) consistent restriction of event histories to overlapping regions; and (v) a well-defined single-outcome realization. Then prove that the probability of every common event-history proposition is independent of the admissible intermediate hypersurface decomposition, and that the CTGP truth profile updates consistently from the realized history. Theorem 13.4 proves this for finitely many slots and finite-dimensional operators, Proposition 13.7 (§13.5) extends it to countably many slots with finite pasts, and Theorem 13.9 (§13.6) establishes its measurement-model version for finite networks of gapless detectors on a free relativistic field. The assumptions of the relativistic version must be made precise enough not to assume the conclusion. 13.5 Toward the Relativistic Case: Countable Event Structures and the Remaining Assumptions One step beyond Theorem 13.4 can be taken now: from finitely many event slots to countably many, provided every slot has only finitely many predecessors. Proposition 13.7 (Countable past-finite event structures). Let (E, ≺) be a countable partially ordered set in which every slot x has a finite predecessor set ↓x. Let H be a Hilbert space with initial state Ψ_0, and attach to each slot an instrument with finitely many outcomes and bounded Kraus operators, such that the Kraus operators of incomparable slots commute. Then: (a) There is a unique probability measure P on the product σ-algebra of the space ∏_{x∈E} A_x of complete outcome assignments (the σ-algebra generated by cylinder sets) whose marginal on every finite order ideal I is the law P_I(α_I) = ‖v_I(α_I)‖² of Theorem 13.4. (b) E admits generative orders, that is, enumerations x_1, x_2, … in which every initial segment is an order ideal. Sequential generation along any generative order reproduces P, so P does not depend on which generative order is chosen. (c) For every finite set X ⊆ E, the marginal of P on X depends only on the instruments in the finite predecessor set ↓X. Proof. Theorem 13.4 and its proof apply verbatim to bounded Kraus operators on an arbitrary Hilbert space, since they use only finite products of operators and the completeness relation. (a) If I ⊆ J are finite order ideals, I is an order ideal of the finite structure J, so by Theorem 13.4(b) the marginal of P_J on I is P_I. Every finite set X lies in the finite order ideal ↓X, and finite order ideals are closed under finite unions, so {P_I} is a consistent family indexed by a directed set that exhausts E. Since every A_x is finite, the Kolmogorov extension theorem yields a unique probability measure P on ∏_{x∈E} A_x with these marginals. (b) Enumerate E as e_1, e_2, …, and for k = 1, 2, … append the elements of the finite set ↓e_k that have not yet been listed, in an order compatible with ≺. Every predecessor of such an element lies in ↓e_k and is listed earlier, so every initial segment is an order ideal, and every element is eventually listed. Sequential generation along any generative order assigns the law P_I to each initial segment I (Theorem 13.4(b)), and hence, by the uniqueness in (a), the measure P. (c) The marginal of P on X is the marginal of P_{↓X}, which by Theorem 13.4(b) involves only the instruments in ↓X. □ Proposition 13.7 removes the finiteness of the event set while keeping discreteness: countably many slots, finitely many outcomes per slot, and bounded operators. It constructs a probability measure over infinite histories; it does not assert that the conditional quantum state converges to a limit after infinitely many events. Past-finiteness is natural in regulated models, and it is the defining property of causal sets grown from a beginning, whose generative orders are the natural labelings of the growth process (§15.2(iii)). In the numerical check (Appendix B, item 14), the law on an order ideal computed from that ideal’s instruments alone agrees with the marginal of the full law to within 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁶, as part (a) requires. A relativistic extension of Theorem 13.4 must supply, in addition, at least the following. (E1) Localization. Instruments attached to spacetime regions rather than to abstract slots, with continuous outcome spaces handled by measure-valued instruments. (E2) Causal locality of operations. The Kraus operators by which events update the state must be localized in the corresponding regions, so that, by microcausality, those attached to spacelike- separated regions commute. This is a requirement on the operators themselves. It does not by itself make the instruments causally admissible (Example 13.10). (E3) Operational causal order. The predecessor relation must coincide with the causal precedence that is relevant to signaling, and the actual instruments and their compositions must be shown not to signal outside it. Commutativity of field observables and localization of Kraus operators are not enough for this (Example 13.10). For extended regions it requires a measurement framework that excludes Sorkin-type signaling, such as the probe-based schemes of Fewster and Verch (2020) and Polo-Gómez, Garay and Martín-Martínez (2022); Theorem 13.9 verifies it for gapless detector networks on a free field. (E4) Continuum limit. A limit in which the countable, past-finite slot structure is replaced by spacetime regions, with the history measure surviving the limit as a point process or measure- valued construction. (E5) Generative hypersurfaces. The level sets of cosmological time (§13.1) must define order ideals of the event structure. This requires a convention for events whose regions straddle a level set, and, for generability, a physically instantiated present state on each level set. Proposition 13.7 does not derive a preferred cosmic-time slicing; it shows only that any generative order of the events yields the same history measure. (E6) Interactions and renormalization. The instruments must arise from an interacting, renormalized field dynamics rather than being posited. Proposition 13.7 settles none of (E1)–(E6). It shows that the step from finitely many to countably many events, with a single history measure independent of the generative order, raises no difficulty of its own. Section 13.6 shows that a finite network of gapless detectors on a free relativistic field satisfies (E1)–(E3), and (E5) when its coupling regions are separated by level sets of cosmological time, and §13.7 extends this to spontaneous Gaussian collapses of smeared fields on a fixed family of regions. Section 13.10 extends it further to collapses at Poisson-distributed locations, conditionally on causal orderability, and §13.9 gives a sufficient condition for (E3) that does not depend on free fields. (E4) and (E6) remain open. 13.6 A Relativistic Construction: Gapless Detectors on a Free Field The requirements (E1)–(E3) and (E5) can be met exactly in one relativistic setting: finite networks of gapless two-level detectors coupled linearly to a free scalar field on a fixed globally hyperbolic spacetime. In this measurement model the realized events are detector outcomes triggered by specified couplings, not spontaneous flashes; the spontaneous version is discussed in §13.7. Setting. Let φ be a free real scalar field on a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g), obeying a linear hyperbolic field equation. For real test functions f and h, the smeared fields satisfy [φ(f), φ(h)] = i E(f, h), where E is the causal propagator, the difference of the advanced and retarded fundamental solutions (sign conventions aside). Its key property is that E(f, h) = 0 whenever no causal curve joins the supports of f and h (Wald 1994; Hollands & Wald 2015). Write W(f) = exp(i φ(f)) for the Weyl operators, which satisfy W(f) W(h) = e^{−(i/2) E(f,h)} W(f + h), W(−h) W(g) W(h) = e^{i E(h,g)} W(g). The field may be in any state ω; where a formula needs one, ω is a quasi-free state with vanishing one-point function, such as the vacuum, for which ω(W(g)) = exp(−½ ω(φ(g)²)). Definition 13.8 (Gapless detector network). A gapless detector network consists of finitely many two-level detectors j = 1, …, n. Detector j has a coupling region O_j, a real test function f_j (smooth and compactly supported) with support in O_j, and a coupling constant λ_j. It starts in the state |0⟩, interacts through the unitary U_j = exp(i λ_j σ_x^{(j)} ⊗ φ(f_j)), and is then read out by a projective measurement of σ_z^{(j)}. The regions are causally orderable: the transitive closure ≺ of the relation “O_i meets the causal future of O_j”, for i ≠ j, is a strict partial order. Gapless means that the detector has no internal Hamiltonian while it interacts. The time-ordered evolution generated by a coupling λ_j χ_j(t) σ_x ∫ F_j φ then differs from U_j only by a phase, because the commutators of the interaction at different times are multiples of σ_x² = 1. Since U_j = cos(λ_j φ(f_j)) + i σ_x sin(λ_j φ(f_j)), the instrument induced on the field by detector j has Kraus operators K^j_0 = cos(λ_j φ(f_j)), K^j_1 = i sin(λ_j φ(f_j)), which are bounded functions of the smeared field in O_j and satisfy (K^j_0)† K^j_0 + (K^j_1)† K^j_1 = 1. The nonselective update of detector j acts on Weyl operators by multiplication with a number: N_j*(W(g)) = ⟨0| U_j† (W(g) ⊗ 1) U_j |0⟩ = cos(λ_j E(f_j, g)) W(g). This follows from the second Weyl relation, applied separately to the two eigenvalues s = ±1 of σ_x, each occurring with probability 1/2 in |0⟩. A unitary kick W(α f_j) acts in the same diagonal way: W(−α f_j) W(g) W(α f_j) = e^{i α E(f_j,g)} W(g). Theorem 13.9 (Gapless detector networks). In a gapless detector network on a free field, for any state of the field: (a) Realized-history consistency. The probability of every complete outcome history, and the corresponding conditional state, are the same for every foliation, that is, for every linear extension of ≺. (b) Operational no-signaling, including Sorkin configurations. Let X be a set of detectors, and let j ∉ X be a detector with E(f_j, f_x) = 0 for every x ∈ X; this holds in particular when no causal curve joins O_j to any O_x. Then the joint law of the outcomes of the detectors in X is the same whether detector j is operated with any coupling λ_j, replaced by a kick W(α f_j), or not operated at all, whatever other detectors are operated before, between, or after them. (c) Explicit statistics. If detector B is preceded by detectors j_1, …, j_r whose outcomes are not conditioned on, and the field is in a quasi-free state with vanishing one-point function, then P(B reads 1) = ½ − ½ exp(−2 λ_B² ω(φ(f_B)²)) · Π_{k=1}^{r} cos(2 λ_B λ_{j_k} E(f_{j_k}, f_B)). Proof. (a) If two regions are incomparable, no causal curve joins them, so E(f_i, f_j) = 0, the Weyl operators W(s f_i) and W(t f_j) commute for all s and t, and so do all bounded functions of φ(f_i) and φ(f_j). The Kraus operators of incomparable detectors therefore commute. Theorem 13.4 applies, in the form for bounded Kraus operators noted in the proof of Proposition 13.7, in the GNS representation of the state. (b) Compute the joint law of X in the Heisenberg picture, along any foliation. Every detector outside X is marginalized, so it contributes its nonselective update N_k*. Every detector in X contributes a selective update K† (·) K with K = cos(λφ(f_x)) or i sin(λφ(f_x)); each of these maps a Weyl operator W(g) to a linear combination of Weyl operators W(g + c f_x) with real c. Starting from the identity W(0), the operator under evaluation is therefore at every step a finite linear combination of Weyl operators W(g) with g in the real span V_X of { f_x : x ∈ X }. Each nonselective update of a detector k ∉ X multiplies such a W(g) by cos(λ_k E(f_k, g)). For k = j, E(f_j, g) = 0 for every g ∈ V_X by linearity, so the factor is 1, and the same holds for a kick. Detector j therefore drops out of the joint law of X. (c) Write sin²(λ_B φ(f_B)) = ½ − ¼ (W(2λ_B f_B) + W(−2λ_B f_B)). Each preceding detector, marginalized, multiplies W(±2λ_B f_B) by cos(2 λ_B λ_{j_k} E(f_{j_k}, f_B)), and ω(W(±2λ_B f_B)) = exp(−2 λ_B² ω(φ(f_B)²)). □ Part (b) is the statement that matters for CTGP, and its scope should be stated exactly. It is state-independent; it holds for arbitrary smooth, compactly supported real smearings within finite, causally orderable networks, under the free-field and gapless-detector assumptions; and it holds in Sorkin’s configuration. There, Alice’s region A is causally separated from Bob’s region B, while an intermediate region C overlaps the causal future of A and the causal past of B. A gapless detector at C cannot carry Alice’s choice to Bob, because its nonselective update multiplies each Weyl operator by a number instead of spreading it. Part (c) makes this explicit: Bob’s statistics depend on a preceding detector only through the factor cos(2 λ_B λ_j E(f_j, f_B)), which equals 1 for every detector causally separated from B. The essential property is that the nonselective update is diagonal on Weyl operators. It holds for gapless linear couplings with any such smearing. It can fail for detectors with internal dynamics during the interaction, and de Ramón, Papageorgiou and Martín-Martínez (2021) find that spatially extended non-relativistic detector models can propagate a field’s initial conditions faster than light. The conclusion agrees with detector-based measurement theory, in which updates induced by local probe couplings avoid Sorkin-type signaling (Polo-Gómez, Garay & Martín-Martínez 2022; compare Fewster & Verch 2020). Three things should be distinguished in interpreting the theorem: the spacetime support O_j of the detector–field interaction; the location and timing of the detector’s readout; and the effective instrument on the field obtained by conditioning on that readout. Theorem 13.9 concerns the third. It proves causal properties of the effective field instruments of the network, assuming that each detector is read out after its interaction and treating the detector as an idealized probe. When a coupling region is extended, a fully spacetime-local implementation of the detector together with its readout would require further modeling of the probe itself. This qualification concerns physical realization; it does not affect the algebraic proof. Theorem 13.9 is exact within its model, and the numerical checks below only illustrate it. Exactness within a model is not physical completeness. The model assumes a fixed spacetime, a free field, externally specified couplings, and detectors with no internal Hamiltonian during the interaction, and these assumptions are central to the proof. A nonzero energy gap makes the coupled detector observable time-dependent, and the nonselective update then generally ceases to be diagonal on Weyl operators. The theorem is therefore not a claim about arbitrary relativistic detector measurements, realistic detector hardware, or interacting fields. Example 13.10 (Localized Kraus operators are not enough). Replace the detector at C by the unsharp Lüders instrument with Kraus operators √(g(φ(f_C))) and √(1 − g(φ(f_C))), where g(x) = (1 + tanh 2x)/2. Its Kraus operators are bounded functions of the smeared field in C, so it satisfies (E2), and they commute with every operation localized in a region causally separated from C. But its nonselective update is not diagonal on Weyl operators: Σ_s √(g_s(φ(f_C))) W(h) √(g_s(φ(f_C))) = W(h) Σ_s √(g_s(φ(f_C) + E(h, f_C))) √(g_s(φ(f_C))), with g_+ = g and g_− = 1 − g. The right-hand side is W(h) times a non-constant function of φ(f_C), which fails to commute with φ(f_A) when E(f_A, f_C) ≠ 0. An operation by Alice can therefore change Bob’s statistics through C even though A and B are causally separated. This is Sorkin’s problem. A numerical realization reproduces the commutation pattern of Sorkin’s configuration (E(f_A, f_B) = 0, E(f_A, f_C) = E(f_C, f_B) = 1) with two oscillator modes in the vacuum state, using λ_A = 0.9, λ_C = 0.8, λ_B = 0.7, and a kick with α = 1.5 (Appendix B, item 17). With a gapless detector at C, Bob’s probability of reading 1 is 0.3665447170, equal to the formula of Theorem 13.9(c), and neither Alice’s detector nor her kick changes it by more than 10⁻¹⁵. With the unsharp Lüders instrument at C, Bob’s probability is 0.275038, and Alice’s detector shifts it by −0.021492 and her kick by −0.046628. These values change by less than 10⁻⁷ when the Fock cutoff is raised from 40 to 50 quanta per mode. Because Theorem 13.9(b) is state-independent and uses only the commutation relations, the two-mode realization is an exact test of the algebraic mechanism, not a computation in a particular spacetime. Example 13.10 shows why (E2) and (E3) must be kept separate. Localizing the Kraus operators of each operation is not enough. Causal admissibility is a property of the actual instruments and of their composition, and it must be proved for the class of operations in use; Theorem 13.9 proves it for gapless detector networks on a free field, and Theorem 13.13 for every network of Weyl-diagonal instruments. Connection to CTGP. In a gapless detector network the realized detector outcomes are Tier 1 events, and the complete present state on an admissible hypersurface consists of the field state together with the detectors’ records. For a specified network, the law of the next outcome depends only on that present state, so the network is generable in the sense of Definition 12.1, (A2)–(A5). The truth profile records the realized outcomes and is dynamically idle. Because cosmological time is in general only locally Lipschitz (§13.1), the construction is formulated for arbitrary admissible orderings. A generic globally hyperbolic spacetime also admits smooth Cauchy temporal functions (Bernal & Sánchez 2003, 2005). Whenever the coupling regions can be separated by level sets of the cosmological time T, ordering them by T is a linear extension of ≺, because T increases strictly along future-directed causal curves. CTGP’s generative order is then one of the admissible foliations, and by Theorem 13.9(a) and Corollary 13.5 the history law is the same for it as for any other. Three steps must be kept apart here. First, the network’s history law is independent of the admissible ordering of its regions; Theorem 13.9 establishes this. Second, the background spacetime admits a regular cosmological time whose level sets separate the regions; this is an assumption about the geometry. Third, those level sets are the ontological presents; this is CTGP’s interpretation. Theorem 13.9 supports only the first step, which is what makes the other two consistent with it. Within this class, then, the measurement-model version of Target 13.6 holds for finite networks: (i)–(v) are satisfied, with (iii) secured by part (b). What remains open is the spontaneous version, detectors with internal dynamics, interacting fields, and infinitely many or continuously distributed events, that is, (E4) and (E6). 13.7 Toward Spontaneous Events: Weyl-Diagonal Collapse The proof of Theorem 13.9(b) uses one property of the gapless detector: its nonselective update multiplies every Weyl operator by a number. Inserting arbitrary stochastic collapses into the model would risk reintroducing the signaling of Example 13.10. The natural route toward spontaneous events is therefore to keep that property and ask which event-generating instruments have it. Definition 13.11 (Weyl-diagonal instrument). Let O be a bounded spacetime region and H_O a finite- dimensional space of real test functions supported in O. An instrument with finitely or continuously many outcomes is a Weyl-diagonal instrument localized in O if (i) its Kraus operators belong to the von Neumann algebra generated by the Weyl operators W(h), h ∈ H_O, and (ii) its nonselective update satisfies N*(W(g)) = c(g) W(g) for every test function g, for some function c. Examples are the gapless detector of Definition 13.8, with c(g) = cos(λ E(f, g)), and the unitary kick, with c(g) = e^{iαE(f,g)}. The random-displacement channel ρ ↦ ∫ W(h) ρ W(h)* dμ(h), with μ a probability measure on H_O, is also Weyl-diagonal, with c(g) = ∫ e^{iE(h,g)} dμ(h). The nonselective gapless detector is exactly the random displacement by +λf or −λf, each with probability 1/2. Proposition 13.12 (Gaussian collapse of a smeared field). Let h be a real test function supported in O and σ > 0. The instrument with outcome y ∈ ℝ and Kraus operators K_y = (2πσ²)^{−1/4} exp(−(φ(h) − y)² / (4σ²)) is normalized, ∫ K_y† K_y dy = 1, and Weyl-diagonal, with N*(W(g)) = exp(−E(h, g)² / (8σ²)) W(g). Its outcome y is distributed by the Born rule for φ(h) smoothed at width σ, and the selective update multiplies the spectral amplitude of φ(h) by a Gaussian of width σ centred at y. It is a collapse of the smeared field φ(h) around the realized value y. Combining it with a hit probability p, so that with probability 1 − p nothing happens, gives the Weyl-diagonal instrument with c(g) = 1 − p + p exp(−E(h, g)² / (8σ²)). Proof. Normalization is the Gaussian integral ∫ (2πσ²)^{−1/2} exp(−(x − y)²/(2σ²)) dy = 1, applied in the spectral representation of φ(h). By the second Weyl relation, W(g)* φ(h) W(g) = φ(h) + E(g, h), so each Kraus operator satisfies K_y W(g) = W(g) K_y(φ(h) + e) with e = E(g, h). Hence N*(W(g)) = W(g) ∫ (2πσ²)^{−1/2} exp(−[(φ(h) + e − y)² + (φ(h) − y)²] / (4σ²)) dy. Writing u = φ(h) − y, the exponent is −[2(u + e/2)² + e²/2] / (4σ²), and the integral over y equals exp(−e²/(8σ²)), independently of φ(h). Since E(g, h)² = E(h, g)², this is the stated multiplier. The version with hit probability adds (1 − p) W(g). □ Theorem 13.13 (Weyl-diagonal networks). Theorem 13.9(a) and (b) hold for every finite, causally orderable network on a free field in which each operation is a Weyl-diagonal instrument localized in its region, with finitely or continuously many outcomes. For (b), j may be any operation whose space H_j satisfies E(h, f) = 0 for all h ∈ H_j and all f ∈ H_x, x ∈ X; in particular any operation in a region causally separated from every region of X. Proof. (a) Weyl operators of causally separated regions commute, and hence so do the von Neumann algebras they generate and the Kraus operators in them. The proof of Theorem 13.4 then applies, with sums over continuous outcomes replaced by integrals. (b) Let V_X be the sum of the spaces H_x for x ∈ X, and M_X the von Neumann algebra generated by the W(g), g ∈ V_X. In the Heisenberg picture, the selective updates of the operations in X preserve M_X, because their Kraus operators lie in M_X. The nonselective update of any other operation multiplies each W(g) by a number; it therefore preserves the linear span of { W(g) : g ∈ V_X }, and, being normal, it preserves the σ-weak closure of that span, which is M_X. For the operation j, every W(g) with g ∈ V_X commutes with j’s Kraus operators, so N_j*(W(g)) = W(g) Σ K† K = W(g). The update of j thus acts as the identity on M_X, and j drops out of the joint law of X. □ Corollary 13.14 (A regulated spontaneous-collapse network). Fix a finite, causally orderable family of bounded regions O_k, with smearings h_k supported in O_k, hit probabilities p_k, and widths σ_k. Let the law specify that at each O_k a collapse event occurs with probability p_k, independently of the state, and that its centre y is drawn by the Born rule with the update of Proposition 13.12. The resulting single-outcome process is spontaneous in the sense of GRW: its events are prescribed by the law, not by an experimenter. It has foliation-independent history probabilities, its event statistics in any set of regions do not depend on operations in causally separated regions, including in Sorkin’s configuration, and it is generable from the present field state together with the realized events. By Proposition 13.7, whose Kolmogorov extension holds equally for standard Borel outcome spaces such as ℝ, the history measure extends to countable past-finite families of regions. Proof. Theorem 13.13 with Proposition 13.12, and Proposition 13.7. □ In the two-mode realization of Sorkin’s configuration used in Example 13.10, replacing the intermediate operation by a Gaussian collapse with σ = 0.6 gives Bob’s probability of reading 1 as 0.3449027185 when a collapse is certain and 0.2541731691 when it occurs with probability 0.4. Both values agree with the formula ½ − ½ exp(−λ_B²)(1 − p + p exp(−λ_B²/(2σ²))). Neither Alice’s detector nor her kick changes them by more than 10⁻¹³ (Appendix B, item 18). Corollary 13.14 is a first construction of spontaneous relativistic events with the properties CTGP requires, but its limitations are substantial and should be stated plainly. (L1) Fixed regions. The family of collapse regions is fixed in advance. This is a regularization and is not Lorentz-invariant. Section 13.10 lets a covariant Poisson law choose the centres, proves what then holds exactly, and identifies the obstructions to a fully covariant version. (L2) Linear field observables.
