The collapse localizes the value of a smeared field, which is linear in φ. Collapse theories that localize matter act on mass density, which is quadratic in the fields, and functions of quadratic observables are not Weyl-diagonal in general; Theorem 13.13 does not apply to them. A mathematically consistent stochastic update need not produce the localization that an objective-collapse ontology requires, and whether field-linear collapse can play that role is open. (L3) Energy. As in collapse models generally, the collapses do not conserve the field’s energy, because narrowing the distribution of φ(h) broadens that of its conjugate. Proposition 13.16 gives the exact, state-independent cost of each collapse. Any physical version must respect the experimental bounds on such effects (Bassi et al. 2013). (L4) Free fields and fixed background. Interacting fields, for which commutators of smeared fields are not multiples of the identity, and dynamical geometry are not covered. The construction also illustrates Proposition 13.2 once more. The same random-displacement channel can be unravelled by unitary kicks, which localize nothing, by the cosine and sine operators of the gapless detector, which localize φ(f) only modulo π/λ, or by Gaussian collapses, which localize it around a single value. No-signaling depends only on the channel. Localization depends on the unravelling, which is the part that a collapse ontology must justify. Proposition 13.15 shows that the unread channel of a Gaussian collapse is itself a Gaussian mixture of unitary kicks. 13.8 The Unread Collapse Channel: Random Kicks and Energy–Momentum Cost Conventions for continuous outcomes. From here on, an instrument with outcome space (Ω, 𝔉), a standard Borel space such as ℝ or ℝⁿ, is a map Δ ↦ I(Δ) from 𝔉 to normal completely positive maps on trace-class operators, countably additive in trace norm on every state, with I(Ω) trace-preserving. Its Heisenberg duals I*(Δ) are normal, and the dual N* = I*(Ω) of the unread channel is unital. The Gaussian collapse of Proposition 13.12 is such an instrument, with Ω = ℝ and I(Δ)(ρ) = ∫_Δ K_y ρ K_y dy, the integral converging in trace norm. All statements about continuous outcomes below are made in this sense. Proposition 13.15 (The unread Gaussian collapse is a random kick). Let A be a self-adjoint operator, possibly unbounded, σ > 0, and K_y = (2πσ²)^{−1/4} exp(−(A − y)²/(4σ²)) for y ∈ ℝ. (a) The operators K_y are bounded, y ↦ K_y is strongly continuous, and ∫ K_y² dy = 1. The outcome y is distributed as the Born-rule distribution of A convolved with a centred Gaussian of variance σ². (b) The unread channel is a Gaussian mixture of unitary kicks generated by A: N(ρ) = ∫ K_y ρ K_y dy = ∫ γ(t) e^{−itA} ρ e^{itA} dt, γ(t) = (2σ²/π)^{1/2} exp(−2σ²t²), so the kick parameter t is centred Gaussian with variance 1/(4σ²). In a spectral representation of A, N multiplies the kernel ρ(a, a′) by exp(−(a − a′)²/(8σ²)), the characteristic function of γ evaluated at a − a′. Proof. (a) Boundedness and strong continuity follow from the functional calculus. For every vector ψ with spectral measure μ_ψ of A, ∫ ⟨ψ, K_y² ψ⟩ dy = ∫∫ (2πσ²)^{−1/2} exp(−(a − y)²/(2σ²)) dy dμ_ψ(a) = ‖ψ‖², and the same integrand, integrated over a only, is the convolution formula for the outcome density. (b) By the spectral theorem in multiplication form, the Hilbert space is L²(X, ν) with A acting as multiplication by a real function a. A trace-class ρ is Hilbert–Schmidt, with a kernel ρ(ξ, ξ′). The kernel of K_y ρ K_y is κ_y(a(ξ)) ρ(ξ, ξ′) κ_y(a(ξ′)), with κ_y(a) = (2πσ²)^{−1/4} exp(−(a − y)²/(4σ²)); completing the square as in the proof of Proposition 13.12 and integrating over y gives exp(−(a(ξ) − a(ξ′))²/(8σ²)) ρ(ξ, ξ′). The kernel of e^{−itA} ρ e^{itA} is e^{−it(a(ξ) − a(ξ′))} ρ(ξ, ξ′), and ∫ γ(t) e^{−itd} dt = exp(−d²/(8σ²)). The two trace-class operators therefore have the same kernel and coincide. □ The selective instrument and its unread channel must be kept apart. Selectively, a collapse with outcome y multiplies the spectral amplitude of A by a Gaussian of width σ centred at y and genuinely narrows the distribution of A. The unread channel is a random-unitary channel, and it has a second unravelling in which the recorded outcome is the kick parameter t. That record is independent of the state, and each kick leaves the distribution of A unchanged. The two unravellings localize differently and have the same channel, which is Proposition 13.2 in its sharpest form. No-signaling, a property of the channel, can therefore be established in the random-kick picture, while localization, a property of the unravelling, cannot. For a free field, e^{itφ(h)} W(g) e^{−itφ(h)} = e^{−itE(h,g)} W(g), so each kick multiplies Weyl operators by phases. This is the mechanism behind Proposition 13.12, and it is what Theorem 13.17 below abstracts. Proposition 13.16 (Energy–momentum cost of Gaussian collapse). Let N be the unread channel of Proposition 13.15. (a) Position collapse of a particle. Take one degree of freedom with [Q, P] = iħ and a real potential V that is locally integrable and bounded below, and let H be the self-adjoint operator of the closed quadratic form q(ψ) = ‖Pψ‖²/(2m) + ⟨ψ, Vψ⟩ on the form domain D(P) ∩ D(|V|^{1/2}). Call a state ρ = Σ_i p_i |ψ_i⟩⟨ψ_i| of finite energy if every ψ_i lies in the form domain and ℰ(ρ) = Σ_i p_i q(ψ_i) is finite. For A = Q, if ρ has finite energy, then so does N(ρ), and ℰ(N(ρ)) − ℰ(ρ) = ħ² / (8mσ²), independently of ρ and of V. Collapsing all three coordinates of a particle in three dimensions costs 3ħ²/(8mσ²). (b) Quadratic systems. For finitely many canonical degrees of freedom R = (q_1, …, q_n, p_1, …, p_n), let H = ½ Rᵀ M R with M real, symmetric, and positive definite, and let A be linear in R. Then [A, [A, H]] is a multiple of the identity and, for every state of finite energy in the sense of (a), ℰ(N(ρ)) − ℰ(ρ) = −(1/(8σ²)) [A, [A, H]]. (c) Free fields in 3 + 1 dimensions. For a free scalar field of mass m ≥ 0 on four-dimensional Minkowski space in its Fock representation, with energy–momentum P^μ, a real test function h with Eh ≠ 0, and A = φ(h), every state ρ of finite energy, Tr(ρ H) < ∞, satisfies Tr(N(ρ) P^μ) = Tr(ρ P^μ) + ΔP^μ(h) for all four generators, where ΔP^μ(h) = (1/(4σ²)) ∫ dμ_m(k) k^μ |ĥ(k)|², dμ_m(k) = d³k / ((2π)³ 2ω_k), with k⁰ = ω_k = (|k|² + m²)^{1/2} and ĥ(k) = ∫ h(x) e^{ik·x} d⁴x. Equivalently, ΔP^μ(h) is the conserved energy–momentum of the classical solution Eh, divided by 4σ². It does not depend on the state; it is unchanged when h is translated; it transforms as a four-vector, ΔP(h ∘ Λ^{−1}) = Λ ΔP(h); and it is future-directed and timelike. (d) Curved backgrounds. Let φ be the minimally coupled free scalar field of mass m ≥ 0 on a four-dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime, ω a Hadamard state, and h a real test function. Then the state ω_N = ω ∘ N* after an unread Gaussian collapse of φ(h) is Hadamard, its symmetrized two-point function is that of ω plus (Eh ⊗ Eh)/(4σ²), and its renormalized stress–energy tensor, defined by Hadamard point-splitting, is ⟨T_μν⟩_{ω_N} = ⟨T_μν⟩_ω + T^cl_μν[Eh] / (4σ²), where T^cl_μν[u] = ∇_μu ∇_νu − ½ g_μν (∇^αu ∇_αu − m²u²) is the classical stress–energy tensor, in signature (+, −, −, −). Proof. In each case N*(B) = ∫ γ(t) e^{itA} B e^{−itA} dt by Proposition 13.15(b). (a) The unitary e^{−itQ} preserves the form domain: it commutes with V, and the Weyl relation e^{itQ} P e^{−itQ} = P − ħt gives P e^{−itQ} = e^{−itQ}(P − ħt) on D(P). Hence q(e^{−itQ}ψ) = q(ψ) − (ħt/m)⟨ψ, Pψ⟩ + ħ²t²‖ψ‖²/(2m), where ⟨ψ, Pψ⟩ is finite on the form domain. The energy functional ℰ is affine, and, being a supremum of normal functionals given by spectral truncations of the bounded-below operator H, it satisfies ℰ(∫ γ(t) ρ_t dt) = ∫ γ(t) ℰ(ρ_t) dt by monotone convergence. Averaging over t, with γ of mean zero and variance 1/(4σ²), gives the result. (b) The unitary e^{itA} is a Weyl operator of the canonical system. It preserves the form domain of H and shifts R by t times a constant vector s, so H becomes H + t L + t² H(s), with L linear in R and H(s) the value of the quadratic form on s. Averaging gives H(s)/(4σ²), and differentiating twice at t = 0 shows that −[A, [A, H]] = 2H(s). (c) For real t, e^{itφ(h)} φ(f) e^{−itφ(h)} = φ(f) − t E(h, f), so conjugation by e^{itφ(h)} displaces the field by the classical solution −t Eh. Under a displacement by a classical solution u of finite energy, the normal-ordered generators transform as P^μ ↦ P^μ + t L^μ + t² P^μ[u], with L^μ linear in the creation and annihilation operators and P^μ[u] the classical energy–momentum of u. Because ω_k ≥ |k^i| mode by mode, −H ≤ P^i ≤ H as quadratic forms, so a state of finite energy has finite expectations of all four generators, and N(ρ) again has finite energy. The linear terms have the form L^μ = a(g_μ) + a(g_μ)*, with ω^{−1/2} g_μ square-integrable because ĥ decays rapidly on the mass shell, and the bound |Tr(ρ a(g))| ≤ ‖ω^{−1/2} g‖ Tr(ρ H)^{1/2} makes their expectations finite. Averaging over t therefore gives ΔP^μ = P^μ[Eh]/(4σ²). In the mode expansion, φ(h) = ∫ dμ_m(k) (ĥ(k)* a_k + ĥ(k) a_k†) with [a_k, a_q†] = (2π)³ 2ω_k δ³(k − q), and the same computation gives the stated integral. Translating h multiplies ĥ by a phase. Since ĥ ∘ Λ^{−1} is the transform of h ∘ Λ^{−1} and dμ_m is Lorentz-invariant, ΔP transforms as a four-vector. The Fourier transform of Eh is proportional to ĥ on the mass shell, so Eh ≠ 0 exactly when ĥ is not identically zero there. Then ΔP is an integral of future-directed causal vectors with a nonnegative weight that is not almost everywhere zero. For m > 0 these vectors are timelike. For m = 0 they are null, but ĥ is real- analytic and the future light cone in 3 + 1 dimensions, with its vertex removed, is connected, so ĥ is nonzero on an open subset of the cone containing non-parallel null directions, and their sum is timelike. (d) By Proposition 13.12, ω_N(W(f)) = exp(−E(h, f)²/(8σ²)) ω(W(f)). Differentiating ω_N(W(sf + tg)) twice at s = t = 0 shows that the symmetrized two-point function changes by E(h, f) E(h, g)/(4σ²), whose integral kernel is (Eh)(x)(Eh)(y)/(4σ²), a smooth function because Eh is a smooth solution; the antisymmetric part, iE/2, is unchanged. Adding a smooth symmetric kernel preserves the wavefront-set condition that defines Hadamard states. In Hadamard point-splitting, the renormalized stress–energy tensor is obtained by applying a fixed bidifferential operator to the two-point function minus a state-independent Hadamard parametrix, taking the coincidence limit, and adding local curvature terms that are the same for every state (Wald 1994; Hollands & Wald 2015). The difference between ω_N and ω is therefore that operator applied to (Eh ⊗ Eh)/(4σ²), whose coincidence limit is T^cl_μν[Eh]/(4σ²). □ The dimension matters for the last statement of (c). In 1 + 1 dimensions the same formula holds with d³k/(2π)³ replaced by dk/(2π), and ΔP(h) is timelike if m > 0. For m = 0, however, the light cone consists of two null rays, ĥ can vanish on one of them, and ΔP(h) is then null. Three consequences follow. First, the exact cost agrees with the standard collapse-model result. With σ² = r_C²/2, K_y is the GRW localization operator of width r_C, and part (a) gives 3ħ²/(4m r_C²) per hit, hence 3λħ²/(4m r_C²) per unit time at hit rate λ (compare Bassi et al. 2013). Second, part (b) is the exact case of the weak-collapse expansion N* = exp(−ad_A²/(8σ²)), with ad_A(B) = [A, B], which gives N*(H) − H = −(1/(8σ²)) [A, [A, H]] + O(σ^{−4}) whenever the series makes sense. The expansion terminates when [A, [A, H]] is a multiple of the identity. Third, the field cost grows as the collapse region shrinks. For a massless field and h_ℓ(x) = ℓ^{−4} χ(x/ℓ), substituting k → k/ℓ gives ΔP(h_ℓ) = ℓ^{−3} ΔP(χ) at fixed σ, while the vacuum variance of φ(h_ℓ) scales as ℓ^{−2}. At a fixed ratio ε of σ to the vacuum spread of φ(h_ℓ), the energy injected per collapse is therefore proportional to ħc/(ε² ℓ). Part (d) gives the corresponding local statement on a curved background. In the numerical checks (Appendix C.1, items C1 and C2), the Kraus integral and the Gaussian mixture of kicks both reproduce the multiplier exp(−(a − a′)²/(8σ²)), to 2.4 × 10⁻¹⁴ and 7.8 × 10⁻¹⁶. For a particle with an anharmonic potential, the energy increment is ħ²/(8mσ²) to twelve digits, in the ground state and in a random state alike. On a lattice field, the double-commutator and Fourier expressions agree to 5 × 10⁻¹⁶. For a massive field in 1 + 1 dimensions, under a boost of rapidity 0.5, ΔP transforms as a four-vector to 1.3 × 10⁻¹⁶. 13.9 Algebra Preservation: A Sufficient Condition for No-Signaling The proofs of Theorems 13.9(b) and 13.13(b) use the free field only to show that a certain algebra of observables is carried into itself by the operations between the one being tested and the targets. That structure can be isolated, and it does not depend on central commutators. Theorem 13.17 (No-signaling by algebra preservation). Let ω be a normal state on B(H), and let o_1, …, o_n be operations performed in this order, each a normal instrument I_m with a standard Borel outcome space (Ω_m, 𝔉_m). Write I_m*(Δ) for the Heisenberg dual of I_m(Δ) and N_m* = I_m*(Ω_m) for the dual of its unread channel. For a set X of target operations and Borel sets Δ_x, x ∈ X, the unconditioned joint probability of the target outcomes is P(Δ_x : x ∈ X) = ω(T_1*(T_2*(⋯ T_n*(1) ⋯))), where T_m* = I_m*(Δ_m) if m ∈ X and T_m* = N_m* otherwise, so that the outcomes of all non-target operations are summed over rather than conditioned on. Fix a non-target operation j, and suppose there is a linear subspace 𝔐 ⊆ B(H) containing 1 such that: (i) I_x*(Δ)(𝔐) ⊆ 𝔐 for every target operation x performed after j and every Δ; (ii) N_k*(𝔐) ⊆ 𝔐 for every non-target operation k ≠ j performed after j; (iii) N_j*(B) = B for every B ∈ 𝔐. Then the joint law of the target outcomes is the same whether j is performed, omitted, or replaced by any other operation satisfying (iii). Condition (iii) holds in particular whenever j has Kraus operators in the commutant 𝔐′, and operations performed before j are unrestricted. Proof. Let j be the m-th operation and set B = T_{m+1}*(⋯ T_n*(1) ⋯). Starting from 1 ∈ 𝔐 and applying the maps T_n*, …, T_{m+1}* in turn, conditions (i) and (ii) keep every intermediate operator in 𝔐, so B ∈ 𝔐. By (iii), T_j*(B) = N_j*(B) = B, which is the value obtained when j is omitted, and the same holds for any replacement satisfying (iii). The remaining maps T_{m−1}*, …, T_1* and the state ω are unchanged. If K_y ∈ 𝔐′ for almost every outcome y of j, then N_j*(B) = ∫ K_y† B K_y dν(y) = B ∫ K_y† K_y dν(y) = B. □ Corollary 13.18 (Random local automorphisms). Suppose that every unread non-target operation k performed after j is an average of automorphisms, N_k*(B) = ∫ U_k(t)* B U_k(t) dμ_k(t) with unitaries U_k(t) and a probability measure μ_k, and that 𝔐 is σ-weakly closed and mapped into itself by B ↦ U_k(t)* B U_k(t) for μ_k-almost every t. Then (ii) holds. For a free field, Gaussian collapses and random displacements are of this form with U_k(t) = e^{−itφ(h_k)} or W(h), each of which multiplies every Weyl operator by a phase. They therefore preserve the von Neumann algebra 𝔐_X generated by { W(g) : g ∈ V_X }, for every target set X, and Theorem 13.17 gives Theorem 13.13(b) for these operations. Proof. A weak integral of a bounded measurable family in a σ-weakly closed subspace lies in that subspace, since a normal functional that vanishes on the subspace vanishes on every member of the family and hence on the integral. □ The theorem locates the role of relativistic causality exactly. Microcausality supplies (iii): if 𝔐 is generated by observables localized in the target regions and j is localized in a region causally separated from all of them, the Kraus operators of j commute with 𝔐. It does not supply (ii), which is a condition on the composed dynamics of the intervening operations. Example 13.10 is a case in which (ii) fails. The unread Lüders instrument at C maps W(s f_B) to W(s f_B) G(φ(f_C)) for a non- constant function G, which lies outside the algebra generated by W(s f_B), and Alice can then signal to Bob. Microcausality alone therefore does not exclude Sorkin-type signaling. What must be controlled is closure of a target algebra under the composed intervening operations. The theorem also concerns unconditioned statistics only: conditioning on the outcome of an intervening operation is not covered, and in general it can reveal what was done at j. Its scope is that of a sufficient condition. It shows that one particular unread operation cannot influence the unconditioned target statistics; it does not cover protocols in which later operations are chosen according to earlier outcomes, and it is not a theorem about every relativistic signaling protocol. Its operational reading depends on identifying correctly where each operation is localized and which regions are causally separated, and for interacting fields its hypotheses must be verified for physically realizable instruments before it yields any conclusion. Nothing in Theorem 13.17 refers to free fields. For interacting fields, conjugation by e^{itA}, with A an interacting local observable, does not in general map a target algebra into itself, and the problem is to find nontrivial instruments for which (i)–(iii) hold. Instruments induced by coupling a probe to the field in a compact region are natural candidates, because their causal properties are controlled by the coupled dynamics (Fewster & Verch 2020). An operationally causal measurement scheme is not, however, a spontaneous collapse law, and the latter needs an additional physical mechanism. Target result 13.19 (Interacting probe instruments). For a scalar field with a λφ⁴/4! self- interaction, coupled to a probe in a compact region, compute the induced instrument to the lowest nontrivial order in λ. Check that the free limit reproduces §13.6. Then determine whether conditions (i)–(iii), or the causal factorization of the probe framework, hold at that order for composed operations, including an intermediate operation in Sorkin’s configuration, with a deliberately inadmissible instrument as a negative control. Such a calculation establishes a result to the order computed. It is not an existence theorem for interacting quantum field theory in four dimensions. 13.10 Randomly Located Collapses Corollary 13.14 fixes the possible collapse regions in advance. The natural next step is to let the law choose the locations, with a distribution that refers to no coordinates or foliation. This subsection carries out that step on a fixed globally hyperbolic background, proves what survives exactly, and identifies the two places where the construction meets obstructions. Definition 13.20 (Poisson collapse network). Fix the free field of §13.6 on a globally hyperbolic spacetime (M, g), in a quasi-free state such as the vacuum or another quasi-free Hadamard state. Fix a Borel set D ⊆ M of finite four-volume, an intensity λ > 0, a width σ > 0, and a smearing rule x ↦ h_x that assigns to each x ∈ D a real test function whose compact support O_x contains x, continuously in the topology of test functions. The collapse centres form a Poisson point process Ξ on D with intensity measure λ dVol_g: the numbers of centres in disjoint Borel sets are independent Poisson variables whose means are λ times the four-volumes of the sets (Last & Penrose 2017). A realized configuration ξ = {x_1, …, x_n} is admissible if the regions O_{x_1}, …, O_{x_n} are causally orderable in the sense of Definition 13.8. At each centre of an admissible configuration a Gaussian collapse of φ(h_{x_i}) with width σ occurs (Proposition 13.12), in any order compatible with the causal order of the regions. The realized history is the finite marked configuration {(x_i, y_i)} of centres and outcomes. The location of each collapse is fixed by the law, independently of the field state; only its outcome is drawn by the Born rule. A separate hit probability is unnecessary, since thinning a Poisson process independently with probability p yields a Poisson process of intensity pλ. Theorem 13.21 (Poisson collapse networks: what holds exactly). The construction of Definition 13.20 defines a normalized collapse dynamics conditionally on the admissible sector 𝒜. It does not define the dynamics on inadmissible configurations, so it is not an unconditional collapse law on the full Poisson configuration space. Within that scope: (a) Conditional foliation independence. For every admissible configuration ξ, the joint law Q_ξ of the outcomes is the same for every linear extension of the causal order of its regions, and it depends measurably on ξ. (b) Averaging over locations. On the event 𝒜 of admissible configurations, the joint law of centres and outcomes, Π(dξ) Q_ξ(dy) with Π the law of Ξ, is foliation independent, and so is the law conditioned on 𝒜. Because the centres are drawn independently of the field, conditioning on 𝒜 does not bias the outcomes: given an admissible ξ, they have the Born-rule law Q_ξ. (c) Conditional no-signaling. For an admissible ξ and any set Y of its collapses, the unconditioned joint law of the outcomes in Y is unchanged by any operation j whose Kraus operators commute with every W(g), g in the span of the h_y with y ∈ Y. This holds in particular for every Weyl-diagonal operation localized in a region causally separated from all the regions O_y, y ∈ Y. (d) Covariance of the law. Let ψ be a time-orientation-preserving isometry of (M, g) with ψ(D) = D, and suppose the smearing rule is ψ-covariant, h_{ψ(x)} = h_x ∘ ψ^{−1}. Then transforming the field state by ψ transforms the joint law of centres and outcomes by ψ. This is covariance of the law. A realized configuration is not ψ-invariant. (e) Incremental sampling of the locations. For disjoint Borel sets D_1, D_2 ⊆ D, the configurations Ξ ∩ D_1 and Ξ ∩ D_2 are independent, so the centres in each newly generated region can be drawn from that region alone, without reference to later regions. This concerns the locations only. It does not show that the quantum-state updates can be carried out generatively along a succession of cosmic-time presents, particularly when collapse regions straddle the level sets. That is a separate obligation, (E5) of §13.5, which §13.11 meets for one candidate ordering rule. Proof. (a) For a fixed admissible ξ, Theorem 13.13 applies, with the Gaussian collapses of Proposition 13.12 as its Weyl-diagonal operations. In the GNS representation of a quasi-free state, h ↦ W(h) is strongly continuous, so the Kraus operators, and with them the history probabilities, depend continuously on the centres. (b) Integrate (a) against Π over 𝒜. The conditional law of the outcomes given ξ is Q_ξ, because Ξ is independent of the field. (c) Apply Theorem 13.17 with 𝔐 the von Neumann algebra generated by the W(g), g in the span of { h_y : y ∈ Y }. Condition (i) holds because the Kraus operators of the target collapses lie in 𝔐, (ii) holds by Corollary 13.18, and (iii) holds by the commutation assumption. (d) An isometry preserves dVol_g, so by the mapping theorem for Poisson processes ψ(Ξ) has the law of Ξ. The free-field dynamics is covariant: the map W(f) ↦ W(f ∘ ψ^{−1}) preserves the Weyl relations, because E is invariant under isometries, and it carries the collapse at x to the collapse at ψ(x). (e) This is the complete independence property of Poisson processes. □ Theorem 13.21 settles the conditional question. Two questions remain: whether admissible configurations are typical, and whether the smearing rule can be chosen covariantly. The answer to both is negative, in a precise sense. Proposition 13.22 (Orderability fails in large volume). Suppose D contains N pairwise disjoint Borel cells C_1, …, C_N, each of four-volume at least v, such that O_x ∩ O_{x′} ≠ ∅ whenever x and x′ lie in a common cell. Then Pr(Ξ is admissible) ≤ [e^{−λv} (1 + λv)]^N. In Minkowski space, if every h_x is a translate of one test function whose support has nonempty interior, the cells can be taken as coordinate cubes of a fixed size. The probability that a configuration in a large box is admissible then decays exponentially with the box’s four-volume. Proof. Every region lies in its own causal future, so two overlapping regions each meet the causal future of the other, and the relation of Definition 13.8 has a cycle. An admissible configuration therefore has at most one centre in each cell. The cell counts are independent Poisson variables with means μ_i ≥ λv, and Pr(count ≤ 1) = e^{−μ}(1 + μ) is decreasing in μ. For translates, suppose the support contains a coordinate ball of radius r about some point c. If x and x′ lie in a cube of side r/2, then |x − x′| < r, so c + x lies in both translated supports. □ Proposition 13.23 (The order of interfering collapses matters). Let h_1 and h_2 be real test functions, σ_1, σ_2 > 0, and ρ a state in which φ(h_1) and φ(h_2) have finite variances V_1 and V_2. Apply Gaussian collapses of φ(h_1) and φ(h_2) to ρ in either order. If collapse 1 is applied first, the outcomes have variances V_1 + σ_1² and V_2 + σ_2² + E(h_1, h_2)²/(4σ_1²). If collapse 2 is applied first, the outcomes have variances V_1 + σ_1² + E(h_1, h_2)²/(4σ_2²) and V_2 + σ_2². Hence, whenever E(h_1, h_2) ≠ 0, the two orders give different joint laws, for every such state. Proof. Each outcome is the Born-rule value of the collapsed observable in the state at its collapse, plus independent Gaussian noise of variance σ_k² (Proposition 13.15(a)). If collapse 1 comes first, its unread effect on the second outcome is the random kick of Proposition 13.15(b). Since e^{itφ(h_1)} φ(h_2) e^{−itφ(h_1)} = φ(h_2) − t E(h_1, h_2), the distribution of φ(h_2) after the kick is that of its value in ρ shifted by −t E(h_1, h_2), with t independent of the state, of mean zero and of variance 1/(4σ_1²). The mean is unchanged and the variance grows by E(h_1, h_2)²/(4σ_1²). The other order is symmetric. □ Proposition 13.24 (No Lorentz-invariant law for random smearings). On four-dimensional Minkowski space, with a free field of mass m ≥ 0, or in any dimension with m > 0, let x be a point and μ a Borel probability measure on the set of real test functions h with Eh ≠ 0. Then μ is not invariant under the Lorentz transformations about x. In particular, no single test function with Eh ≠ 0 is invariant under them. Proof. By Proposition 13.16(c), and its analogue in other dimensions for m > 0, the unit vector u(h) = ΔP(h)/(ΔP(h) · ΔP(h))^{1/2} is a continuous, Lorentz-equivariant map into the hyperboloid H³ of future-directed unit timelike vectors, and it is unaffected by translating h. If μ were invariant, its image under u would be a Lorentz-invariant probability measure ν on H³. Choose R with ν(K) > 1/2, where K is the closed hyperbolic ball of radius R about a unit vector e. A boost of rapidity greater than 2R carries K onto the ball of radius R about a point at hyperbolic distance greater than 2R from e, which is disjoint from K. By invariance it also has measure greater than 1/2, so the total measure would exceed 1. □ The hypothesis on dimension and mass excludes one genuine exception. For a massless field in 1 + 1 dimensions, ΔP(h) is null whenever ĥ vanishes on one of the two null rays of the light cone, and the map u is then undefined; the proof does not apply to such smearings. The proposition concerns the class it states: point-centred smearings of a free field, drawn from a law attached to the centre. It does not exclude every conceivable Lorentz-invariant stochastic localization mechanism. Constructions with additional dynamical fields, different primitive ontologies, or event laws not of this form lie outside it, and routes (iv) and (vi) of Remark 13.25 are of that kind. What the obstructions teach. Randomizing the locations does not by itself yield a covariant spontaneous-collapse law. The construction holds exactly on every admissible configuration (Theorem 13.21). It fails in two precise places. First, admissibility is lost in large volumes (Proposition 13.22), and the order of interfering collapses then has physical consequences (Proposition 13.23). Second, no Lorentz-invariant law for the smearing of a free field at a centre exists (Proposition 13.24). A point process with a covariant law therefore does not supply covariant extended events. Each available repair adds structure at each event, and the structure should be named. Remark 13.25 (Repairs and their costs). (i) A background frame. Translates of a fixed smearing are covariant under translations, and under whatever rotations leave the smearing invariant, but not under boosts. By Campbell’s formula, the expected energy–momentum injected by the collapses centred in D is then λ Vol(D) ΔP(h). It is finite, and it is a fixed future-directed four-vector whose rest frame is a preferred frame for the energy injection. (ii) Conditioning on admissibility. By Proposition 13.22 this conditions on an event whose probability tends to zero as the domain grows, so it has no infinite-volume version without further work. On a finite domain the conditioned law has a forward form (Proposition 11.1), but that form depends on the extent of the domain still to be generated. (iii) Ordering conflicting collapses by a rule. Ordering them by the times of their centres in a chosen frame makes the law frame-dependent. In the numerical check, 234 of 300 sampled non- admissible configurations receive different laws in two frames of relative rapidity 0.6 (Appendix C.1, item C3(f)). A thinning rule applied in generative order, which keeps a candidate centre only if its region does not conflict with one already kept, is generable and produces admissible configurations by construction. But which collapses occur then depends on the generative order. (iv) Structure carried by realized events. In Tumulka’s rGRWf, the law for each flash refers to hyperboloids centred on the same particle’s previous flash (Tumulka 2006). The local frame is then supplied covariantly by an earlier realized event rather than by a background. For CTGP the law would depend on the location of a past event, so causal inheritance (C-g) requires that location to be physically instantiated in the present state; whether rGRWf admits such a representation is the open question noted in §13.12. (v) Cosmological structure. Where cosmological time T is regular, it is defined by the metric alone, so T ∘ ψ = T for every time-orientation-preserving isometry ψ. A smearing rule built only from T and g is then covariant under the entire isometry group. In an FLRW spacetime, for example, h_x can be the product of a function of T(p) − T(x) and a function of the distance from the comoving worldline through x, measured within the level set of p, with support small enough for that function to be smooth (Candidate extension 13.27). In FLRW spacetimes with a big-bang singularity the isometries fixing a point form a compact group, the rotations about the comoving worldline through it, so the obstruction of Proposition 13.24 does not arise. By symmetry, the stress–energy injected by a collapse then singles out the cosmic rest frame. Cosmological time could also fix the order of conflicting collapses covariantly. (vi) A mediating field. Bedingham (2011) obtains a Lorentz-covariant collapse dynamics free of the divergences of point-like collapse by letting the collapse act through an additional relativistic field. Whether a construction of this kind can be represented generably on CTGP’s hypersurfaces is open. (vii) State-dependent smearings would make the unread dynamics nonlinear in the state. Theorem 13.17, which concerns linear maps, would then no longer apply, and such rules are not pursued here. The CTGP-specific conclusion should be stated carefully. Route (v) uses only structure that CTGP already posits, and §13.11 shows that it addresses both obstructions consistently: the resulting dynamics is complete and normalized on every domain of finite four-volume, it can be generated forward along cosmological time in its free-field representation, and it admits no signaling between causally separated regions for the operations it covers. But it gives cosmological time a dynamical role. The collapse law, and in principle its observable consequences, would then refer to the generative foliation, which would change from empirically invisible, as in Corollary 13.5, to a physical hypothesis subject to test, the dynamical case of §14.6. The same holds for thinning in generative order in route (iii). Whether CTGP should accept such a role is a decision about the framework, not a result. Section 13.11 therefore develops route (v) as an explicitly labelled candidate extension, so that what it would assert and predict can be assessed, and does not adopt it. Without it, the remaining covariant options are routes (iv) and (vi) and a formulation of local collapse that does not attach an extended region to a point. None of these has yet been given for fields in a form that CTGP can adopt. The numerical checks (Appendix C.1, item C3) use a massless field in 1 + 1 dimensions, with causal diamonds of half-size 0.5 as regions. For these smearings the causal propagator can be computed exactly, and it vanishes exactly for causally separated regions. Poisson counts have mean 96.00 and variance 96.35 at expected value 96. At intensity 1.5, the fraction of admissible configurations falls from 0.599 to 0.015 as the box side grows from 1 to 2.5, below the bound of Proposition 13.22 throughout. Over 300 admissible configurations, with 1265 collapses and 1176 distinct linear extensions, the outcome covariances agree to 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁵. In direct Hilbert-space computations for three-collapse configurations, the history laws of different linear extensions agree to 4.8 × 10⁻¹⁶, and the characteristic function matches the closed form to 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁴. For two overlapping regions, the variance shift is E(h_1, h_2)²/(4σ_1²) to ten digits, and the two orders differ in total variation by 0.057. 13.11 A Candidate Extension: Collapse Guided by Cosmological Time This subsection develops route (v) of Remark 13.25 as a candidate extension of CTGP. It is not adopted. It gives cosmological time a role in the collapse law, which CTGP’s postulates do not contain, and accepting that role would be a decision about the framework (§14.6). The purpose is to state exactly what the extension would assert, what can be proved about it, what it would predict, and how far it can be realized by a physical coupling. Lemma 13.26 (Cosmological-time order and causal order). Let T be a time function, and suppose that every collapse region lies in a slab of cosmological time around its centre, O_x ⊆ { p : |T(p) − T(x)| ≤ τ }. (a) If T(x_j) − T(x_i) > 2τ, then O_i does not meet the causal future of O_j. Ordering collapses by the cosmological time of their centres therefore agrees with causal precedence for every pair whose centres differ in cosmological time by more than 2τ. (b) In Minkowski space with T = t, suppose the regions are translates of one compact convex region that is symmetric under time reflection about its centre. If O_j meets the causal future of O_i but O_i does not meet the causal future of O_j, then t(x_i) < t(x_j). Consequently, on every admissible configuration, ordering by the times of the centres is a linear extension of the causal order. Proof. (a) T increases strictly along future-directed causal curves, and every point of O_i has T ≤ T(x_i) + τ < T(x_j) − τ, which is at most the value of T at every point of O_j. (b) Write vectors as a = (a⁰, a^s), with a^s the spatial part, and let O_k = O + x_k, d = x_j − x_i, and D = O − O. Then O_j meets the causal future of O_i exactly when d ∈ J⁺ + D, with J⁺ the closed future cone, and O_i meets the causal future of O_j exactly when −d ∈ J⁺ + D. The set D is convex and invariant under a ↦ −a, and, because O is symmetric under time reflection, it is also invariant under (a⁰, a^s) ↦ (−a⁰, a^s). Suppose d⁰ ≤ 0 and d = k + w with k ∈ J⁺ and w ∈ D. Then w⁰ = d⁰ − k⁰ ≤ d⁰ ≤ 0. The set D contains (−w⁰, −w^s) and (w⁰, −w^s), and hence, by convexity, w′ = (w⁰ − 2d⁰, −w^s), because w⁰ ≤ w⁰ − 2d⁰ ≤ −w⁰. Then −d − w′ = (k⁰, −k^s), which lies in J⁺, so −d ∈ J⁺ + D and the relation is mutual. A one-way relation therefore forces d⁰ > 0. On an admissible configuration every relation between regions is one-way, and the causal order is the transitive closure of these relations, so it increases the time of the centres. □ Candidate extension 13.27 (Collapse guided by cosmological time; not adopted). Let (M, g) be an FLRW spacetime with a big-bang singularity, so that its cosmological time T is regular, coincides with the FLRW time, is smooth, and has Cauchy surfaces as level sets (§13.1), and let φ be the free field of §13.6. The candidate is stated for this class only. For other spacetimes with regular cosmological time, a smooth smearing law built from T and g alone would first have to be specified. (i) Locations. Collapse centres form a Poisson process with intensity λ dVol_g in the region T > t_0, as in Definition 13.20, where t_0 > τ is fixed by condition (ii). If the universe recollapses at a finite cosmological time T_max, the region is t_0 < T < T_max − τ, so that every collapse region lies in the spacetime. (ii) Smearing law. Each smearing h_x is built from T and g alone and is supported in the slab |T − T(x)| ≤ τ: h_x(p) = c χ((T(p) − T(x))/τ) ψ₀(d_x(p)²/ℓ²), where d_x(p) is the proper distance, within the level set of p, from the comoving worldline through x, χ and ψ₀ are fixed smooth functions with ψ₀ supported in [0, 1), and c is a constant. The radius ℓ and the earliest time t_0 of item (i) are chosen so that, for every centre with T(x) > t_0, the slab stays away from the big bang and the support lies within a normal neighbourhood of the worldline in every level set it meets, that is, inside the injectivity radius of those level sets, which is infinite for flat and open FLRW slices and equals π times the curvature radius for closed ones. There d_x² is smooth, so h_x is a smooth test function of compact support; the distance itself can fail to be smooth at cut points, which this choice excludes. (iii) Ordering rule. Collapses are applied in order of increasing T(x); ties have probability zero. (iv) Update. Each collapse is the Gaussian collapse of φ(h_x) with width σ (Proposition 13.12). The parameters are λ, τ, ℓ, σ, c, and t_0. Proposition 13.28 (Properties of the candidate extension). (a) Completeness. Every finite configuration of centres, admissible or not, receives a normalized joint law of outcomes. The candidate therefore defines an unconditional collapse dynamics on the full Poisson configuration space of any domain of finite four-volume. (b) Agreement with the causal-order dynamics. The ordering rule agrees with causal precedence for every pair of collapses whose centres differ in cosmological time by more than 2τ. In the flat local model of Lemma 13.26(b), the law coincides with that of Theorem 13.21 on every admissible configuration, and the candidate extends it to the remaining configurations. (c) No signaling for the covered operations. In any finite configuration, let Y be a set of collapses, and let j be an operation, inserted at any position in the sequence, whose Kraus operators commute with every W(g), g in the span of the h_y with y ∈ Y, the other operations being the law’s collapses or Weyl-diagonal operations. Then the unconditioned joint law of the outcomes in Y does not depend on whether j is performed. This holds in particular when j is localized in a region causally separated from every O_y. (d) Forward generation in the free-field representation. The collapse at x is a function of φ(h_x), which equals the symplectic product of Eh_x with the Cauchy data of the field on the level set T = T(x). It can therefore be applied when the generative present reaches T(x), and the centres in each successive slab of cosmological time are drawn from that slab alone. The process is generated forward along cosmological time from the present field state and the realized events, with no future input. This is forward generation of the mathematical description; it is not a fully local dynamical implementation, because the support of h_x extends to T(x) + τ. (e) Preferred-frame structure. Each collapse changes the expected renormalized stress–energy tensor by T^cl[Eh_x]/(4σ²) (Proposition 13.16(d)), which is invariant under the rotations about the comoving worldline through x. In the flat local model with rotation-invariant smearings, the mean four-momentum injected per unit four-volume is λ ΔP(h), a fixed timelike vector at rest in the cosmic frame. This is a property of field expectation values and a theoretical prediction of the free-field model, not by itself a measurable signature (see the discussion of measurable signatures below). The order that the rule assigns to law collapses among themselves, by contrast, affects only the joint law of their outcomes: their unread channels commute, so reordering law collapses among themselves, without changing their positions relative to other operations, leaves every unconditioned statistic of those other operations unchanged. For two collapses with E(h_i, h_j) ≠ 0 whose centres differ in cosmological time by at most 2τ, the joint law of the outcomes depends on which centre is earlier in cosmological time (Proposition 13.23). When such centres are spacelike-separated, different inertial frames would order them differently, so the ordering rule produces cosmic-frame-dependent correlations among collapse outcomes. (f) Infinite histories when past regions are finite. Suppose that every past region D_t = { p : t_0 < T(p) ≤ t } has finite four-volume, as in spatially closed FLRW spacetimes; every level set of T has four-volume zero. Then almost surely only finitely many centres lie in each D_t and no two share a value of T, so the centres form a sequence x_1, x_2, … ordered by T, which is finite if the region of centres has finite four-volume and infinite otherwise. There is a unique probability measure on the corresponding histories of marked centres, finite or infinite, whose marginal on the centres and outcomes in each D_t is the law of part (a) for the domain D_t. This projective consistency holds for the nested past regions D_t. It does not hold for an arbitrary subdomain D of finite four-volume: collapses outside D that precede some of those in D change the state before them, so the marginal of the global law on D differs in general from the law of part (a) for D, which applies only the collapses in D. If these regions have infinite four- volume, as in spatially infinite FLRW spacetimes, every slab of cosmological time contains infinitely many centres almost surely, so its collapses cannot be applied one after another in order of T, and the existence of a global process is open. Proof. (a) A finite sequence of normalized instruments composes to a normalized instrument. (b) Lemma 13.26 and Theorem 13.21(a). (c) Theorem 13.17 with Corollary 13.18; the conditions of the theorem do not depend on how the order of the operations was chosen. (d) For a free field on a globally hyperbolic spacetime, φ(h) = ∫_Σ ((Eh) ∇_nφ − φ ∇_n(Eh)) dΣ for every Cauchy surface Σ, up to a sign fixed by the convention for E (Wald 1994), and the level sets of T are Cauchy surfaces by assumption. The statement about the centres is Theorem 13.21(e). (e) Proposition 13.16(c) and (d), the rotational symmetry of h_x, and Campbell’s formula give the energy–momentum. The unread channels commute because each multiplies every Weyl operator by a number, so their duals commute on the Weyl operators and, being normal, on the von Neumann algebra those operators generate, which contains every observable of the field. The order dependence of the joint outcome law is Proposition 13.23. (f) Since Vol_g(D_t) is finite, the Poisson count in D_t is finite almost surely, and since level sets have four-volume zero, ties in T have probability zero. Given the past, the next centre is the first Poisson point above the current value of T, by the complete independence of the Poisson process, or there is no further centre, an outcome of probability exp(−λ v(t′)) at the current value t′, where v(t′) is the four-volume of the part of the region of centres with T > t′; this probability is positive when v(t′) is finite. Including that terminal state, both the kernel for the next centre and the Born-rule kernel for the next outcome are measurable on standard Borel spaces, and the Ionescu-Tulcea theorem gives a unique measure on histories with these kernels. Its marginal on D_t is the law of part (a) for D_t, because every collapse in D_t precedes every collapse outside it, and the later collapses, with their outcomes summed over, are trace-preserving. For an arbitrary subdomain this argument fails at its first step, since collapses outside the subdomain can precede collapses inside it. A Poisson process of infinite intensity measure on a slab has infinitely many points there almost surely. □ In the numerical checks (Appendix C.1, items C3(g) and C3(h)), ordering by the times of the centres is a linear extension of the causal order in all 300 admissible configurations of item C3(c), as Lemma 13.26(b) requires for causal diamonds. In a Sorkin-type configuration whose two intermediate law collapses overlap, so that it is not admissible, applying the collapses in order of time leaves the target’s binned law unchanged, to within 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁵, when a kick, a Gaussian collapse, or an unsharp Lüders instrument, which is not Weyl-diagonal, is applied in a region causally separated from the target. If an unsharp Lüders instrument replaces the first intermediate collapse, the kick shifts the target law by 8.1 × 10⁻⁴. In the tested configuration, the cosmological-time ordering rule produces no signaling for the specified interventions. The Lüders control shows that this conclusion cannot be extended to arbitrary intervening instruments. In item C3(e), the unread channels of two overlapping collapses give the same final state in either order, to 6.8 × 10⁻¹⁵, although the joint laws of their outcomes differ. Four limits of Proposition 13.28 should be stated. First, part (d) establishes computability from present Cauchy data, not physical causal locality. A smeared free-field observable can always be rewritten through the causal propagator as an observable on a single Cauchy surface, but the collapse of φ(h_x) concerns the field smeared over a region that extends to cosmological time T(x) + τ, so its physical localization is extended in time, and a fully local dynamical implementation of the centre-time rule, for instance through a coupled field, has not been given; Propositions 13.29 and 13.31 below examine two alternatives. Second, part (c) covers the stated class of operations. It does not show that arbitrary physically implementable interventions can be inserted without detectable effects of the ordering rule: an intervention whose region interferes, within 2τ in cosmological time, with a law collapse is ordered relative to it by the rule, and the joint statistics can then depend on that order, as in Proposition 13.23. In the realization of Proposition 13.31, where the law’s collapses and the interventions are all realized by gapless pointers, no rule orders them, and this limit does not arise. Third, the correlation structure of part (e) is a statement about the collapse outcomes, which CTGP treats as primitive occurrences. It shows that the event statistics depend on the cosmic frame; it does not by itself show measurable Lorentz violation. By part (e), no unconditioned statistic of other operations reveals the order of law collapses among themselves, so whether these correlations induce operationally detectable signatures requires an explicit account of how collapse outcomes are recorded in accessible observables. The candidate preferred-frame effects identified so far are the energy–momentum injection of part (e) and the order effects for interfering interventions described under the second limit; neither has yet been shown to be measurable, as explained below. Fourth, no-signaling and Lorentz invariance are different properties. Four notions must be kept apart: a Lorentz-invariant law, which refers to no frame; a covariant formulation with additional background structure, here the cosmological time, which is covariant under all isometries but not Lorentz-invariant on scales where that structure matters; preferred-frame effects, which are physical consequences of such structure that single out the background frame, such as the energy–momentum injection of part (e), and which amount to measurable Lorentz violation only once an operational prediction for them is given; and superluminal signaling, which is excluded for the operations of part (c). The candidate contains preferred-frame structure and satisfies operational no-signaling for the class of interventions covered by Proposition 13.28(c). Whether a complete physical implementation remains non-signaling under arbitrary admissible interventions is open, and so is the quantitative question whether its preferred-frame effects are experimentally viable. Predicted preferred-frame effects and measurable signatures. The energy–momentum statement of Proposition 13.28(e) is a theoretical prediction of the free-field model about expectation values: per unit four-volume, the collapses inject on average the four-momentum λΔP(h), which is at rest in the cosmic frame. In a laboratory moving with velocity β, in units of c, relative to that frame, this four-vector has energy component γλΔP⁰ and momentum component −γλΔP⁰ β, so the injected momentum is a fraction |β| of the injected energy and points against the laboratory’s motion. If the cosmic frame is identified with the rest frame of the cosmic microwave background, relative to which the Solar System moves at about 370 km/s (Planck Collaboration 2020), then |β| ≈ 1.2 × 10⁻³. This is a predicted preferred-frame effect. It is not a demonstrated measurable Lorentz-violation signature, for four reasons. First, the prediction concerns a free scalar field, whereas an apparatus is made of matter, for which no collapse law has been given (L2, D7). Second, an operational prediction needs an account of how the apparatus couples to the field, so that the injected energy–momentum appears in a recorded quantity: a heating rate of a sample, a stress–energy response, or a directional effect, such as a mean momentum transfer aligned with the velocity of the cosmic frame, which an Earth-fixed apparatus would see modulated with the sidereal day. Third, the size of any such effect depends on λ, σ, τ, ℓ, and c, which are not fixed. Fourth, any predicted signal must be compared with the experimental bounds on collapse-induced heating (Bassi et al. 2013) and on preferred-frame effects (Kostelecký & Russell 2011). Until these are supplied, the energy–momentum injection is a stated prediction of the candidate, not an established observable consequence, and the same holds for the other effects named in the third limit. Timing and physical realization. The first limit raises the question whether the collapses can be timed or realized differently. Three implementations can be compared. Centre-time ordering, rule (iii) of Candidate extension 13.27, applies each collapse when the generative present reaches the centre of its region. Completion-time ordering applies it only when the whole region lies in the generated past. A physical realization replaces each postulated instrument by the reading of a probe coupled locally to the field and derives the instrument from the coupled dynamics. Propositions 13.29 and 13.31 state what holds for the second and the third. Proposition 13.29 (Completion-time ordering). In Candidate extension 13.27, replace rule (iii) by the rule that the collapse at x is applied when the generative present reaches T(x) + τ, the largest value of T on its support, collapses being applied in order of increasing T(x) + τ. (a) Same law. Because every smearing has the same half-width τ, the completion-time order of every configuration is its centre-time order. The two rules therefore define the same joint law of centres and outcomes on every domain of finite four-volume, and the same measure on infinite histories in the setting of Proposition 13.28(f). (b) Surviving properties. Parts (a), (b), (c), (e), and (f) of Proposition 13.28 hold for the completion-time rule, with the same proofs. (c) Forward generation from the generated past. When the collapse at x is applied, its support lies in the region T ≤ T(x) + τ, which has already been generated, and φ(h_x) is a function of the Cauchy data of the field on the level set T = T(x) + τ within the compact set in which that level set meets the causal future of O_x. (d) Varying half-widths. If the half-width varied from centre to centre, the two rules would order some configurations differently. Lemma 13.26(b), which concerns translates of a single region, would then apply to neither rule, and part (b) of Proposition 13.28 would need a separate proof for whichever rule was used. Proof. (a) T(x) + τ < T(x′) + τ exactly when T(x) < T(x′), so the two rules apply the same instruments in the same order. (b) This follows from (a). (c) Every point of O_x has T ≤ T(x) + τ. Let Σ be the level set T = T(x) + τ. Since T increases strictly along future-directed causal curves, a point of Σ in the causal past of O_x lies in O_x. The solution Eh_x vanishes outside the union of the causal past and the causal future of O_x, so on Σ it vanishes outside the intersection of Σ with the causal future of O_x, which is compact because Σ is a Cauchy surface and O_x is compact. The formula for φ(h_x) in the proof of Proposition 13.28(d) then involves only the Cauchy data in that set. (d) With half-widths τ_x that depend on the centre, the order of the values T(x) + τ_x differs in general from the order of the values T(x). □ Completion-time ordering thus changes when, in the generative succession, each collapse is applied, and it changes no probability. It removes the feature that a collapse is applied before part of its region has been generated. It does not supply a mechanism: the collapse is still an instantaneous update of the state on one level set by a function of field data in a region whose size is set by ℓ and τ, and its outcome refers to a field configuration that by then lies in the generated past. Gapless pointers. Section 13.6 derives a two-outcome instrument from a gapless two-level detector. The Gaussian collapse is obtained in the same way from a pointer with a continuous reading, in the manner of von Neumann’s measurement model (see Wiseman & Milburn 2010). Write the causal propagator as E(f, h) = E_ret(f, h) − E_ret(h, f), where E_ret(f, h) = ±∫ f (G_ret h) dVol_g, G_ret is the retarded fundamental solution of the field equation, and the sign is fixed by the convention for E. Then E_ret(f, h) = 0 unless the support of f meets the causal future of the support of h. For a finite configuration of centres x_1, …, x_n with smearings h_k = h_{x_k} and regions O_k, set Γ_kl = E_ret(h_k, h_l). Thus E(h_k, h_l) = Γ_kl − Γ_lk, and Γ_kl = 0 unless O_k meets the causal future of O_l; the diagonal entries Γ_kk are in general nonzero. Attach to centre k a pointer, one canonical degree of freedom with [q_k, p_k] = i and no Hamiltonian of its own, coupled to the field through the interaction density p_k h_k(x) φ(x), and let its reading be a value of q_k obtained after its coupling has ended. Proposition 13.31 gives the Born-rule statistics of the readings; whether the readings are definite events is a separate question, taken up after its proof. Let η_k(q) = (2πσ_k²)^{−1/4} exp(−q²/(4σ_k²)). The pointer is pre-chirped if it is prepared in the state exp(iΓ_kk p_k²/2) η_k, a Gaussian state whose position and momentum are correlated so as to cancel the pointer’s back-reaction on its own reading. The following lemma supplies the geometric input for infinite configurations of centres. Lemma 13.30 (Finitely many causal antecedents). Let (M, g) be an FLRW spacetime with a big-bang singularity, M = (0, T_max) × S with T_max ≤ ∞ and g = dT² − a(T)² γ, where a is smooth and positive and (S, γ) is a complete Riemannian three-manifold of constant curvature, so that T is the cosmological time. Write π(p) ∈ S for the comoving position of a point p. Fix τ > 0, ℓ > 0, and t_0 > τ, and let the region of centres be the set of points x with t_0 < T(x) < T_max − τ, the upper bound being absent if T_max = ∞. For such x, let R_x be the set of points p with |T(p) − T(x)| ≤ τ and a(T(p)) d_γ(π(p), π(x)) ≤ ℓ, which lies in M and contains the support of the smearing h_x of Candidate extension 13.27(ii). (a) For every compact set K ⊂ M, the centres x whose sets R_x meet the causal past J⁻(K) lie in a compact subset of M, which has finite four-volume. (b) Let the centres form a Poisson process with intensity λ dVol_g on the region of centres. Almost surely every compact set contains only finitely many centres, and then, for every finite set S of centres, the set S⁻ of centres whose regions meet the causal past of the union of the regions O_k, k ∈ S, is finite. Proof. (a) Let t_K be the largest value of T on K. The level set Σ = { T = t_0 − τ } is a Cauchy surface, because t_0 − τ > 0, and in a globally hyperbolic spacetime the intersection of the causal past of a compact set with the causal future of a Cauchy surface is compact. Hence A = J⁻(K) ∩ { T ≥ t_0 − τ } is compact, and so is its projection π(A) ⊆ S. Suppose that R_x meets J⁻(K) at a point p. Then T(p) ≥ T(x) − τ > t_0 − τ, so p ∈ A, and T(x) ≤ T(p) + τ ≤ t_K + τ, because T increases along future-directed causal curves. The value T(p) lies in the compact interval [t_0 − τ, t_K], on which a has a positive minimum a_*, so the condition a(T(p)) d_γ(π(p), π(x)) ≤ ℓ gives d_γ(π(x), π(A)) ≤ ℓ/a_*. The set N of points of S within γ-distance ℓ/a_* of π(A) is closed and bounded, hence compact by the Hopf–Rinow theorem. Therefore x lies in the compact set [t_0, t_1] × N, where t_1 is the smaller of t_K + τ and T_max − τ, and a compact set has finite four-volume. (b) The intensity measure is finite on compact sets, so the number of centres in a compact set is a Poisson variable with finite mean and is finite almost surely. Exhausting M by countably many compact sets shows that, almost surely, every compact set contains finitely many centres. For a finite set S of centres, the union K of the regions O_k ⊆ R_{x_k}, k ∈ S, is compact, and S⁻ lies in the compact set of part (a), which then contains finitely many centres. □ The lemma uses the FLRW structure: the product form of M, the positive minimum of the scale factor on compact time intervals, and the completeness of the spatial sections. It is not claimed for arbitrary globally hyperbolic spacetimes. Proposition 13.31 (Collapse by gapless pointers). Let φ be the free field of §13.6 on a globally hyperbolic spacetime, in a state ω, and consider any finite configuration of centres, admissible or not, with pointers as just described. (a) Foliation-independent coupled evolution. Ordered by any Cauchy time function, the coupled evolution from before all couplings to after them is the same unitary, U = exp(−(i/2) Σ_{k,l} Γ_kl p_k p_l) exp(−i Σ_k p_k φ(h_k)). After the coupling, the pointer positions are U† q_k U = q_k + φ(h_k) + Σ_l Γ_kl p_l, where, as in U, φ is the field of the uncoupled theory. These operators commute with one another. Each q_k commutes with the interaction after the coupling of pointer k has ended. The joint law of the readings, and the field state conditional on them, therefore do not depend on when or in what order the pointers are read, provided that each is read after its own coupling has ended. (b) Outcome law. For pre-chirped pointers, the readings y_1, …, y_n have the characteristic function χ(s), the expectation of exp(i Σ_k s_k y_k) for s ∈ ℝⁿ, given by χ(s) = ω(W(Σ_k s_k h_k)) exp(−½ Σ_k σ_k² s_k² − ½ Σ_l b_l² / (4σ_l²)), with b_l = Σ_{k≠l} s_k Γ_kl. In a quasi-free state with vanishing one-point function, the readings are jointly Gaussian with covariance Σ + diag(σ_k²) + Σ_l g_l g_lᵀ / (4σ_l²), where Σ is the covariance of the smeared fields, (g_l)_k = Γ_kl for k ≠ l, and (g_l)_l = 0. Without the pre-chirp, (g_l)_l = Γ_ll, so the readings acquire further terms from each pointer’s back- reaction on its own reading. (c) Unread channel. With all readings summed over, the field undergoes the composition, in any order, of the unread Gaussian collapses of φ(h_1), …, φ(h_n) with widths σ_1, …, σ_n (Proposition 13.15), with or without the pre-chirp and for every configuration. The energy–momentum and the stress–energy injected are therefore those of Proposition 13.16, added over the collapses. (d) Agreement with sequential collapse. If the configuration is admissible, then for pre-chirped pointers the joint law of the readings, together with the conditional field state, is that of the Gaussian collapses of φ(h_k) with widths σ_k (Proposition 13.12) applied in any linear extension of the causal order; in particular, in a quasi-free state, the law of the readings is the law Q_ξ of Theorem 13.21. If the configuration is not admissible, Γ_kl and Γ_lk can both be nonzero, and the law of part (b) then differs in general from the law of the collapses applied in order of their centres. (e) No signaling without an ordering rule. For a set S of pointers, let S⁻ be the set of pointers whose regions meet the causal past of some O_k with k ∈ S. The joint law of the readings in S depends only on the values ω(W(g)) for g in the span of the h_k with k ∈ S, and on the smearings and states of the pointers in S and in S⁻. It is therefore unchanged by adding, removing, re-preparing, or reading any further pointer, or by coupling the field to a classical source, which acts as a kick, provided that its region does not meet the causal past of any O_k with k ∈ S, in particular when that region is causally separated from all of them. This holds for every configuration, admissible or not, including Sorkin’s. Part (e) concerns the modeled operations, namely gapless pointers and classical sources. It is not a statement about arbitrary interventions, such as operations involving interacting matter, probes with internal dynamics, or measurements that such pointers do not realize. (f) A law of records for infinite configurations. Let the centres form the Poisson process of Candidate extension 13.27, with the smearings of its item (ii) and pre-chirped pointers of width σ, on any FLRW spacetime of that class, whether its spatial sections are closed, flat, or open, and let ω be quasi-free. Almost surely S⁻ is finite for every finite set S of centres, and there is a unique probability law on the readings of all centres whose marginal on every finite set S is the law of part (b) for the configuration S ∪ S⁻. Averaged over the Poisson process, it gives a law of marked centres. Neither an enumeration of the centres in order of T nor finiteness of the past regions is needed. This is a probability law for the readings, which are the outcome records. It is not a present quantum state containing infinitely many pointers, and no such state is constructed here. The law is compatible with a single-outcome reading, in which each record is one definite event, but it does not establish the physical generation of infinitely many actual events, one present after another, in an infinite volume; that infinite-volume generability is open. (g) Forward generation for finite configurations. For a finite configuration, let each pointer be prepared when the generative present reaches the smallest value of T on its region and read when it reaches the largest, which are T(x) − τ and T(x) + τ for the smearings of Candidate extension 13.27. Between successive presents, the field then evolves by its field equation with the local source Σ_k p_k h_k, each pointer interacts only within its region, and each reading concerns a single pointer whose coupling has ended. The present state at each value of T, consisting of the field and the pointers whose couplings have begun, conditioned on the readings already made, is a density operator, and the law of the next increment depends only on it. The readings and conditional states are those of parts (a)–(d). For the Poisson centres of Candidate extension 13.27 on a spatially closed FLRW spacetime, only finitely many pointers begin before any given value of T, almost surely, so the construction applies at every present. On spatially flat or open FLRW spacetimes, infinitely many pointers begin in every slab of cosmological time, and a complete stochastic evolution of a present state containing them is not constructed here; for these spacetimes part (f) supplies the law of the records only. Proof. (a) The coupling commutes with every p_k, so the momenta are conserved, and in their joint spectral representation the field is driven, for each value of the momenta, by the classical source j = Σ_k p_k h_k. For a free field driven by a classical source of compact support, the interaction- picture evolution through the support of the source is W(−j) times the phase exp(−(i/2) E_ret(j, j)). To see this, order the coupling by a Cauchy time function and split j into pieces j_1, …, j_N supported in successive thin slabs. The slab unitaries are W(−j_a) up to corrections that vanish as the slabs are refined, and by the first Weyl relation W(−j_N) ⋯ W(−j_1) = exp(−(i/2) Σ_{a>b} E(j_a, j_b)) W(−j). For a > b and slabs that do not overlap, the support of j_b does not meet the causal future of the support of j_a, so E_ret(j_b, j_a) = 0 and E(j_a, j_b) = E_ret(j_a, j_b). The phase therefore tends to exp(−(i/2) E_ret(j, j)) as the slabs are refined, the terms with a = b vanishing in the limit, and E_ret(j, j) = Σ_{k,l} Γ_kl p_k p_l does not refer to the time function. Since U is unitary and the q_k commute, so do the U† q_k U. To compute them, conjugate q_k first by the phase factor, which adds Σ_l Θ_kl p_l with Θ = (Γ + Γᵀ)/2, and then by exp(−i Σ_l p_l φ(h_l)), which adds φ(h_k) + ½ Σ_l E(h_k, h_l) p_l, the commutator series terminating because E is a number; and Θ_kl + ½ E(h_k, h_l) = Γ_kl. After the coupling of pointer k has ended, the interaction density no longer contains p_k, so it commutes with q_k, and measuring q_k then has the same effect as measuring it at the end. (b) Because exp(−iΓ_kk p_k²/2) q_k exp(iΓ_kk p_k²/2) = q_k − Γ_kk p_k, expectations in the pre-chirped states equal expectations in the states η_k with q_k replaced by q_k − Γ_kk p_k. The readings thus become q_k + φ(h_k) + Σ_{l≠k} Γ_kl p_l in the state ω ⊗ η_1 ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ η_n. Then Σ_k s_k y_k corresponds to φ(Σ_k s_k h_k) + Σ_l (s_l q_l + b_l p_l), a sum of commuting terms, and for the Gaussian η_l, ⟨exp(i(s q_l + b p_l))⟩ = exp(−½ (s² σ_l² + b²/(4σ_l²))). The covariance is the quadratic term in s of the logarithm. Without the pre-chirp, the term Γ_kk p_k remains in the k-th reading. (c) In the momentum representation of the pointers, U acts for each value of the momenta as a phase times exp(−i Σ_k p_k φ(h_k)), which is the product of the single kicks exp(−i p_k φ(h_k)) up to a phase. Tracing out the pointers integrates over the momenta with the density Π_k |η̂_k(p_k)|², where η̂_k is the momentum-space wave function of η_k; the pre-chirp does not change this density. Each factor is the kick density γ of Proposition 13.15(b) with σ = σ_k, so the unread channel is the composition of the unread Gaussian collapses, which commute (proof of Proposition 13.28(e)). Proposition 13.16 gives the energy–momentum. (d) A Gaussian collapse of φ(h_k) with width σ_k is the instrument obtained from an unchirped pointer by the instantaneous coupling V_k = exp(−i p_k φ(h_k)) followed by reading q_k: its Kraus operator for the reading y is ⟨y|V_k|η_k⟩ = η_k(y − φ(h_k)), the operator K_y of Proposition 13.12. For collapses applied in the order 1, …, n, the first Weyl relation gives V_n ⋯ V_1 = exp(−(i/2) Σ_{k>l} E(h_k, h_l) p_k p_l) exp(−i Σ_k p_k φ(h_k)). In a linear extension of the causal order of an admissible configuration, if l precedes k then O_l does not meet the causal future of O_k, so Γ_lk = 0 and Γ_kl = E(h_k, h_l). Hence Σ_{k,l} Γ_kl p_k p_l = Σ_k Γ_kk p_k² + Σ_{k>l} E(h_k, h_l) p_k p_l, and the diagonal part of the phase in U cancels the pre-chirp. U applied to the pre-chirped pointers therefore gives the same joint state of the field and the pointers as V_n ⋯ V_1 applied to the unchirped ones, and the readings and conditional field states agree; Theorem 13.21 identifies the law of the sequential collapses as Q_ξ. For the non- admissible case, item C4(d) of Appendix C.1 gives an example. (e) By (a), the readings in S are functions of q_k and φ(h_k) for k ∈ S and of the p_l with Γ_kl ≠ 0 for some k ∈ S, that is, of pointers in S and in S⁻. In the product state, the joint law of these commuting operators is fixed by the data listed. A classical source f adds E_ret(h_k, f) to the k-th reading, as a pointer with a definite momentum would, and this vanishes unless the support of f meets the causal past of O_k. Reading other pointers measures operators that commute with the readings in S, and summing over their outcomes leaves the law of S unchanged. (f) By Lemma 13.30(b), almost surely S⁻ is finite for every finite set S of centres. Given the configuration, the law of the readings in S is the same for every finite configuration containing S ∪ S⁻, by (e), because the further pointers have regions that do not meet the causal past of the regions in S. For S ⊆ S′, S⁻ ⊆ S′⁻, so the marginal on S of the law for S′ is the law for S. There are countably many centres, so the Kolmogorov extension theorem gives a unique law on the readings with these marginals. It depends measurably on the configuration, because the characteristic functions of part (b) depend continuously on the centres in a quasi-free state. (g) By (a), the coupled evolution may be computed slab by slab along T, and within a slab the source involves only the pointers whose regions meet that slab. Each pointer is read after its coupling has ended, so by (a) the readings and conditional states are those of the complete evolution. For finitely many centres, the field together with finitely many pointers has a density operator conditioned on the realized readings, and the next increment, namely evolution through the next slab, preparation of the pointers whose regions begin there, and reading of those whose regions end there, depends only on it. For the Poisson centres of Candidate extension 13.27, the centres whose regions begin in a new slab are those with T(x) − τ in it, and they can be drawn from that region alone (Theorem 13.21(e)). On a spatially closed FLRW spacetime, the centres with T(x) − τ below a given value lie in a set of finite four-volume, because the level sets are compact, so there are finitely many of them almost surely. □ Because the pointers have no Hamiltonian, their coupling densities at spacelike-separated points commute, as for the gapless detectors of §13.6, and part (e) holds without any rule for ordering overlapping couplings. Pointers with internal dynamics would require a separate analysis (compare de Ramón, Papageorgiou & Martín-Martínez 2021). In the numerical checks (Appendix C.1, item C4), the sums that give the phase of the time-ordered coupling agree exactly along two foliations of relative rapidity 0.6 and approach Γ as the grid is refined, with largest deviations 2.8 × 10⁻² and 9.0 × 10⁻³ at 32 and 64 nodes per axis. Explicit pointer computations reproduce the characteristic function of part (b) to 7.3 × 10⁻¹⁴ and the unread channel of part (c) to 1.7 × 10⁻¹⁴. For a pair of regions in which one meets the causal future of the other but not conversely, the pointer law equals that of sequential collapse in causal order to 1.8 × 10⁻¹³. For the overlapping pair of item C3(e), the pointer law differs from the law of collapses in centre-time order: the variances of the two readings are larger by 0.0011 and 0.0537. Statistics and definite outcomes. Proposition 13.31 derives the statistics of the readings from local coupling densities. It does not derive that the readings have definite values. The coupled evolution U is unitary and selects no outcome: it produces an entangled state of the field and the pointers in which every reading allowed by the Born-rule density is represented. A spontaneous- collapse reading of the construction therefore needs, in addition, a primitive single-outcome postulate for the pointers. (R) Reading postulate. When the coupling of a pointer ends, a reading event occurs. Its value y has the Born-rule probability density of q_k in the present state of the field and the pointers, conditioned on the readings already made, and the event replaces that state by the normalized conditional state, given y, of the field and the other pointers, together with the record y. The state after a reading can be stated exactly. Write the present state as a density operator ρ on L²(ℝ) ⊗ H_r = L²(ℝ, H_r), where L²(ℝ) carries the pointer being read in its position representation and H_r is the space of the field and the other present pointers. Then ρ = Σ_i w_i |Φ_i⟩⟨Φ_i| with weights w_i ≥ 0 summing to 1 and unit vectors Φ_i ∈ L²(ℝ, H_r), and for almost every y the operator ρ(y) = Σ_i w_i |Φ_i(y)⟩⟨Φ_i(y)| on H_r is positive and trace-class, with trace p(y), the Born-rule density of the reading, since Σ_i w_i ∫ ‖Φ_i(y)‖² dy = 1. For every such y with p(y) > 0, the post- reading state is the normalized density operator ρ(y)/p(y) on H_r together with the record y. The pointer that has been read does not enter this state as a quantum system: it takes no further part in the dynamics, and its idealized position eigenvector, which is not normalizable, is not needed. The present physical state after a reading is therefore well defined, as generability requires, for every reading outside a set of probability zero. Three achievements should be kept apart. (i) Deriving the Gaussian measurement statistics, the effective field instruments, and their causal properties from local coupling densities: established in the stated free-field model (Proposition 13.31(a)–(e)). (ii) Constructing a consistent probability law for the pointer records, including infinitely many centres: established under the stated assumptions (Lemma 13.30, Proposition 13.31(f)), as a law of records rather than a global present state; it does not establish infinite-volume generability. (iii) Deriving objectively definite, spontaneous pointer outcomes without a further postulate: not established. The local unitary coupling does not select a single outcome, and the construction relies on postulate (R) instead. Postulate (R) is of the same kind as the event law of the finite model (Definition 4.1) and as the collapse law of Candidate extension 13.27: in each, the occurrence of a definite event, with probabilities fixed by the law, is primitive. What the pointer variant changes is where the postulate acts. Candidate extension 13.27 postulates an instrument on the field; the pointer variant postulates readings of pointers and derives the instrument on the field. It thus relocates the collapse postulate from the field to the pointers; it does not remove it. For CTGP the postulate has three consequences. First, a reading event is a Tier 1 event (Definition 5.1): its occurrence, labelled by y, is primitive, and the truth profile past-tenses it. The pointer takes no further part in the dynamics and serves afterwards as the record of y, and the present physical state after the reading is the normalized conditional state of the field and the other pointers together with that record. Second, causal inheritance (C-g) and physical instantiation (C-f) require the pointers to belong to the present physical state: during its coupling a pointer is part of that state, and the law of its reading depends only on that state, so the variant is generable for finite configurations (Proposition 13.31(g)). Third, physical adequacy requires an account, not given here, of what the pointers are, why one exists at each Poisson centre without an experimenter, what prepares it in the pre-chirped state, and what physical system carries its record. Without such an account, the pointer variant is a measurement construction read as a spontaneous law, and it shifts the collapse problem from the field to the pointers rather than solving it. Which properties survive. Measured against the six parts of Proposition 13.28, the three implementations compare as follows. (a) Completeness. Centre-time ordering: proved. Completion-time ordering: unchanged, with the same proof. Gapless pointers: holds for every finite configuration, with a separate proof (Proposition 13.31(a)–(b)), and needs no ordering rule. (b) Agreement with the causal-order dynamics. Centre-time ordering: proved in the flat local model. Completion-time ordering: unchanged for a uniform half-width; a separate proof would be needed if the half-width varied. Gapless pointers: holds on every admissible configuration of any globally hyperbolic background, with a separate proof (Proposition 13.31(d)), provided that the pointers are pre-chirped; without the pre-chirp it fails by the self-terms of Proposition 13.31(b). (c) No signaling. Centre-time and completion-time ordering: proved for the covered operations. Gapless pointers: holds, with a separate proof, for every pointer or classical source whose region does not meet the causal past of the targets, on every configuration (Proposition 13.31(e)); it is not established for arbitrary interventions. The second limit above does not arise for such operations, since no rule orders them. (d) Forward generation. Centre-time ordering: forward computability from present Cauchy data, not a mechanism. Completion-time ordering: strengthened to computability from the generated past (Proposition 13.29(c)), still without a mechanism. Gapless pointers: replaced, for finite configurations and given postulate (R), by forward generation through a local coupling, each collapse being the reading of a pointer whose coupling has ended (Proposition 13.31(g)); on spatially flat or open FLRW spacetimes, a forward evolution of the present state is not constructed. (e) Preferred-frame structure. Centre-time and completion-time ordering: proved. Gapless pointers: the energy–momentum injection survives unchanged, because the unread channel is the same (Proposition 13.31(c)); the cosmic-frame dependence of the outcome correlations that comes from the ordering rule fails: the law of Proposition 13.31(b) contains no ordering rule, and its correlations depend on the cosmic frame only through the smearings. (f) Infinite histories. Centre-time and completion-time ordering: proved when the past regions have finite four-volume, and open otherwise. Gapless pointers: a law of the pointer records exists and is unique on every FLRW spacetime of the class, including spatially infinite ones, with a separate proof (Lemma 13.30, Proposition 13.31(f)); it is a law of records, not a global present state containing infinitely many pointers, and it does not establish the generation of those records in an infinite volume. What fails is the identification of the pointer law with the law of Candidate extension 13.27.
