On non-admissible configurations the two differ, so the gapless-pointer construction is a different candidate law, not a physical implementation of rule (iii). The gapless pointer, the natural local realization of a Gaussian collapse, does not reproduce centre-time ordering: it yields a law with no ordering rule. The pointer realization has further costs. First, as explained above, it adds physical degrees of freedom, one pointer at each Poisson centre prepared in a fixed state that depends on the smearing through Γ_kk, together with postulate (R). Second, cosmological time still enters the law, through the smearing law of Candidate extension 13.27(ii), which Proposition 13.24 makes necessary for point-centred smearings; only the ordering rule is removed. Third, the coupling is a prescribed function on spacetime, so the energy of Proposition 13.31(c) is supplied by the prescribed coupling, and no dynamical account of the energy budget is given. Fourth, each pointer is a single degree of freedom coupled throughout an extended region. It is a regulated probe, not a local probe field in the sense of Fewster and Verch (2020), and the results depend on the free field and on the absence of pointer dynamics. Fifth, the readings localize smeared field amplitudes, not matter (L2, D7). In Bedingham’s (2011) model, a mediating relativistic field attaches a quantized degree of freedom to each spacetime point; the pointers here attach one degree of freedom to each Poisson centre. The pointer realization is recorded as a further candidate, alongside Candidate extension 13.27, and is not adopted. Comparison with a covariant alternative. Routes (iv) and (vi) of Remark 13.25 avoid a preferred frame, and the two kinds of construction differ point by point. In frame, the candidate refers every collapse to the cosmic rest frame, a structure CTGP already posits, whereas rGRWf refers each flash to an earlier flash and Bedingham’s model works through an additional relativistic field. In scope, the candidate is defined for a free field on a fixed background, on every domain of finite four- volume and, when cosmological-time slabs have finite volume, on infinite histories (Proposition 13.28(f)), and in its gapless-pointer form, as a law of outcome records, on every FLRW spacetime of the class (Proposition 13.31(f)), whereas rGRWf is defined for a fixed number of non-interacting particles from given initial data. In generability, the candidate can be generated forward along cosmological time in its free-field representation (Proposition 13.28(d)), and in its gapless- pointer form, for finite configurations and given its reading postulate, through a local coupling (Proposition 13.31(g)), whereas for rGRWf causal inheritance requires the earlier flash locations to be physically instantiated in the present state, which is open (§13.12). In phenomenology, the candidate predicts, as theoretical consequences of the free-field model, energy injected at rest in the cosmic frame and, at the scales τ and ℓ, order effects for interfering interventions and cosmic- frame-dependent correlations among collapse outcomes. The last two arise from the ordering rule and are absent in the gapless-pointer form, whose outcome correlations depend on the cosmic frame only through the smearings. None of these effects has yet been turned into an operational prediction for an apparatus; any that become observable must respect the experimental bounds on collapse effects (Bassi et al. 2013) and on preferred-frame effects (Kostelecký & Russell 2011), whereas a fully covariant model predicts no such frame dependence. In localization, both leave open how matter, rather than a field amplitude, is localized (L2, D7). Adopting the candidate would change CTGP in one respect only: cosmological time would enter the collapse law, and the generative foliation would become empirically accessible in principle. In the gapless-pointer form it would enter only through the smearing law. That is a substantive commitment, whose consequences become testable once its predicted preferred-frame effects are turned into operational predictions. It is recorded here as a candidate, with its law, its variants, and its predicted signatures stated, and it is not adopted. 13.12 Candidate Relativistic Realizations Relativistic flash model. Tumulka’s relativistic GRW model (rGRWf) treats discrete spacetime flashes as objectively realized events, with a Lorentz-invariant law for their joint distribution, for a fixed number of non-interacting distinguishable particles (Tumulka 2006). Its law refers to hyperboloids centred on each particle’s previous flash, which is one way of supplying the local frame that, by Proposition 13.24, a point-centred law needs. It is the natural first test case. A continuous generative present can contain a locally finite set of discrete flash events, so discrete flashes do not imply discrete ontological time. The open questions are whether the flash law can be represented as a generable process on CTGP’s cosmic-time hypersurfaces, with a physically instantiated present state on each, and whether the construction extends to interacting fields, particle creation, renormalization, and curved backgrounds. A realistic interacting relativistic quantum field theory is a much harder target than a non-interacting model. Spontaneous events. The detector networks of §13.6 are a measurement model: their events are triggered by specified couplings. A spontaneous relativistic model would replace them by events prescribed by the law itself, with a conditional intensity λ(x | S_Σ) defined from the present state, together with the state update associated with each event. Such a law must be covariant across admissible hypersurface descriptions, well defined on a regulated field state space, causally admissible in the sense of (E3), normalized and free of pathological accumulation of events, and generable from the physically instantiated present state. This is the relativistic objective- collapse realization that CTGP contemplates. Section 13.7 constructs a regulated version, Gaussian collapse of smeared fields on a fixed family of regions (Corollary 13.14), and §13.10 lets a covariant Poisson law choose the locations (Theorem 13.21) and identifies the obstructions to a fully covariant version (Propositions 13.22–13.24). A covariant version that localizes matter, rather than linear field observables, remains open. Bohmian dynamics. A second route keeps a definite configuration rather than collapsing the quantum state: the present state is S_Σ = (Ψ_Σ, Q_Σ), with Q_Σ a particle or field configuration on the hypersurface, and the dynamics is a guidance law. Such a theory already contains one actual configuration and therefore one realized history. Relativistic Bohmian theories typically require a distinguished foliation (Dürr, Goldstein, Münch-Berndl & Zanghì 1999), and the foliation may itself be determined covariantly, for instance by the wave function (Dürr, Goldstein, Norsen, Struyve & Zanghì 2014). CTGP’s cosmological-time foliation is a natural candidate. It must be shown that the resulting predictions remain compatible with relativistic observations. Success in either route would mean that at least one mathematically consistent single-outcome relativistic quantum model can be equipped with CTGP’s ontology and its generability requirement without unacceptable observational effects. It would not mean that every relativistic quantum field theory or every interpretation is compatible with CTGP. 13.13 Eight Distinctions Any Extension Must Respect (D1) Geometric covariance versus physical foliation independence. A foliation defined covariantly from the metric is not arbitrary, but a quantum dynamics built around it may still produce physically detectable preferred-frame effects. Theorem 13.4 identifies, for finite event structures, one sufficient condition under which it does not: causal locality of the realized- event dynamics, relative to an order that must itself be shown to be the operationally relevant causal order. (D2) Ensemble consistency versus realized-history consistency. A covariant master equation does not by itself supply a covariant stochastic ontology (Proposition 13.2). (D3) Clock neutrality versus absence of ontological ordering. A constrained quantum theory can lack a preferred clock variable while still admitting an additional generative interpretation; whether that interpretation is consistent must be proved (§14). (D4) Conditional compatibility propositions versus completed existence theorems. The Tomonaga–Schwinger and continuum-limit results of CTGP – explained (Proposition 17.16 and Theorem 17.17) are conditional, and their operator-theoretic and stochastic assumptions require further justification before they can count as existence theorems. (D5) Selective versus unread updates. Operational no-signaling concerns the unconditioned statistics of target outcomes, with the outcomes of all other operations summed over. Conditioning on an intermediate outcome is a different question, and Theorem 13.17 does not address it. (D6) Covariance of a law versus invariance of a realization. A Poisson law for collapse centres can be covariant under every isometry, although no realized configuration is invariant, and a covariant law for locations does not by itself supply covariant extended events (Theorem 13.21(d), Proposition 13.24). Likewise, events triggered by the law rather than by an experimenter are spontaneous whether or not their locations have a covariant law. (D7) Localization of a field amplitude versus localization of matter. A collapse that localizes a smeared linear field observable does not thereby localize matter, and macroscopic localization is an independent obligation of physical adequacy (L2). (D8) Lorentz invariance, covariance with background structure, preferred-frame effects, and superluminal signaling. A law can be covariant under all isometries while depending on background structure such as cosmological time; such structure can produce preferred-frame effects, which are measurable only if an operational prediction shows how they reach a record; and a theory with preferred-frame effects need not permit superluminal signaling (§13.11). Each of the four must be established or excluded separately. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 14. Temporal Quantum Reference Frames ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 14.1 Clock-Neutral Descriptions A temporal quantum reference frame is not merely a coordinate clock. It is a quantum subsystem whose correlations with the rest define the evolution of another subsystem. In a constrained system, physical states satisfy Ĉ Ψ_phys = 0, and such a state is clock-neutral: it is not yet a description relative to any particular clock. Choosing clock A yields a conditional description ψ^{(A)}(τ_A) of the remaining degrees of freedom; choosing clock B yields ψ^{(B)}(τ_B). Höhn, Smith and Lock (2021) show, for a class of constrained systems whose constraint is a sum of clock and system Hamiltonians, that three formulations of relational dynamics are equivalent (clock-neutral relational observables, Page–Wootters conditional states, and quantum deparametrization), and they develop transformations between temporal frames. The spatial construction of Part II transfers only in part. Temporal reduction maps R_A and R_B can be defined, and where their inverses exist on the relevant sectors one can form V_{A→B} = R_B R_A^{−1}. For realistic clocks, however, these maps involve conditional states, clock POVMs, and sector restrictions, and they are not in general global unitaries of the kind used in Proposition 3.3. For ideal clocks they are unitary (Proposition 14.5). 14.2 Generation, Clock Readings, and Clock-Conditional Descriptions Definition 14.1 (Three temporal notions). In a CTGP model with clock subsystems: (i) The generative succession is the order of stages, or of event slots, in which the history is generated. (ii) A clock reading is the value of a clock observable of a physical subsystem at a stage. It is a Tier 3 proposition (§5.1) unless a recording event makes it a Tier 1 fact. (iii) A clock-conditional description is a description of the rest of the world conditioned on the readings of a chosen clock. CTGP identifies the ontological order of existence with (i) alone. A clock reading is physically instantiated information about a process, in the sense of the signal-based epistemology discussed in More Info, not automatically the fundamental ordering of existence. 14.3 A Worked Model With Imperfect Clocks Proposition 14.2 (Imperfect clocks in a generative model). Consider a model of the kind defined in Part II whose physical state includes one or more clock subsystems. (a) Clock-independence of event identity and probability. The measure over histories of Tier 1 events is fixed by the law and the complete initial state. Describing those events relative to one clock rather than another changes no event label, no stage, and no probability. (b) Periodic clocks. A clock of period M takes the same reading at stages σ_n and σ_{n+M}, so equal readings do not imply equal stages: τ_A(σ_1) = τ_A(σ_2) is compatible with σ_1 ≠ σ_2. A repeated reading means that the clock fails to distinguish two stages, not that generation has reversed. (c) Temporal delocalization. If a clock is in a superposition of readings at the stage of an event, the Tier 3 proposition “the clock read r when the event occurred” has no determinate value, although the event and its stage are determinate. For example, a counter prepared in an equal superposition of the readings 0 and 4 and advancing by one per stage reads n or n + 4, each with probability 1/2, at stage n. (d) Clock-relative order can disagree with generative order. If a non-ideal clock’s readings are recorded at successive events, the later event can carry an earlier reading. Consider a drifting quantum-walk clock on a ring of 32 sites, which advances one site per stage on average under the one-step propagator S exp(−i θ (S + S†)) with θ = 0.9. Because a ring has no intrinsic linear order, comparing two readings requires a convention: the displacement between consecutive readings r_1 and r_2 is the representative m of r_2 − r_1 modulo 32 in {−15, …, 16}, and the second reading counts as lower when m < 0 and as equal when m = 0. Assume that each event records the clock’s reading by a projective measurement of its position, so that the first recording leaves the clock in a definite position state, and that exactly one stage transition separates two consecutive recordings. Then, conditional on the first recorded reading, the exact probabilities on the 32-site ring are P(m < 0) = 0.104042655 and P(m = 0) = 0.338161965, to nine decimal places. They agree to better than 10⁻¹² with the infinite-lattice expressions (1 − J_0(1.8)² − 2 J_1(1.8)²)/2 and J_1(1.8)², where J_n are Bessel functions. By translation invariance these values do not depend on the first reading. Displacements of eight or more sites have total probability below 10⁻⁸, so any comparison convention that agrees with this one on displacements of fewer than eight sites gives the same values to that accuracy. The values do depend on the preparation and recording procedure: a clock not recorded at the first event, an unsharp recording, or a comparison across more than one transition gives different values. Proof. (a) Clock subsystems are part of the physical state, and the law takes the complete present state as input; the choice of a subsystem as clock for purposes of description is not an input to the law. If a clock interacts with the system, event probabilities depend on the clock’s physical state, but that is a dependence on the present physical state, not on the choice of description. (b) and (c) follow from the definitions. (d) The first recording projects the clock onto a position eigenstate |r⟩. During the single intervening transition the clock evolves by the stated propagator. By the Jacobi–Anger expansion, exp(−i θ (S + S†)) = Σ_{k∈ℤ} (−i)^k J_k(2θ) S^k, so on the infinite lattice the amplitude to move from r to r + m is, up to a phase, J_{m−1}(2θ). On the ring S^{32} = 1, and the amplitude for displacement m is the sum of the lattice amplitudes for all displacements congruent to m modulo 32. For m ∈ {−15, …, 16} the additional terms involve J_k(1.8) with |k| ≥ 16, each of magnitude below 10⁻¹⁴, so the ring probabilities differ from the lattice values J_{m−1}(1.8)² by less than 10⁻¹² in total. Summing the lattice values over m ≤ −1 gives Σ_{k≥2} J_k(2θ)² = (1 − J_0(2θ)² − 2 J_1(2θ)²)/2, using Σ_k J_k² = 1 and J_{−k}² = J_k², and m = 0 gives J_{−1}(2θ)² = J_1(2θ)². The exact ring values were computed directly from the 32-site propagator. The computation is item 16 of Appendix B. □ Proposition 14.2 has an external generative order built in: the stage index is a parameter of the model. It therefore shows that the three-way distinction of Definition 14.1 is consistent, and that clock-relative localization and ordering can differ from generative order without contradiction. It does not derive a definite generative order from clock-neutral data. That is the task of §§14.4–14.6. 14.4 A Proposed Temporal Identification Criterion Definition 14.3 (Temporal cross-frame identification; a proposal). A clock-relative description consists of a valid clock reduction, which may be partial, state-dependent, or defined only on a subspace, together with its domain of physical states. Two clock-relative descriptions identify the same CTGP history when (i) the physical state lies in both domains, (ii) they arise from the same realized event history, and (iii) they agree on every clock-neutral event proposition, that is, every Tier 1 proposition stated without reference to clock readings, that can be evaluated in both. A family of clock-relative descriptions is consistent when every pair satisfies (i)–(iii) on the intersection of their domains, and when, wherever three descriptions share a domain, the correspondences between events that they induce compose. The criterion deliberately does not require that two clocks assign the same time coordinates to events, nor that their conditional state vectors coincide. It is the temporal counterpart of Criterion 5.5, with clock-neutral event propositions in place of the translated truth profile. Criterion 5.5 identifies descriptions through a single global orbit, which presupposes globally defined unitary reductions. Definition 14.3 replaces that orbit by a family of overlapping admissible descriptions with consistency conditions on their common domains, which is what is needed when temporal reductions are partial, state-dependent, or available only locally (§14.1). Conjecture 14.4 (Clock-neutral realized-history equivalence). For a specified class of constrained systems with two admissible quantum clocks, possibly imperfect, and a clock-neutral normalized event instrument whose interventions are represented within the constraint, all valid clock reductions assign the same probabilities to corresponding finite realized-event histories. Moreover, on the domains of the reductions, the clock-neutral instrument intertwines with clock-relative instruments, R_A ∘ ℐ^phys = ℐ^A ∘ R_A and R_B ∘ ℐ^phys = ℐ^B ∘ R_B, for compositions of events as well as single events. This holds although the two descriptions can assign different temporal localizations, and different orderings, to the events. A statement of the conjecture for a given class must specify the domains of the reductions and what makes events in two descriptions correspond. Agreement of probabilities is not enough, because one physical state supports many instrument decompositions (Proposition 13.2). The conjecture therefore concerns a clock-neutral instrument, not merely a clock-neutral effect. A counterexample is possible: a clock-relative description may admit no globally valid ordering of the events. Section 14.5 proves it for one family of instruments, late-record projective measurements, in a model with two ideal clocks. A proof for a broader class would establish a realized-history measure shared across clocks. It would not show that the events are generated in a fundamental succession, which requires an ordering compatible with the constraint and its gauge symmetries (§14.6). 14.5 Two Ideal Clocks With the Interventions in the Constraint The construction of this subsection removes the external stage index of Proposition 14.2. The interventions are part of the constraint, and the events, their probabilities, and their instruments are defined on the physical Hilbert space. Setting. Clocks A and B are ideal: each has Hilbert space L²(ℝ) and a canonical pair (T, P) with [T, P] = i (ħ = 1). The system and its records have a finite-dimensional Hilbert space K and a Hamiltonian H_S. Interactions k = 1, …, r have Hermitian operators V_k on K and bounded continuous switching functions g_k supported in [a_k, b_k], and each interaction is keyed to one clock, c(k) ∈ {A, B}. The constraint is Ĉ = P_A + P_B + H_S + Σ_k g_k(T_{c(k)}) V_k. Proposition 14.5 (Two ideal clocks and record events). (a) Physical states and reductions. In the representation in which T_A and T_B act by multiplication, ĈΨ = 0 is the transport equation i(∂_{t_A} + ∂_{t_B})Ψ = H_Δ(t_A)Ψ along the lines of constant offset Δ = t_A − t_B, where H_Δ(s) = H_S + Σ_{c(k)=A} g_k(s) V_k + Σ_{c(k)=B} g_k(s − Δ) V_k. Its solutions are Ψ(t_A, t_B) = U_Δ(t_A) χ(Δ), with U_Δ(s) the unitary propagator of H_Δ from a fixed initial value s_0 and χ: ℝ → K. With the inner product ⟨Ψ, Ψ′⟩ = ∫ ⟨χ(Δ), χ′(Δ)⟩ dΔ, the physical Hilbert space is H_phys ≅ L²(ℝ, K). The offset T_A − T_B commutes with Ĉ. For every pair of readings τ and τ′, the reductions R_A(τ): Ψ ↦ Ψ(τ, ·) and R_B(τ′): Ψ ↦ Ψ(·, τ′) are unitary from H_phys onto L²(ℝ, K). (b) Temporal localization. Relative to clock A, interactions keyed to A act at the definite A-times [a_k, b_k], and interactions keyed to B act at the A-times [a_k + Δ, b_k + Δ], which are spread according to the distribution of the offset in the state. Relative to clock B the roles are exchanged. (c) Record events. Let E_1, …, E_n be commuting projections on K, each commuting with H_S. Let H_phys^I be the subspace of physical states whose offsets lie in a bounded interval I = [δ_−, δ_+], and let s* be an A-time after every interaction for every Δ ∈ I, that is, s* ≥ b_k for interactions keyed to A and s* ≥ b_k + δ_+ for those keyed to B. Then the operators O_j = U_Δ(s*)† E_j U_Δ(s*), acting pointwise in Δ, are commuting projections on H_phys^I, and R_A(τ) O_j = E_j R_A(τ) for τ ≥ s*, R_B(τ′) O_j = E_j R_B(τ′) for τ′ ≥ s* − δ_−. Every finite history of record outcomes therefore has the same probability in the clock-neutral description and in either reduction at late readings. The clock-neutral instruments {O_j, 1 − O_j} intertwine with the record measurements {E_j, 1 − E_j} relative to either clock, and they compose in any order. (d) Superposed order. If an interaction keyed to A and one keyed to B have non-commuting operators, the order in which they act depends on Δ. The law of the record outcomes is then the mixture ∫ ‖χ(Δ)‖² P_Δ(α) dΔ of the laws P_Δ for the definite orders fixed by each offset, with P_Δ computed from χ(Δ)/‖χ(Δ)‖. Interference between the orders appears only in observables that are not diagonal in the offset. (e) Interference between the orders. Suppose H_S = 0, let f be a wave packet in the offset whose translates by δ_1 and δ_2 have disjoint supports in I, let ψ be a unit vector in K, and let χ(Δ) = (f(Δ − δ_1) + f(Δ − δ_2)) ψ/√2. Suppose that U_Δ(s*) equals a fixed unitary W_k for every Δ in the support of f(· − δ_k), as when each interaction is completed within either branch. Let 𝒰* multiply χ(Δ) by U_Δ(s*), let F_+ be the projection onto the offset-coherent wave function f_+ = (f(· − δ_1) + f(· − δ_2))/√2, acting trivially on K, and let O_+ = 𝒰*† F_+ 𝒰*. As in (a), physical states are represented by their initial data χ, so 𝒰* carries a physical state to its late-time data, and O_+ is the late-time projection F_+ expressed in the initial-data representation. Then O_+ is a projection on H_phys. Relative to A at τ ≥ s*, and relative to B at τ′ ≥ s* − δ_−, it is the projection onto the corresponding coherent superposition of readings of the other clock, and ⟨Ψ, O_+ Ψ⟩ = ½ (1 + Re⟨W_1 ψ, W_2 ψ⟩). For an incoherent mixture of the two offsets the same probability is ½, and when W_1ψ = W_2ψ it is 1. Its departure from ½ is half the real part of the overlap of the states produced by the two orders of the interactions. Proof. (a) The operator ∂_{t_A} + ∂_{t_B} differentiates along the lines of constant Δ, with s = t_A as parameter, so on each line the constraint reads i dΨ/ds = H_Δ(s)Ψ, whose solutions are U_Δ(s)χ(Δ). The offset commutes with P_A + P_B, since [P_A, T_A] = [P_B, T_B] = −i, and with every function of T_A and T_B. Relative to A, the conditional state is ψ_A(τ)(t_B) = U_{τ − t_B}(τ) χ(τ − t_B). Its norm is ∫ ‖χ(Δ)‖² dΔ because each U_Δ(τ) is unitary, and every ψ is attained, with χ(Δ) = U_Δ(τ)^{−1} ψ(τ − Δ). The argument for B, with ψ_B(τ′)(t_A) = U_{t_A − τ′}(t_A) χ(t_A − τ′), is the same. (b) This follows from the form of H_Δ, read along the lines t_A = τ (relative to A) or t_B = τ′ (relative to B). (c) For Δ ∈ I and s ≥ s*, no interaction is active, so U_Δ(s) = e^{−iH_S(s − s*)} U_Δ(s*), and E_j commutes with the first factor. For τ ≥ s* this gives U_Δ(τ) O_j(Δ) = E_j U_Δ(τ), pointwise in t_B = τ − Δ, which is the first identity. For τ′ ≥ s* − δ_−, every point t_A = τ′ + Δ with Δ ∈ I satisfies t_A ≥ s*, and the same computation gives the second. The O_j are projections that commute because the E_j do, and they preserve H_phys^I because they act pointwise in Δ. Probabilities agree because the reductions are unitary. (d) Each O_j acts pointwise in Δ, so ‖Π_j O_j^{(α_j)} Ψ‖² = ∫ ‖Π_j O_j^{(α_j)}(Δ) χ(Δ)‖² dΔ. (e) The probability is ⟨χ, 𝒰*† F_+ 𝒰* χ⟩ = ‖F_+ 𝒰* χ‖², in which the evolution 𝒰* is applied once, to the initial data χ = f_+ ⊗ ψ. By construction 𝒰*χ = (f(· − δ_1) W_1ψ + f(· − δ_2) W_2ψ)/√2, so F_+ 𝒰*χ = f_+ ⊗ (W_1ψ + W_2ψ)/2, whose squared norm is ½(1 + Re⟨W_1ψ, W_2ψ⟩). Since H_S = 0, U_Δ(s) = U_Δ(s*) for s ≥ s*, so at late readings R_A(τ) and R_B(τ′) differ from 𝒰* only by relabelling the offset as a reading of the other clock, which gives the stated forms. For a mixture, each branch has overlap ½ with f_+. □ Proposition 14.5 proves clock-neutral equivalence for a specified family of instruments, late-record projective measurements built from commuting projections, together with the offset-coherent projection of part (e), in an ideal two-clock constrained model. It is the ideal-clock case of Conjecture 14.4 for this family only. It does not establish equivalence for arbitrary clock-neutral instruments, for events that occur while interactions are active, or for an indefinitely extended succession of physically generated records, and it is not a general solution of temporal-QRF compatibility. In the numerical check (Appendix C.2), a record written by an interaction keyed to A, with the offset spread over [0.06, 1.64], has probability 0.139627690767 in the clock-neutral description and in both reductions at late readings. Relative to A, the record probability is exactly zero before A-time 0, the start of the interaction. Relative to B, it is already 0.021 at B-time −1.0, because in part of the offset distribution the interaction has begun. For these instruments, the event, its probability, and its instrument are the same in both descriptions; only its temporal localization differs. With one interaction keyed to each clock and the offset in an equal superposition of two wave packets centred at −1.6 and +1.6, the record law is 0.706791827009 in all three descriptions. This equals the equal-weight mixture of the two definite-order values, 0.868488 and 0.545095. Part (e) answers the question that part (d) leaves open: whether the superposition of orders has operational consequences. It does, for an observable that measures the clocks’ relative offset in a coherent basis. In the numerical check, this probability is 0.926776695297 in the clock-neutral description and in both reductions, equal to ½(1 + Re⟨W_1ψ, W_2ψ⟩), whereas an incoherent mixture of the two offsets gives exactly ½. The statistic depends jointly on both orders. It is reproduced neither by one definite order common to both offsets, which would give 1, nor by an order correlated classically with the offset, which would give ½. This is a calculation within the model, not a device-independent certification of indefinite causal order, which would require a causal witness in the sense of Araújo et al. (2015). For CTGP, parts (d) and (e) have a specific lesson. The order of unitary interactions keyed to different clocks can be indefinite, with operational consequences, as in the analyses of Castro-Ruiz et al. (2020) and Baumann and Lock (2026). The realized events, the record outcomes and the outcome of an offset-coherent measurement, nevertheless have clock-neutral identities, probabilities, and instruments. CTGP’s definite-ordering commitment (C-e) concerns stages and the realized events located in them, not unitary couplings, so parts (d) and (e) do not conflict with it. What Proposition 14.5 does not supply is a generative order of the record events. Their instruments commute, so every order gives the same law, and designating one order as generative is interpretive in the sense of §14.6. The proposition is also limited by its idealizations. Ideal clocks have Hamiltonians unbounded below, and for clocks with bounded Hamiltonians the reductions are no longer unitary (Höhn, Smith & Lock 2021). Events that occur while interactions are active, and interventions that do not single out one clock, are not covered. 14.6 The Possible Obstruction A fully relational quantum theory may treat all clock choices as physically equivalent and deny that any further, objective generative ordering exists. CTGP could then still be added as an ontological interpretation, but the added structure would have to be compatible with the theory’s constraints and gauge symmetries. Two cases must be distinguished. If the added ordering has no physical effects, it is interpretive rather than a new physical variable. If it affects event probabilities, it is a physical hypothesis subject to empirical test. Section 13.10 meets the same alternative from the side of collapse dynamics: cosmological time could fix the local frame and the order of conflicting collapses covariantly, but only as a dynamical ordering (Remark 13.25). Recent work sharpens the problem. Baumann and Lock (2026) report that, of the ways of modeling interventions within relational dynamics, only one that builds the interventions into the constraint remains causally consistent across multiple clocks, that it requires some temporal delocalization, and that it naturally describes indefinite causal order. CTGP cannot simply stipulate that every clock-relative temporal ordering is the same. It must identify which physical ordering is ontologically fundamental and prove that legitimate temporal descriptions can be related to it. If indefinite causal order turned out to be fundamental, rather than a feature of clock-relative descriptions, CTGP’s primitive ordering would require generalization (C-e; CTGP – explained, §6.4). Three claims must be kept apart in assessing this possibility. (i) Every effective quantum operation has a definite classical order. The quantum switch (Chiribella, D’Ariano, Perinotti & Valiron 2013) is the standard example of a process for which this fails, and photonic quantum-switch experiments (Procopio et al. 2015) support processes whose effective operations cannot be represented as ordinary probabilistic mixtures of fixed orders within the relevant operational framework. (ii) The finer-grained physical interactions have a definite causal order. A failure of (i) does not establish a failure of (ii): for realizations in a classical background spacetime, and under specified assumptions about relativistic causality and the spacetime localization of systems, Vilasini and Renner (2024) show that such processes admit a fine-grained description with a definite acyclic causal order. This is not a general theorem that every quantum process has a definite underlying causal order, and it does not address superpositions of spacetime geometry (§15.1). (iii) A single history of physical events is realized. Neither (i) nor (ii) supplies this; it requires single-outcome realism (C-d). In suitable models, Bohmian mechanics supplies definite trajectories and thereby one realized history; a GRW flash ontology supplies definite flashes, but those flashes need not identify which effective operation order was realized. These are illustrations, not established relativistic solutions. CTGP’s definite-ordering commitment (C-e) concerns stages and the realized events located in them, so operational causal nonseparability alone neither establishes nor refutes CTGP. The generalization contemplated above would be needed only if indefinite order proved fundamental, at the level of (ii) or of the generative ordering itself, rather than a feature of effective operations or clock-relative descriptions. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 15. Quantum Gravity ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 15.1 Fundamental Generative Order Versus Classical Cosmological Time In the classical regime, CTGP identifies the present with a level set of the cosmological time T_g(p) = sup { L_g(γ) : γ a past-directed causal curve from p }, subject to the regularity conditions of §13.1. A fundamental quantum theory of gravity may have no single classical metric. A state of the schematic form α|g_1⟩ + β|g_2⟩, with g_1 and g_2 inducing different cosmological-time functions, has no unique classical T. The program therefore distinguishes fundamental generative order ≠ classical cosmological-time function. The two must agree in the appropriate classical limit, but they need not coincide at the fundamental level. This develops the distinction between primitive generation and emergent geometry that CTGP already draws (CTGP – explained, §§10.3–10.4). It also raises a demanding question: what physical structure carries the objective generative ordering before classical spacetime exists? Naming a parameter does not answer it. 15.2 Three Pathways (i) Semiclassical and quantum-geometric emergence. Begin with quantum matter on an approximately classical metric and progressively allow gravitational degrees of freedom to fluctuate. This is the most incremental route from the constructions of this preprint, and it tests how much geometric uncertainty CTGP can accommodate before its definition of a unique present becomes problematic. (ii) Canonical quantum gravity. Start from constrained gravitational dynamics, with the Hamiltonian constraint written schematically as ĤΨ = 0, and ask whether an internal or emergent time permits a single-history generative interpretation. A formally stationary physical state does not mean that relational observables are static (Kuchař 1992; Isham 1993), but an objective succession of unique outcomes requires further interpretation or dynamics. Material reference systems such as Brown–Kuchař dust (Brown & Kuchař 1995) are candidates for a physically instantiated time. (iii) Causal sets. Causal-set approaches make causal order fundamental, and classical sequential growth models generate a causal set element by element (Bombelli, Lee, Meyer & Sorkin 1987; Rideout & Sorkin 2000). The labels of the growth sequence are, however, gauge: the physics is required to be independent of the order in which elements are born. Theorem 13.4 and Proposition 13.7 offer an analogue of this situation: for a past-finite causal order with commuting operations at unrelated elements, every natural labeling generates the same history measure, so the choice of generative labeling is empirically invisible (Corollary 13.5, Proposition 13.7(b)). A CTGP reading would then treat one labeling as ontologically generative without physical effect, which places it in the interpretive case of §14.6, unless an invariant ordering or an appropriate continuum limit can be found. The recommended order is to begin with pathway (i), to use pathway (ii) to examine the problem of time, and to treat pathway (iii) as a possible realization rather than CTGP’s presumed foundation. 15.3 Compatibility Conditions A candidate quantum-gravity theory would have to answer five questions affirmatively. (Q1) Forward generation. Does it admit a physically meaningful forward-generative formulation? A global mathematical description is not disqualifying; an indispensable independent future boundary input would conflict with generability. (Q2) One realized history. Can it support one realized physical history? A superposition in a quantum state is not a contradiction, but CTGP requires a definite factual history rather than many equally realized branches. (Q3) Gauge symmetry. Does it respect the relevant gauge symmetries? A proposed generation parameter must not promote an arbitrary coordinate label to a physical observable. (Q4) Classical spacetime. Can it recover classical spacetime, reproducing Einstein’s equations and the cosmological-time construction wherever CTGP’s classical assumptions hold? (Q5) Phenomenology. Does it preserve acceptable relativistic phenomenology? Any preferred- ordering dynamics must avoid experimentally excluded effects. The classical-limit requirement can be written schematically as 𝒬_CTGP → (M, g, Σ_{T_g}) in the appropriate semiclassical limit, where 𝒬_CTGP denotes a hypothetical completed quantum-generative theory. The arrow represents a research objective, not an existing derivation. 15.4 What Would, and What Would Not, Rule Out Compatibility The following would be genuinely problematic for CTGP: (R1) a fundamental theory that requires independent final boundary conditions which cannot be replaced by legitimate present-state dynamics; (R2) a theory in which no invariant notion of realized physical history can be defined; (R3) a quantum-gravity framework whose physical predictions necessarily depend on mutually incompatible fundamental generative orderings; (R4) a demonstrated impossibility of recovering CTGP’s classical cosmic-time structure in the domain where CTGP claims to apply. By contrast, indefinite causal order, timeless constraint equations, and quantum superpositions of geometries are not by themselves sufficient to establish any of these conclusions. They are reasons to investigate, not refutations. 15.5 First Test Problems Three finite problems would test the quantum-gravity conditions without attempting a complete theory. Each is a target, not a result. (G1) Label-invariant realized causal-set histories. Define a stochastic growth law on labeled causal sets, in which each step adds one element to the future of some of the existing ones, with a quantum-instrument version in which operations at unrelated elements commute. Impose a discrete form of general covariance and prove that the probabilities of unlabeled completed causal sets do not depend on the natural labeling used to compute them. For a fixed causal order this is Theorem 13.4 and Proposition 13.7. For growth models it adapts the discrete general covariance of classical sequential growth (Rideout & Sorkin 2000), and it should be presented as an adaptation rather than as a new theorem. CTGP’s contribution would be ontological: whether one birth order can be real while its labeling is unobservable. That reading faces a genuine tension. If the birth orders of unrelated elements are pure gauge, asserting that one of them is physically real adds structure that the invariant theory cannot detect. That may be acceptable as empirically inaccessible ontology, the interpretive case of §14.6, but it requires a principled defense. (G2) A finite quantum-geometry model. Take finitely many geometric configurations q, a physical state Σ_q c_q |q⟩, and an instrument representing geometry-related events. Prove that each update maps physical states to physical states, that outcome probabilities are gauge-invariant, that the law generates one realized history without a future boundary condition, and that the history measure is consistent under admissible changes of description. Such a model would be a controlled finite analogue, not compatibility with an established theory of quantum gravity. (G3) Two semiclassical branches. Take a state concentrated on two classical geometries whose cosmological-time functions T_1 and T_2 differ, without assuming that any cosmological time exists on the superposition. Ask whether one branch-independent ordering of events can be defined that reduces in each branch i to F_i(T_i), with F_i increasing, wherever T_i is regular. The possible outcomes are that a common ordering exists, that it exists only approximately, that an additional physical clock or reference field such as Brown–Kuchař dust is needed, or that the construction fails. A failure would be informative, since it would identify a genuine incompatibility between a particular formulation of CTGP and a particular model of quantum geometry. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 16. Roadmap ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Stage | Goal and deliverable | Status in this preprint --------------------------+--------------------------------------+------------------------------- 1. Quantum geometry | Constrained or pre-geometric model; | Test problems G1–G3 (§15.5); | event ontology; semiclassical limit | open Table 4. A staged program, with the status of each stage. Each stage should be independently publishable if it yields a substantive result. Failure at a stage would also be informative: it would identify either a needed refinement of CTGP or a genuine incompatibility, rather than leaving the question open. CTGP does not need to abandon its continuous global generation, its rejection of density-weighted generation, or its unique factual past to pursue this program. It does need to keep a clear line between its foundational ontology and any particular quantum implementation. ==================================================================================================== PART VI. Results and Outlook ==================================================================================================== ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 17. Scope, Results, and Open Problems ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 17.1 What Kind of Result This Is The preprint establishes an existence result, conditional on the CTGP interpretation: at least one well-defined model, within an explicitly specified class, has a structure that jointly satisfies the formal counterparts of CTGP’s quantum commitments. The mathematics establishes the structure; reading that structure as one realized present grounded in primitive past-tensed properties is CTGP’s interpretation (Ontological Reading 4.3, Thesis 5.10, §5.7), and mathematical consistency is not metaphysical vindication. It does not establish general compatibility, which would require showing that every physically admissible QRF theory can accommodate those commitments. It does not establish physical adequacy, which would require reproducing the relevant features of established quantum physics, including relativistic quantum field theory, and of general relativity. Existence results of this kind are a standard and useful form of progress: they show that a set of commitments is consistent and provide a concrete object on which objections can be tested. In the terms of Table 3, the finite model establishes mathematical compatibility on its own domain. Part V establishes finite-dimensional analogues of the relativistic and temporal results it requires, and states the rest as targets. 17.2 Established Within the class of models of Part II (finite symmetry group, regular representations, invariant unitary-plus-instrument dynamics) and its continuous-time form (§9), and in the explicit instance of Part III: (1) Two frame descriptions describe one complete state iff they satisfy Criterion 5.5, iff they lie in one description orbit; the orbits partition the frame descriptions (Theorem 5.6, Proposition 5.8). Ontological identity of the represented world is the further interpretive Thesis 5.10. (2) Admissible truth profiles are characterized exactly (Theorem 4.8). (3) State correspondence does not settle identity of history (Proposition 5.9); for invertible laws, every present ray is reachable along every finite event sequence from a suitably chosen initial ray (Proposition 4.12). (4) Determinacy is frame-independent, with a three-tier criterion in which primitive events, distinguished from sharp values, carry single-outcome realism (Definition 5.1, Propositions 4.4 and 5.2). (5) Valid frame changes intertwine the generative law: the measure over histories, the conditional independence of future from past given the present, and the truth of every determinate proposition are frame-invariant and preserved by generation (Theorems 5.12 and 5.16, Corollary 5.14). Laws that merely look alike in different frames do not describe one world (Corollary 5.13). (6) Ontological identity, physical equivalence, and observational indistinguishability are distinct relations, ordered by strict implication (Proposition 5.19). (7) L is generated, admissible, not reducible to S, accurate, frame-covariant, and dynamically idle (Theorem 5.20). (8) The model has a continuous-time form, with histories as marked point processes, for which admissibility, reconstruction, dynamical idleness, cross-frame correspondence, and truth preservation are proved (Propositions 9.2–9.9, Theorems 9.7 and 9.10); the identification of its time parameter with cosmic time is stated as a postulate (§9.4). (9) Within the class, generative order is shared by all frames, the law is generable, the future is open, and there are no relative facts (§10). (10) The truth profile is formally interchangeable with the event history (Proposition 5.21); its independent role is ontological, as the present ground for past-tense truths required by CTGP’s truthmaker principle, and the choice between this reading and an eternalist flash ontology rests on CTGP’s independent arguments (§5.7). (11) CTGP’s commitments exclude models that are otherwise QRF-covariant (Table 2); in particular, a QRF-covariant law that depends on past facts not carried by the present state is not generable (Proposition 11.4), whereas conditioning on a future event has an equivalent forward formulation and is not by itself a failure of generability (Proposition 11.1, Example 11.2). Within the perspective-neutral construction, events that carry physical states to physical states require collapse operators whose action on physical states agrees with that of invariant, relational operators; this does not single out the particular flash ontology used here (§7.4). (12) The finite model is CTGP-admissible on its domain under the proposed criterion of Definition 12.1 (Proposition 12.2). (13) The level sets of regular cosmological time are future Cauchy surfaces, and a level set is a Cauchy surface exactly when every future-inextendible causal curve passes beyond it (Proposition 13.1). (14) Agreement of density operators on every hypersurface does not fix the probabilities of realized histories (Proposition 13.2). For causally local instruments on a finite causal order, realized-history probabilities are foliation-independent and outcome marginals do not depend on instruments outside the designated predecessor set, so within such finite models the choice of generative foliation is empirically invisible (Theorem 13.4, Corollary 13.5); the result extends to countably many events with finite pasts (Proposition 13.7), and identifying the predecessor set with the relativistic causal past requires the further assumptions (E1)–(E6). (15) For finite networks of gapless detectors on a free relativistic field, realized-history probabilities are foliation-independent, detector statistics are given explicitly, and there is no operational signaling between causally separated regions, including in Sorkin’s three-region configuration (Theorem 13.9). Localizing the Kraus operators of an operation does not by itself secure this (Example 13.10). (16) The same conclusions hold for every finite, causally orderable network of Weyl-diagonal instruments on a free field (Theorem 13.13). Gaussian collapse of a smeared field is Weyl- diagonal (Proposition 13.12), which yields a regulated spontaneous-collapse network with foliation-independent histories and no signaling between causally separated regions (Corollary 13.14), subject to the limitations (L1)–(L4). (17) Generative succession, clock readings, and clock-conditional descriptions are distinct; with an external generative order, imperfect clocks can repeat readings, delocalize events, and invert their recorded order without affecting event identity or probability (Proposition 14.2). (18) The unread Gaussian collapse channel is a Gaussian mixture of unitary kicks. Position collapse of a particle injects exactly ħ²/(8mσ²), whatever the potential, and Gaussian collapse of a smeared free field in 3 + 1 dimensions injects a state-independent, future-timelike energy–momentum equal to that of the classical solution Eh divided by 4σ². On a curved background it changes the renormalized stress–energy of a Hadamard state by the classical stress–energy of Eh divided by 4σ² (Propositions 13.15 and 13.16). (19) If the operations performed between a tested operation and the targets preserve an algebra on which the tested operation acts trivially, the tested operation cannot signal to the targets, whatever the field theory; microcausality supplies only the second condition (Theorem 13.17, Corollary 13.18). (20) For Gaussian collapses at Poisson-distributed locations with a covariant law, histories are foliation-independent and non-signaling conditionally on causally orderable configurations, which is not yet an unconditional collapse law (Theorem 13.21). Orderability fails in large volumes, the order of interfering collapses then matters physically, and no Lorentz-invariant law for point-centred smearings exists (Propositions 13.22–13.24). Covariant repairs require additional structure at each event, and those that use cosmological time would give the generative order a dynamical role (Remark 13.25). (21) For two ideal clocks with the interventions in the constraint, a family of late-record projective instruments has clock-neutral identities, probabilities, and instruments, which intertwine with both reductions, while temporal localization differs between the clocks. When the order of interactions depends on the clock offset, an offset-coherent observable exhibits interference between the orders (Proposition 14.5). (22) A candidate extension, not adopted, in which cosmological time fixes the smearing and the order of collapses, is complete and normalized on every domain of finite four-volume, and on infinite histories when cosmological-time slabs have finite volume; it can be generated forward along cosmological time in its free-field representation and admits no signaling between causally separated regions for the operations it covers. It agrees with the causal-order dynamics wherever that is defined in the flat local model. It injects energy at rest in the cosmic frame and makes the correlations among collapse outcomes depend on that frame, which is distinct from superluminal signaling and not yet shown to be operationally detectable (Lemma 13.26, Proposition 13.28). Ordering the collapses by the completion of their regions gives the same law, with forward generation from the generated past (Proposition 13.29). Realizing each collapse as the reading of a gapless pointer coupled locally to the field derives the collapse statistics and gives a variant with no ordering rule: its evolution is foliation-independent on every finite configuration, it admits no signaling by the modeled pointers and classical sources outside the causal past of the targets, it agrees with sequential collapse on causally orderable configurations, it has a law of outcome records on every FLRW spacetime of the class, and it injects the same energy–momentum, while its outcome law differs from the candidate’s where collapses interfere (Lemma 13.30, Proposition 13.31). Its forward generation is established for finite configurations, and its definite readings require a primitive single-outcome postulate, which the coupling does not supply. The energy–momentum injection is a theoretical prediction; an experimental signature would require a collapse law for matter and an account of how an apparatus records the effect. 17.3 Open Problems (O1) Continuous groups. Physical QRFs use translations, rotations, and boosts, which form continuous groups (Giacomini et al. 2019). For non-compact groups, invariant states are not normalizable in H_kin, and H_phys must be built by group averaging with a physical inner product. Reduction maps remain unitary in the perspective-neutral framework (Vanrietvelde et al. 2020), so Part II is expected to carry over, but the flash dynamics needs a relational version of the GRW or CSL smearing in continuous space, and its well-definedness must be proved. (O2) Relativistic quantum fields. The ring model is non-relativistic, and Theorem 13.4 and Proposition 13.7 are discrete analogues; the assumptions a relativistic extension must supply are listed in §13.5 as (E1)–(E6). Theorem 13.9 settles the measurement-model case for finite networks of gapless detectors on a free field. Theorem 13.13 with Corollary 13.14 gives a regulated spontaneous-collapse network for Gaussian collapse of smeared fields on a fixed family of regions, and Theorem 13.21 extends it to Poisson-distributed locations on causally orderable configurations. Open are a covariant law for extended collapse events, for which Propositions 13.22–13.24 identify the obstructions, Remark 13.25 the available repairs, and §13.11 a candidate completion that uses cosmological time, together with a gapless-pointer variant that needs no ordering rule (Proposition 13.31), neither of which is adopted; a physical account of the pointers and of their definite readings, which the variant postulates; a forward evolution of the present state with infinitely many pointers; a local probe field in place of single- degree-of-freedom pointers; an operational prediction for the predicted preferred-frame energy injection; collapse of matter density rather than of linear field observables; detectors with internal dynamics; interacting fields, for which Theorem 13.17 gives a sufficient condition and Target 13.19 a first test; and continuously distributed events. The relativistic realized- history consistency result (Target 13.6) is open: it requires an event-history measure for a regulated relativistic single-outcome model, a measurement framework free of Sorkin-type signaling, and, for CTGP, a generable representation on cosmological-time hypersurfaces that would derive the identification postulated in §9.4. CTGP’s ensemble-level results (CTGP – explained, Proposition 17.16 and Theorem 17.17) are conditional and do not by themselves supply a covariant stochastic ontology (Proposition 13.2). (O3) Temporal QRFs. This is the most consequential open problem; §14 sets out the distinctions, a worked model, a proposed identification criterion for overlapping descriptions (Definition 14.3), its proof for late-record instruments with ideal clocks and the interventions in the constraint (Proposition 14.5), and a target for imperfect clocks (Conjecture 14.4). The construction here assumes a shared stage or time index, and Proposition 10.1 preserves it because frame changes act only on spatial relational degrees of freedom. Temporal QRFs, in which a quantum clock is the frame (Page & Wootters 1983; Höhn, Smith & Lock 2021), can assign superposed readings to one clock relative to another and can make the temporal localization of events indefinite (Castro-Ruiz, Giacomini, Belenchia & Brukner 2020). Two structures must then be distinguished: the ontological generation order, which CTGP requires to be definite, and quantum-clock relational time, the readings of physical clock subsystems relative to one another. CTGP does not require quantum clocks to reveal a preferred cosmic time. It requires an account of how relational clock descriptions correspond to an underlying generative order, if there is one. The research question is whether a temporally relational model can be constructed with a definite underlying order of events that remains invariant under every admissible clock- frame transformation, without that order being assumed and then merely preserved. One candidate route is to add a quantum clock subsystem to the ring model: clock-relative descriptions would assign superpositions of readings to events, while the order of events in the record is fixed by generation. A second is a material reference system of the Brown–Kuchař dust type (Brown & Kuchař 1995), whose proper time could serve as a physically instantiated cosmic time akin to CTGP’s matter-frame cosmological time. Both are conjectures, and the result of Baumann and Lock (2026) on causal consistency across clocks (§14.6) indicates that temporal delocalization, and possibly indefinite causal order, must be expected in clock-relative descriptions. (O4) Quantum gravity and indefinite causal order. As CTGP – explained notes (§6.4), if quantum gravity requires indefinite causal order at the level of spacetime itself, CTGP’s primitive ordering would require generalization. Proposition 10.1 holds for the class of models here but says nothing about such theories. Section 15 distinguishes fundamental generative order from classical cosmological time, sets out three pathways, and lists the conditions a quantum-gravity theory would have to meet (Q1–Q5) and the findings that would rule out compatibility (R1–R4), and §15.5 states three finite test problems. (O5) Records. The model has no record subsystems; evidence about the past is whatever the present state encodes. Adding pointer or environment systems would allow a formal treatment of how records serve as evidence for L across frames, of how records degrade while L does not (CTGP – explained, §14.6), and of observers with access to records rather than to the full present state. (O6) Weak non-supervenience at generic parameters. Whether two distinct histories from a fixed initial state can end in exactly the same present state for generic finite-width parameters is open (§8.3). It is not needed for CTGP, which requires only strong non-supervenience (Proposition 4.12). (O7) Empirical parameters. The parameters used here are illustrative. Real collapse models are tightly constrained by experiment (Bassi et al. 2013), and a relational collapse model must respect the same bounds. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 18. The open questions and where they are answered. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- 19. Conclusion ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- The present world is represented here by a perspective-neutral quantum state together with a Lucretian truth profile drawn from an exactly specified space of admissible histories. The generative law is a relational, single-outcome instrument in discrete stages, or equivalently a relational GRW-type jump process in continuous time. Its primitive events are occurrences whose probabilities the law fixes, not sharp values of observables. Frame descriptions are unitary reductions of the neutral state, and two descriptions describe one complete state exactly when they lie in one description orbit. The interpretive thesis that such an orbit is one realized world with one history is what CTGP adds, and it is stated as a thesis, not presented as a theorem. The truth profile does work that the quantum state cannot do. With finite-width collapse, any present state can be reached along any sequence of events from a suitable initial state, so nothing in the present physical state settles what happened; what settles it is what the present world has been. That is the Lucretian thesis, given an exact form. The same results separate what is true about the past from what can be recovered about it and from what still influences the future: the past is definite, it may be unrecoverable, and it acts on the future only through the present. Beyond the finite model, the same structure points to a definite program. The distinction that matters most is between agreement about states and agreement about realized histories: theories can agree on every density operator and still disagree about what happened, so single-outcome compatibility with relativity must be proved at the level of histories. For causally local instruments in finite models it holds, and the choice of generative foliation is then empirically invisible, as CTGP’s observational equivalence requires. For finite networks of gapless detectors on a free relativistic field it holds as well, with operational no-signaling even in Sorkin’s configuration, and it extends to spontaneous Gaussian collapses of smeared fields on a fixed family of regions and to collapses at Poisson-distributed locations on every causally orderable configuration. Beyond that, a covariant law needs structure at each event: orderability is lost in large volumes, interfering collapses then depend on their order, and no Lorentz-invariant law for point-centred smearings of a free field exists. Within CTGP, cosmological time can supply that structure consistently, as a candidate extension, but only by giving the generative order a dynamical role, which is a decision for the framework rather than a result. Realizing each collapse by a gapless pointer coupled to the field removes the need to order the collapses, although cosmological time still fixes their smearings and the definite pointer readings must still be postulated. A covariant spontaneous-collapse theory that localizes matter remains to be established. Given an external generative order (Proposition 14.2), quantum clocks, including imperfect clocks, may repeat readings, temporally delocalize events, and assign different clock-relative orderings without changing the identity of the underlying realized events; and for two ideal clocks with the interventions inside the constraint, late-record events are clock-neutral even when the order of the interactions that write them is superposed, and that superposition has observable consequences for offset-coherent measurements. Deriving a definite generative order from clock-neutral data, treating realistic clocks, and carrying generation into quantum gravity remain open. The law generates the history, and the truth profile records it. Frames describe the result differently, but they never disagree about what happened. ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- References ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Aharonov, Y., & Kaufherr, T.
